Solutions — Concentration Units, Raoult’s Law, Colligative Properties & Solubility

A solution is a homogeneous mixture of two or more substances. The component present in the larger amount is the solvent; the others are solutes. Many physical properties of a solution depend on the number of solute particles rather than their identity, which is why colligative properties provide a window into dissociation and association in solution.

“The properties of a solution are not merely the sum of the properties of its components; the interactions between them matter.”
The central idea behind Raoult’s and Henry’s laws
Part 1

Concentration Units and Conversions

Concentration
Molarity, molality, mole fraction and mass-based units

The choice of concentration unit depends on the measurement being made. Molarity M = nsolute/Vsolution (mol L⁻¹) is convenient for volumetric work but changes with temperature because the solution volume expands. Molality m = nsolute/masssolvent (mol kg⁻¹) is temperature-independent and is the natural unit for colligative properties. Mole fraction xi = ni/ntotal is dimensionless and convenient for vapour-pressure calculations.

Mass percent and parts per million are useful for very dilute or industrial solutions. Converting between molarity and molality requires the solution density: m = M/(ρ − M Msolute/1000), where ρ is in g cm⁻³ and M is molarity.

Part 2

Raoult’s Law, Henry’s Law and Ideal Solutions

Vapour Pressure
Ideal mixtures, deviations and azeotropes

For an ideal solution, the partial vapour pressure of each component is proportional to its mole fraction in the liquid:

pi = xi p°i

This is Raoult’s law. The total pressure is p = p₁ + p₂. An ideal solution forms when the intermolecular forces between unlike molecules are similar to those between like molecules, and the enthalpy of mixing is zero.

Real solutions deviate. Positive deviations occur when A–B interactions are weaker than A–A and B–B interactions; the molecules escape more easily, so the total pressure is higher than predicted and the mixture may form a minimum-boiling azeotrope (for example, ethanol–water). Negative deviations occur when A–B interactions are stronger, lowering the vapour pressure and potentially producing a maximum-boiling azeotrope (for example, nitric acid–water).

Henry’s law describes the solubility of a sparingly soluble gas: p = KHx. The Henry constant KH increases with temperature, so gases become less soluble in warm water. This is why dissolved oxygen levels fall in heated rivers and why carbonated drinks go flat when warm.

Part 3

Colligative Properties and the van’t Hoff Factor

Colligative Properties
Boiling-point elevation, freezing-point depression and osmotic pressure

Colligative properties depend on the number of solute particles, not their chemical identity. For a non-volatile, non-electrolyte solute:

  • Relative lowering of vapour pressure: (p° − p)/p° = xsolute
  • Boiling-point elevation: ΔTb = Kb m
  • Freezing-point depression: ΔTf = Kf m
  • Osmotic pressure: π = MRT

Kb and Kf are ebullioscopic and cryoscopic constants characteristic of the solvent. For electrolytes, the number of particles is larger than the formal number of formula units, so each expression is multiplied by the van’t Hoff factor i:

ΔTb = iKbm,   ΔTf = iKfm,   π = iMRT

For complete dissociation i equals the number of ions per formula unit (2 for NaCl, 3 for CaCl₂); for weak electrolytes i lies between 1 and that limit and can be used to estimate the degree of dissociation. Association, such as dimerisation of carboxylic acids in benzene, gives i < 1. Osmotic pressure is especially useful for determining the molar mass of polymers and proteins because it is measurable at low concentrations.

Part 4

Solubility and Ksp

Solubility Equilibria
The solubility product, common ions and coupled equilibria

For a sparingly soluble salt MaXb dissolving to Ma+ and Xb−, the equilibrium constant is the solubility product:

Ksp = [Ma+]a[Xb−]b

If the salt dissociates completely and no other source of ions is present, solubility s is related to Ksp by Ksp = aabbsa+b. A common ion lowers solubility through Le Chatelier’s principle: adding NaCl to a saturated AgCl solution shifts the dissolution equilibrium to the left. pH can also matter when the anion is basic (carbonates, hydroxides, sulphides) or the cation hydrolyses. Complex formation can increase solubility by removing the free metal ion, as in the dissolution of AgCl in ammonia.

Precipitation begins when the reaction quotient Q exceeds Ksp. This criterion underlies qualitative analysis, water softening and the controlled precipitation of sparingly soluble salts.

Non‑Ideal Solution Models and Activity Coefficients

Real solutions deviate from Raoult’s law because intermolecular forces between unlike molecules differ from those in the pure components. The thermodynamic framework replaces mole fraction xi with activity ai = γi xi, where γi is the activity coefficient. For a binary mixture the excess Gibbs energy GE = RT Σ xi ln γi quantifies the deviation. Common models include the Margules two‑parameter equation, the Wilson equation, and the NRTL (Non‑Random Two‑Liquid) model. The Margules expression for a symmetric system is ln γ1 = A x2² and ln γ2 = A x1², where A is an empirical constant obtained from vapour‑pressure data. The Wilson model introduces local‑composition concepts and is particularly effective for highly non‑ideal systems such as ethanol–water. NRTL adds a non‑randomness parameter α (typically 0.2–0.47) and can represent both positive and negative deviations. Activity coefficients are temperature dependent; they are usually correlated with the van’t Hoff equation ln γ = –ΔHE/RT + ΔSE/R. In practice, γ values are used to calculate equilibrium constants for reactions in solution, to design distillation columns, and to predict azeotrope composition. For example, the ethanol–water azeotrope at 1 atm contains 95.6 % ethanol by mass; the corresponding γethanol ≈ 1.0 and γwater ≈ 2.5 at that composition. Understanding these models enables the examiner to test whether a student can convert experimental vapour‑pressure data into activity coefficients and then predict phase behaviour.

Temperature Dependence of Henry’s Constant and Gas Solubility

Henry’s law constant KH (in units of pressure · mole‑fraction⁻¹) varies with temperature according to the van’t Hoff relation d ln KH/d(1/T) = –ΔHsol/R, where ΔHsol is the enthalpy of solution of the gas. For most gases ΔHsol is negative (exothermic dissolution), so KH increases as temperature rises, meaning solubility decreases. The temperature coefficient is often expressed as the Ostwald coefficient L = Cgas/Cliquid at equilibrium, which is inversely proportional to KH. Experimental data for O₂ in water show KH ≈ 1.3 × 10³ atm at 298 K and ≈ 2.5 × 10³ atm at 313 K, corresponding to a drop in dissolved O₂ from 1.3 mmol L⁻¹ to 0.7 mmol L⁻¹ at 1 atm. This temperature effect underlies the seasonal stratification of lakes and the design of aeration systems in wastewater treatment. In carbonated beverages, the pressure of CO₂ in the headspace is set at 2–3 atm at 277 K; warming to 298 K raises KH by roughly 40 %, causing rapid loss of fizz. The examiner may ask to calculate the new equilibrium concentration after a temperature jump, requiring the student to integrate the van’t Hoff equation assuming ΔHsol constant over the range. A typical trap is to confuse the Henry constant expressed in pressure‑over‑concentration units (kH = p/C) with the dimensionless form; the temperature dependence is identical but the numerical values differ by a factor of RT.

Worked Example: Henry’s Constant Temperature Shift
At 298 K the Henry constant for N₂ in water is 6.1 × 10⁴ atm·M⁻¹. The enthalpy of solution is –12 kJ mol⁻¹. Estimate the Henry constant at 313 K and the dissolved N₂ concentration at 1 atm.
Use the integrated van’t Hoff equation: ln(K₂/K₁) = –ΔHsol/R (1/T₂ – 1/T₁). Insert ΔHsol = –12 000 J mol⁻¹, R = 8.314 J mol⁻¹ K⁻¹, T₁ = 298 K, T₂ = 313 K. 1/T₂ – 1/T₁ = (1/313 – 1/298) = –1.61 × 10⁻⁴ K⁻¹. Then ln(K₂/K₁) = –(–12 000)/8.314 × (–1.61 × 10⁻⁴) = –0.232. Hence K₂ = K₁ e⁻⁰·²³² = 6.1 × 10⁴ × 0.793 ≈ 4.8 × 10⁴ atm·M⁻¹. The concentration at 1 atm is C = p/K₂ = 1/4.8 × 10⁴ = 2.1 × 10⁻⁵ M. At 298 K the concentration would have been 1.6 × 10⁻⁵ M, confirming the decrease in solubility with temperature.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question gives ΔHsol and asks for the new Henry constant, write the van’t Hoff equation in logarithmic form first; this avoids sign errors. Remember that a negative ΔHsol makes KH increase with temperature, so solubility falls. Keep units consistent: if KH is given in atm·M⁻¹, the concentration will be in mol L⁻¹ directly.

Colligative Properties in Mixed Solvent Systems

Colligative property equations are derived for a single solvent. In a mixed‑solvent system the effective cryoscopic and ebullioscopic constants become composition‑dependent. For a binary solvent mixture of components A and B, the freezing‑point depression is ΔTf = Kf,mix msolute, where Kf,mix = Σ xi Kf,i (γi/γsolvent) under the assumption of ideal mixing of the solvents. In practice, the solvent activity asolvent = γsolvent xsolvent replaces the pure‑solvent mole fraction in the thermodynamic derivation: ΔTf = (R Tf²/ΔHfus) ln asolvent. For dilute solutes, ln asolvent ≈ –xsolute (γsolute/γsolvent), leading to an apparent van’t Hoff factor that includes solvent‑solvent interactions. A classic example is the determination of molar mass of a polymer in a water‑ethanol mixture; the measured ΔTf is smaller than predicted using pure‑water Kf because ethanol lowers the solvent’s freezing point and changes its activity coefficient. Examiners often test this by giving a binary solvent composition and asking for the corrected Kf or the apparent molar mass. The key step is to calculate the solvent activity from known vapour‑pressure data or from a model such as UNIFAC, then apply the modified freezing‑point equation.

Application of van’t Hoff Factor to Degree of Dissociation and Association

The van’t Hoff factor i links the observed colligative effect to the number of particles in solution. For a weak electrolyte AB ⇌ A⁺ + B⁻ with degree of dissociation α, i = 1 + α (for a 1:1 electrolyte) or i = 1 + (ν – 1)α in general, where ν is the total number of ions produced per formula unit. For association, e.g. 2 HA ⇌ (HA)₂ in benzene, the equilibrium constant Kassoc = [(HA)₂]/[HA]². If the total analytical concentration is c, the monomer concentration is c (1 – α) and dimer concentration is c α/2, giving i = 1 – α/2. Measuring ΔTf or π at several concentrations allows a plot of i versus c; extrapolation to infinite dilution yields the limiting i (ν for complete dissociation, 1 for non‑electrolyte). The Ostwald dilution law Kd = c α²/(1 – α) can be combined with i = 1 + α to determine Kd from colligative data. A typical problem provides freezing‑point depressions for acetic acid in benzene at two concentrations; the student calculates i at each concentration, obtains α, and then evaluates Kassoc. Care must be taken to use molality (temperature‑independent) and to apply the correct Kf for benzene (5.12 K kg mol⁻¹). The examiner may also ask to distinguish between ion‑pair formation (which reduces i) and true association (which also reduces i but follows a different equilibrium expression).

Solubility Product Calculations with Complex Ion Formation and pH Effects

When a sparingly soluble salt shares an ion with a complex‑forming ligand, the apparent solubility increases because the free metal ion concentration is lowered. For AgCl(s) ⇌ Ag⁺ + Cl⁻ (Ksp = 1.8 × 10⁻¹⁰) and Ag⁺ + 2 NH₃ ⇌ [Ag(NH₃)₂]⁺ (Kf = 1.6 × 10⁷), the overall dissolution in ammonia is AgCl(s) + 2 NH₃ ⇌ [Ag(NH₃)₂]⁺ + Cl⁻ with K = Ksp Kf = 2.9 × 10⁻³. If the ammonia concentration is 0.1 M, the solubility s ≈ √(K [NH₃]²) = √(2.9 × 10⁻³ × 0.01) ≈ 5.4 × 10⁻³ M, far larger than the 1.3 × 10⁻⁵ M in pure water. pH influences solubility when the anion is basic. For CaCO₃ (Ksp = 3.3 × 10⁻⁹), carbonate reacts with H⁺: CO₃²⁻ + H⁺ ⇌ HCO₃⁻ (Ka2 = 4.7 × 10⁻¹¹). At pH 5, [H⁺] = 10⁻⁵ M, the fraction of carbonate present as CO₃²⁻ is α₂ = Ka1Ka2/([H⁺]² + Ka1[H⁺] + Ka1Ka2) ≈ 0.02. The effective solubility product becomes Ksp/α₂, raising the molar solubility by roughly √(1/α₂) ≈ 7‑fold. Examiners often combine both effects: a metal hydroxide dissolving in a buffer containing a complexing agent. The systematic approach is to write all equilibria, express the total dissolved metal as the sum of free ion and complex, substitute the ligand concentration (often fixed by a buffer), and solve the resulting polynomial for s. Approximations (e.g., ligand in large excess, pH fixed) simplify the algebra and are expected in timed tests.

  • Assuming activity coefficients are unity for concentrated solutions when calculating colligative properties.
  • Using molarity instead of molality in ΔT = i K m, ignoring temperature‑induced volume changes.
  • Confusing the Henry constant expressed as pressure‑over‑concentration with the dimensionless form, leading to wrong temperature‑dependence signs.
  • Neglecting the common‑ion effect when a complexing ligand also supplies the common ion (e.g., NH₄Cl in AgCl/NH₃ system).
  • Treating the van’t Hoff factor as constant for weak electrolytes across all concentrations.
  • Forgetting to convert Ksp to the appropriate units when the solubility expression involves mixed molarity and molality.
  • Applying the simple ΔT = K m formula to mixed‑solvent systems without correcting the cryoscopic constant for solvent activity.

Thermodynamic Derivation of Colligative Properties from Chemical Potential

The rigorous basis of every colligative effect lies in the equality of the solvent chemical potential in the solution and in the pure phase at equilibrium. For a solvent A at temperature T and pressure P, the condition μAsolution = μApure leads, after expanding μAsolution = μA⦵ + RT ln aA, to the general expression RT ln aA = ΔμA. For freezing‑point depression the pure solid solvent is in equilibrium with the liquid solution, giving ΔμA = ΔHfus (1 – T/Tf⦵) ≈ ΔHfus ΔTf/Tf⦵2. Substituting aA = γA xA and using ln xA ≈ –xB for dilute solutions yields ΔTf = (R Tf⦵2/ΔHfus) xB (γB/γA). The factor in parentheses is the cryoscopic constant Kf. An identical derivation for boiling‑point elevation replaces ΔHfus with ΔHvap and Tf with Tb. Osmotic pressure follows from μAsolution(P) = μApure(P + Π), giving Π VA = –RT ln aA ≈ RT xB (γB/γA) for dilute solutions, which reduces to the van’t Hoff equation Π = i c RT when concentrations are expressed in molarity and activity coefficients are unity. The derivation makes explicit why molality (temperature‑independent) is required for ΔT = i K m, whereas molarity appears naturally in the osmotic‑pressure form. Examiners frequently test the step where ln xA ≈ –xB is replaced by ln aA = ln γA + ln xA, asking candidates to identify the hidden assumption of ideality or to calculate the correction when γA ≠ 1.

Pitzer Ion‑Interaction Model for Concentrated Electrolyte Solutions

The Debye–Hückel limiting law fails above ~0.01 mol kg⁻¹ because it neglects short‑range ion‑pair and triple‑ion interactions. The Pitzer equations retain the Debye–Hückel long‑range term and add virial‑type coefficients β(0), β(1), β(2) for binary cation–anion interactions and Cφ for ternary interactions. For a 1:1 electrolyte the excess Gibbs energy per kilogram of solvent is GE/RT = 2 m [β(0) + β(1) g(α₁√I) + β(2) g(α₂√I)] + m² Cφ, where I is the ionic strength, m the molality, and g(x) = 2(1 – (1 + x) e⁻ˣ)/x². The activity coefficient of the electrolyte follows from ln γ± = |z₊z₋| f(I) + 2 m [β(0) + β(1) g(α₁√I) + β(2) g(α₂√I)] + 3 m² Cφ. The function f(I) = –Aφ [√I/(1 + b√I) + (2/b) ln(1 + b√I)] contains the Debye–Hückel constant Aφ and the empirical parameter b = 1.2 kg½ mol⁻½. Parameters β and C are tabulated for common electrolytes at 298 K and are temperature‑dependent through empirical correlations. The model predicts mean activity coefficients up to 6 mol kg⁻¹ for NaCl and up to 20 mol kg⁻¹ for CaCl₂ with < 2 % error. In examinations, a typical question supplies β(0), β(1), Cφ for a salt and asks for γ± at a given molality; the candidate must evaluate g(α√I) correctly and remember the factor 2 for the binary term. A common trap is to omit the ternary Cφ term when the concentration exceeds 1 mol kg⁻¹, which leads to systematic under‑prediction of γ±.

Worked Example: Pitzer γ± for 2 mol kg⁻¹ NaCl at 298 K
Given β(0) = 0.0765, β(1) = 0.2664, Cφ = 0.00127, Aφ = 0.3915 kg½ mol⁻½, b = 1.2 kg½ mol⁻½, α₁ = 2.0 kg½ mol⁻½. Compute the mean activity coefficient γ±.

1. Ionic strength I = m = 2 mol kg⁻¹ (1:1 electrolyte). √I = 1.414.

2. Debye–Hückel term f(I) = –Aφ [√I/(1 + b√I) + (2/b) ln(1 + b√I)] = –0.3915 [1.414/(1 + 1.2×1.414) + (2/1.2) ln(1 + 1.2×1.414)] = –0.3915 [1.414/2.697 + 1.667 ln(2.697)] = –0.3915 [0.524 + 1.667×0.992] = –0.3915 [0.524 + 1.654] = –0.3915 × 2.178 = –0.852.

3. g(α₁√I) with α₁√I = 2.0×1.414 = 2.828. g(x) = 2(1 – (1 + x) e⁻ˣ)/x² = 2(1 – 3.828 e⁻²·⁸²⁸)/7.998 = 2(1 – 3.828×0.059)/7.998 = 2(1 – 0.226)/7.998 = 2×0.774/7.998 = 0.1935.

4. Binary contribution = 2 m [β(0) + β(1) g] = 2×2 [0.0765 + 0.2664×0.1935] = 4 [0.0765 + 0.0515] = 4×0.128 = 0.512.

5. Ternary contribution = 3 m² Cφ = 3×4×0.00127 = 0.0152.

6. ln γ± = |z₊z₋| f(I) + binary + ternary = 1×(–0.852) + 0.512 + 0.0152 = –0.3248.

7. γ± = e⁻⁰·³²⁴⁸ = 0.723.

Exam‑Day Strategy
When a Pitzer problem appears, first list the given parameters and the required molality. Compute √I once and reuse it. Evaluate the Debye–Hückel term f(I) carefully; the logarithmic part is the most error‑prone. Write the g(x) function explicitly before substituting numbers. Keep at least four significant figures in intermediate steps; the final γ± is usually reported to three. If the question asks for the osmotic coefficient φ, recall φ = 1 – (ln γ±)/(|z₊z₋| f(I) + …) and use the same intermediate values.

Phase Diagrams of Binary Liquid‑Liquid Systems and Critical Solution Temperature

Binary liquid‑liquid equilibrium (LLE) is governed by the equality of component chemical potentials in the two coexisting liquid phases α and β: μiα = μiβ (i = 1,2). The Gibbs free energy of mixing ΔGmix = RT Σ xi ln ai develops a double‑tangent construction when the mixture splits. The binodal curve (solubility curve) encloses the two‑phase region; the spinodal curve, defined by (∂²ΔGmix/∂x₁²)T,P = 0, marks the limit of metastability. The upper critical solution temperature (UCST) is the highest temperature at which phase separation occurs; above it the mixture is miscible in all proportions. Conversely, a lower critical solution temperature (LCST) appears when miscibility decreases on heating, often due to hydrogen‑bond breaking or specific interactions. The critical point satisfies both (∂ΔGmix/∂x₁) = 0 and (∂²ΔGmix/∂x₁²) = 0, giving xc = 0.5 for symmetric systems. The Flory–Huggins lattice model for polymer–solvent systems predicts χc = 0.5 + 1/√N, where N is the degree of polymerisation; thus high‑molecular‑weight polymers exhibit UCST at χ ≈ 0.5. In examinations, a typical question provides a tie‑line composition at a given temperature and asks for the lever‑rule calculation of phase amounts, or supplies the χ parameter as a function of temperature (χ = A + B/T) and asks to locate the UCST. A frequent trap is to confuse the binodal with the spinodal; the binodal is the experimentally observed solubility curve, while the spinodal is a theoretical limit inside the two‑phase region.

Pressure Dependence of Solid Solubility and the Poynting Correction

For a sparingly soluble solid S in a liquid solvent, the equilibrium condition μSsolid(P) = μSsolution(P) leads to the pressure‑corrected solubility product. The molar volume of the solid VS is nearly constant, so μSsolid(P) = μS⦵ + VS(P – P⦵). In the solution, the solute activity aS = γS xS (or γS mS for molality). Equating chemical potentials gives ln aS = –ΔGfus/RT + (VS/RT)(P – P⦵). The exponential term exp[VS(P – P⦵)/RT] is the Poynting correction. For most solids VS ≈ 10⁻⁵ m³ mol⁻¹; at 100 MPa the correction factor is exp[(10⁻⁵×10⁸)/(8.314×298)] ≈ exp(0.403) ≈ 1.50, i.e. a 50 % increase in solubility. In supercritical fluid extraction the pressure effect is dramatic because the solvent density changes, but for liquid solvents the Poynting factor is the dominant pressure term. Examiners may ask to compute the solubility of CaCO₃ at 500 bar given its molar volume (36.9 cm³ mol⁻¹) and Ksp at 1 bar. The correct approach: calculate the Poynting factor, multiply Ksp by it, then solve the usual solubility expression. A common mistake is to apply the pressure correction to the aqueous ions’ activity coefficients instead of to the solid’s chemical potential.

Colligative Properties of Polymer Solutions and the Flory‑Huggins Theory

Polymer solutions deviate strongly from the van’t Hoff law because each macromolecule occupies many lattice sites. In the Flory–Huggins model the solvent volume fraction φ1 and polymer volume fraction φ2 replace mole fractions. The Gibbs free energy of mixing per lattice site is ΔGmix/RT = φ1 ln φ1 + (φ2/N) ln φ2 + χ φ1 φ2, where N is the number of segments per polymer chain and χ is the interaction parameter. The solvent chemical potential becomes μ1 – μ1⦵ = RT[ln φ1 + (1 – 1/N) φ2 + χ φ2²]. For osmotic pressure Π = –(μ1 – μ1⦵)/V1, the dilute‑limit expression is Π = (RT/V1)[φ2/N + (½ – χ) φ2² + …]. The first term yields the number‑average molecular weight Mn via Π/c = RT/Mn (c = mass concentration). The second virial coefficient A₂ = (½ – χ)/V1 governs the concentration dependence; a θ‑solvent (χ = ½) gives ideal‑chain behaviour and A₂ = 0. Freezing‑point depression for polymers follows ΔTf = Kf (φ2/N) (1 + (½ – χ) N φ2 + …). Because N is large (10³–10⁶), the colligative effect per gram of polymer is tiny; osmometry is therefore the preferred method for Mn determination. Examination questions often provide Π/c data at several concentrations and ask for a Zimm plot (Π/c versus c) to extract Mn and A₂. The trap is to use the simple ΔT = i K m formula with the polymer’s molar mass, which underestimates ΔT by a factor of N and yields an apparent molar mass orders of magnitude too high.

  • Assuming the Debye–Hückel limiting law remains valid above 0.01 mol kg⁻¹ and ignoring Pitzer virial coefficients.
  • Confusing the binodal (solubility) curve with the spinodal in liquid‑liquid phase diagrams.
  • Applying the Poynting correction to solute activity coefficients instead of to the solid‑phase chemical potential.
  • Using the van’t Hoff equation Π = i c RT for polymer solutions without the 1/N factor.
  • Neglecting the temperature dependence of the Flory–Huggins χ parameter when locating UCST or LCST.
  • Treating the lever rule as a simple mass balance without converting tie‑line compositions to the same basis (mole fraction vs mass fraction).
  • Forgetting that the cryoscopic constant Kf in mixed solvents must be corrected for the solvent activity coefficient, not taken as the pure‑component value.
The advanced sections above complete the required depth for this lesson.

Osmotic Coefficient and Its Relation to Activity Coefficients

The osmotic coefficient φ quantifies the deviation of a real solution from the ideal van’t Hoff law for osmotic pressure. For a solvent 1 in a solution containing solute species i, the osmotic pressure Π is expressed as Π = φ c RT, where c is the total molar concentration of solute particles (including dissociation). In terms of the solvent activity a₁, φ = –(ln a₁)/(ν m M₁), with ν the number of particles per formula unit, m the molality, and M₁ the molar mass of the solvent. The solvent activity is linked to the solute activity coefficients γᵢ through the Gibbs–Duhem relation: Σ xᵢ d ln γᵢ = –x₁ d ln a₁. For a binary electrolyte MX that dissociates into M⁺ and X⁻, the mean ionic activity coefficient γ± and the osmotic coefficient are connected by the integral φ – 1 = ∫₀ᵐ (γ± – 1)/m dm. In the Debye–Hückel limiting law region (I < 0.01 mol kg⁻¹), both γ± and φ can be expressed as series in √I: ln γ± = –A|z₊z₋|√I and φ – 1 = (A/3)|z₊z₋|√I, where A ≈ 0.509 kg¹ᐟ² mol⁻¹ᐟ² at 298 K. At higher ionic strengths the extended Debye–Hückel or Pitzer equations provide γ±, and φ is obtained by numerical integration or by the Pitzer osmotic coefficient expression φ – 1 = –(Aφ I¹ᐟ²)/(1 + b I¹ᐟ²) + Σ mᵢ λᵢ + Σ mᵢ mⱼ ζᵢⱼ. Experimental determination of φ via vapor‑pressure osmometry or membrane osmometry yields data that, when combined with γ± from electrochemical cells, test the internal consistency of thermodynamic models. A common examination task supplies φ at several molalities and asks for the excess Gibbs energy Gᴱ = ν RT ∫₀ᵐ (φ – 1) dm, which can be fitted to a Margules or Pitzer parameter set.

Solubility of Solids in Supercritical Fluids

Supercritical fluids (SCFs) such as CO₂ above its critical point (T_c = 304.1 K, P_c = 7.38 MPa) exhibit liquid‑like densities and gas‑like diffusivities, making them powerful solvents for solid extraction. The solubility of a solid solute in an SCF is commonly correlated by the Chrastil equation: ln x₂ = k ln ρ + a/T + b, where x₂ is the mole‑fraction solubility, ρ the SCF density (kg m⁻³), and k, a, b are empirical constants. The density ρ is obtained from an equation of state (e.g., Peng–Robinson) at the system temperature and pressure. Thermodynamically, the equilibrium condition μ₂ˢᵒˡⁱᵈ = μ₂ˢᶜᶠ leads to ln x₂ = –ΔH_sub/RT + ΔV_sub (P – P_sub)/RT + ln φ₂, where ΔH_sub is the enthalpy of sublimation, ΔV_sub the molar volume change on sublimation, and φ₂ the fugacity coefficient of the solute in the SCF. For many pharmaceuticals, ΔH_sub ≈ 100–150 kJ mol⁻¹, so solubility increases sharply with pressure because ρ rises, enhancing the solute‑SCF interaction term k ln ρ. The crossover pressure, where solubility begins to decrease with temperature at constant pressure, occurs when the density effect is outweighed by the vapor‑pressure effect. Experimental data are often presented as isotherms (solubility vs. pressure) and isobars (solubility vs. temperature). A typical exam problem provides density data at several (T, P) points and asks to determine the Chrastil constants by linear regression of ln x₂ against ln ρ and 1/T, then predict solubility at a new condition. The trap is to ignore the fugacity coefficient φ₂, which can deviate from unity by 10–30 % near the critical region.

Worked Example: Chrastil Correlation for Naphthalene in Supercritical CO₂
At 313 K, the following solubilities (mole fraction x₂) of naphthalene in CO₂ were measured: (P = 10 MPa, ρ = 600 kg m⁻³, x₂ = 2.1 × 10⁻⁴); (P = 15 MPa, ρ = 750 kg m⁻³, x₂ = 4.8 × 10⁻⁴); (P = 20 MPa, ρ = 850 kg m⁻³, x₂ = 7.9 × 10⁻⁴). Determine the Chrastil constants k, a, b assuming a/T term is constant at this temperature.
Because T is fixed, the Chrastil equation reduces to ln x₂ = k ln ρ + C, where C = a/T + b. Compute ln x₂ and ln ρ for each point: (ln ρ, ln x₂) = (6.397, –8.468), (6.620, –7.642), (6.745, –7.143). Perform linear regression: slope k = Σ[(ln ρᵢ – ⟨ln ρ⟩)(ln x₂ᵢ – ⟨ln x₂⟩)] / Σ[(ln ρᵢ – ⟨ln ρ⟩)²] ≈ 3.2. Intercept C = ⟨ln x₂⟩ – k⟨ln ρ⟩ ≈ –28.5. Thus k ≈ 3.2, a/T + b ≈ –28.5. If a separate isobar at 323 K gives C = –27.0, then a = (C₁ – C₂) / (1/T₁ – 1/T₂) ≈ 1.5 × 10⁴ K, and b = C₁ – a/T₁ ≈ –76. The model predicts x₂ at 313 K, 18 MPa (ρ ≈ 800 kg m⁻³): ln x₂ = 3.2 ln 800 – 28.5 = –7.38 → x₂ ≈ 6.2 × 10⁻⁴.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a supercritical‑fluid solubility question appears, first identify whether the problem supplies density or expects you to calculate it from an equation of state. If density is given, use the simplified Chrastil form ln x₂ = k ln ρ + constant. If not, recall that the Peng–Robinson EOS requires critical constants and acentric factor; you may be provided with a table of ρ(T,P). Always check units: ρ in kg m⁻³, x₂ dimensionless. The constant term a/T + b is temperature‑dependent; a separate isobar or isotherm is needed to separate a and b. In multiple‑choice questions, the correct answer often corresponds to the only option with the right order of magnitude for k (typically 2–5 for aromatic solids).

Effect of Ionic Strength on Solubility Product: Activity Corrections

The thermodynamic solubility product K_sp⁰ is defined in terms of activities: K_sp⁰ = a_Mⁿ⁺ a_Xᵐ⁻ = (γ_M m_M)ⁿ (γ_X m_X)ᵐ. In dilute solutions (I < 0.01 mol kg⁻¹) the Debye–Hückel limiting law gives log γ_i = –A z_i² √I, with A = 0.509 kg¹ᐟ² mol⁻¹ᐟ² at 298 K. Substituting into K_sp⁰ yields the concentration‑based product K_sp = K_sp⁰ / (γ_Mⁿ γ_Xᵐ). For a 1:1 salt such as AgCl, K_sp = K_sp⁰ / γ±². As ionic strength increases, γ± decreases, so the apparent K_sp (based on concentrations) grows. At I = 0.1 mol kg⁻¹, the extended Debye–Hückel equation log γ± = –A|z₊z₋|√I / (1 + Ba√I) with B ≈ 0.328 Å⁻¹ kg¹ᐟ² mol⁻¹ᐟ² and a the ion‑size parameter (≈ 4 Å for Ag⁺, Cl⁻) gives γ± ≈ 0.78, so K_sp ≈ K_sp⁰ / 0.61 ≈ 1.64 K_sp⁰. For sparingly soluble salts with multivalent ions (e.g., Ca₃(PO₄)₂), the effect is dramatic: at I = 0.05 mol kg⁻¹, γ± for Ca²⁺ and PO₄³⁻ can be 0.4–0.5, raising the concentration‑based solubility by an order of magnitude. The Davies equation, log γ_i = –A z_i²[√I/(1 + √I) – 0.3 I], extends reliability to I ≈ 0.5 mol kg⁻¹. In examination problems, you may be given K_sp⁰ and asked to compute the molar solubility s in a solution containing an inert electrolyte (e.g., 0.1 M KNO₃). The steps: (1) estimate I from the inert electrolyte; (2) calculate γ± using Davies; (3) write K_sp⁰ = (ν₊ γ₊ s)ⁿ (ν₋ γ₋ s)ᵐ; (4) solve for s. A frequent error is to use the limiting law beyond its validity or to neglect the contribution of the dissolving salt itself to I, which is usually negligible for sparingly soluble salts but becomes important for moderately soluble ones.

Thermodynamics of Mixed Solvent Electrolyte Solutions: The UNIQUAC Model

The UNIQUAC (Universal Quasi‑Chemical) model provides a framework for activity coefficients in non‑electrolyte and electrolyte mixtures by separating combinatorial (size/shape) and residual (energy) contributions. For a mixture of solvents 1, 2 and an electrolyte MX, the excess Gibbs energy Gᴱ = Gᴱ_comb + Gᴱ_res. The combinatorial term uses volume fractions φ_i = r_i x_i / Σ r_j x_j and surface fractions θ_i = q_i x_i / Σ q_j x_j, where r_i and q_i are relative Van der Waals volume and surface area parameters. Gᴱ_comb/RT = Σ x_i ln(φ_i/x_i) + (z/2) Σ q_i x_i ln(θ_i/φ_i), with coordination number z = 10. The residual term employs binary interaction parameters τ_ij = exp(–u_ij/RT): Gᴱ_res/RT = –Σ q_i x_i ln(Σ θ_j τ_ji). For electrolytes, the ion‑solvent and ion‑ion interactions are introduced via additional τ parameters, often regressed from mean ionic activity coefficient data. The model predicts both solvent activities (needed for colligative properties) and mean ionic activity coefficients γ±. In a typical exam scenario, you are given r, q, and τ for a water‑ethanol‑NaCl system at 298 K and asked to compute the water activity a_w at a specified composition. The procedure: (1) calculate mole fractions of water, ethanol, Na⁺, Cl⁻ (treating the salt as fully dissociated); (2) compute φ_i and θ_i; (3) evaluate Gᴱ_comb and Gᴱ_res; (4) obtain ln γ_i = ∂(Gᴱ/RT)/∂n_i; (5) a_w = x_w γ_w. The UNIQUAC model reduces to the Wilson or NRTL models when q_i = r_i and τ_ij are symmetric. A common trap is to forget that the electrolyte contributes two species to the mole‑fraction sum, which changes φ_i and θ_i significantly even at low salt concentrations.

Nucleation Theory and Metastable Solubility Limits

Classical nucleation theory (CNT) describes the formation of a new solid phase from a supersaturated solution. The Gibbs free energy to form a spherical nucleus of radius r is ΔG = –(4/3)πr³ Δμ/V_m + 4πr² γ, where Δμ = RT ln S is the chemical potential difference (S = c/c_sat is the supersaturation ratio), V_m the molar volume of the solid, and γ the interfacial tension. The critical radius r* = 2γ V_m/Δμ corresponds to the maximum of ΔG; the nucleation barrier ΔG* = (16π/3) γ³ V_m²/Δμ². The steady‑state nucleation rate J = A exp(–ΔG*/kT) depends exponentially on γ³ and inversely on (ln S)². For many inorganic salts, γ ≈ 0.05–0.1 J m⁻², V_m ≈ 3 × 10⁻⁵ m³ mol⁻¹, giving r* on the order of nanometers at modest supersaturation (S ≈ 2). The metastable limit (the highest concentration achievable without spontaneous nucleation) is often defined by a critical nucleation rate J_c ≈ 10⁶ cm⁻³ s⁻¹. Experimentally, the metastable zone width (MZW) is measured by cooling a saturated solution at a constant rate and detecting the first crystal appearance. The MZW narrows with increased cooling rate, stirring, and presence of impurities that lower γ. In exam questions, you may be given γ, V_m, T, and a target nucleation rate, and asked to compute the required supersaturation S. The steps: (1) express ΔG* in terms of S; (2) set J = J_c and solve for ln S; (3) obtain the metastable concentration c_meta = S c_sat. A typical mistake is to use the planar interfacial tension for a curved nucleus without the Tolman correction, or to ignore the kinetic prefactor A, which contains the diffusion coefficient and Zeldovich factor. Another trap is to confuse the thermodynamic solubility product (equilibrium) with the kinetic solubility limit (metastable).

  • Assuming the Debye–Hückel limiting law remains valid above 0.01 mol kg⁻¹ and ignoring extended or Davies corrections.
  • Confusing the osmotic coefficient φ with the activity coefficient γ±; they are related but not interchangeable.
  • Neglecting the fugacity coefficient φ₂ in supercritical‑fluid solubility calculations, especially near the critical point.
  • Treating the Chrastil equation as universally applicable without verifying the density range for which the constants were regressed.
  • Forgetting that the electrolyte contributes two ionic species to the mole‑fraction sum in UNIQUAC, altering φ_i and θ_i.
  • Using the planar interfacial tension γ directly in the nucleation barrier without considering curvature (Tolman) effects.
  • Equating the thermodynamic solubility product K_sp⁰ with the concentration‑based product K_sp in solutions of significant ionic strength.

These advanced topics complete the required depth for the lesson on solutions and colligative properties.

Azeotropy and the Thermodynamic Criterion for Minimum‑ and Maximum‑Boiling Azeotropes

An azeotrope occurs when the vapour and liquid phases of a binary (or multicomponent) mixture have identical compositions at a given temperature and pressure. Thermodynamically this corresponds to an extremum in the total pressure P (at constant T) or in the boiling temperature T (at constant P) as a function of liquid composition x1. For a binary system the total pressure is

P = x1 γ1 P1sat + x2 γ2 P2sat

where γi are activity coefficients and Pisat the pure‑component saturation pressures. The azeotropic condition is

(∂P/∂x1)T = 0 ⇔ γ1 P1sat = γ2 P2sat

At this point the relative volatility α = (y1/x1)/(y2/x2) equals unity. If the deviation from Raoult’s law is positive (γi > 1), the total pressure curve shows a maximum and the azeotrope boils at a temperature lower than either pure component – a minimum‑boiling azeotrope (e.g. ethanol‑water at 1 atm, 95.6 % ethanol, 78.2 °C). If the deviation is negative (γi < 1), the pressure curve shows a minimum and the azeotrope boils at a higher temperature – a maximum‑boiling azeotrope (e.g. acetone‑chloroform, 78 % chloroform, 64.5 °C). The sign of the excess Gibbs energy GE determines the type: GE > 0 gives minimum‑boiling, GE < 0 gives maximum‑boiling. Pressure‑swing distillation exploits the shift of azeotropic composition with pressure; for ethanol‑water the azeotrope moves to higher ethanol content as pressure increases, allowing separation by two columns operating at different pressures.

Gibbs‑Duhem Consistency Test for Vapor‑Liquid Equilibrium Data

The Gibbs‑Duhem equation at constant temperature and pressure, Σ xi d ln γi = 0, provides a rigorous thermodynamic check on experimental VLE data. For a binary mixture it reduces to

x1 d ln γ1 + x2 d ln γ2 = 0

Integrating from x1 = 0 to 1 gives the area test:

∫01 ln(γ1/γ2) dx1 = 0

If the integral deviates significantly from zero, the data are thermodynamically inconsistent. Practical implementations include the Herington test (plot of ln(γ1/γ2) versus x1 should be antisymmetric about x1 = 0.5) and the van Ness test (numerical integration using the trapezoidal rule). A more powerful method is the Barker‑Prausnitz‑Myers approach, which fits the data to a chosen excess‑Gibbs model (Margules, Wilson, NRTL, UNIQUAC) and evaluates the sum of squared residuals in both pressure and composition. In examinations you may be given a table of x1, y1, P at constant T and asked to compute γ1, γ2 from

γ1 = y1 P / (x1 P1sat), γ2 = y2 P / (x2 P2sat)

and then apply the area test. A common pitfall is to use the total pressure P from the data without correcting for the non‑ideality of the vapour phase; at moderate pressures the fugacity coefficient φi should be included, but for most textbook problems φi ≈ 1 is assumed.

Worked Example: Gibbs‑Duhem Area Test
For a binary system at 350 K the following VLE data at 101.3 kPa are reported (pure‑component saturation pressures: P₁ˢᵃᵗ = 85.2 kPa, P₂ˢᵃᵗ = 62.7 kPa).
x₁ = 0.10, y₁ = 0.22
x₁ = 0.30, y₁ = 0.48
x₁ = 0.50, y₁ = 0.68
x₁ = 0.70, y₁ = 0.82
x₁ = 0.90, y₁ = 0.94
Test the thermodynamic consistency using the area test.
1. Compute γ₁ and γ₂ at each point:
γ₁ = y₁ P / (x₁ P₁ˢᵃᵗ), γ₂ = (1‑y₁) P / ((1‑x₁) P₂ˢᵃᵗ).
Results:
x₁=0.10 → γ₁=2.60, γ₂=1.03
x₁=0.30 → γ₁=1.91, γ₂=1.12
x₁=0.50 → γ₁=1.61, γ₂=1.24
x₁=0.70 → γ₁=1.38, γ₂=1.44
x₁=0.90 → γ₁=1.12, γ₂=1.88
2. Evaluate ln(γ₁/γ₂) at each composition:
0.10: 0.93; 0.30: 0.53; 0.50: 0.26; 0.70: ‑0.04; 0.90: ‑0.51.
3. Numerical integration (trapezoidal rule) over x₁ from 0 to 1 (add endpoints x₁=0,1 where ln(γ₁/γ₂)=0 by definition).
Integral ≈ 0.02 ≈ 0 (within experimental scatter).
Conclusion: The data satisfy the Gibbs‑Duhem area test and are thermodynamically consistent.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a VLE consistency question appears, first calculate γ₁ and γ₂ using the given P, Pᵢˢᵃᵗ, xᵢ, yᵢ. Then apply the area test: ∫ ln(γ₁/γ₂) dx₁ = 0. If you have only 3‑4 data points, use the trapezoidal rule; the integral should be close to zero (|value| < 0.05 is usually acceptable). Remember that at x₁=0 and 1 the ratio γ₁/γ₂ = 1, so ln = 0. If the integral is far from zero, state that the data are inconsistent and suggest a possible cause (e.g., vapour‑phase non‑ideality ignored, experimental error).

Setchenov Equation and Gas Solubility in Electrolyte Solutions

The presence of dissolved salts generally reduces the solubility of non‑polar gases – the salting‑out effect. The quantitative relationship is given by the Setchenov equation:

log₁₀(S₀ / S) = ks · I

where S₀ is the gas solubility in pure water, S the solubility in the electrolyte solution, I the ionic strength (mol kg⁻¹), and ks the Setchenov constant (kg mol⁻¹). For many gases ks is positive (salting‑out); a few gases (e.g. HCl, NH₃) show negative ks (salting‑in) because of specific chemical interactions. Typical values at 298 K: O₂ in NaCl, ks ≈ 0.12 kg mol⁻¹; N₂ in NaCl, ks ≈ 0.10 kg mol⁻¹; CO₂ in NaCl, ks ≈ 0.08 kg mol⁻¹. The constant depends on temperature (usually decreasing with rising T) and on the specific ion pair (Hofmeister series). The equation can be extended to mixed electrolytes by using the total ionic strength I = ½ Σ mi zi². In environmental engineering the Setchenov equation is used to correct oxygen transfer rates in wastewater aeration tanks where high salt concentrations prevail. A frequent examination twist is to give the solubility of a gas in pure water at a certain temperature and ask for the solubility in a 0.5 mol kg⁻¹ NaCl solution; the student must compute I = 0.5 (for NaCl) and apply the logarithmic form.

Donnan Equilibrium and Osmotic Pressure of Polyelectrolyte Solutions

When a solution containing a polyelectrolyte (e.g. a protein or a sulfonated polymer) is separated from a simple electrolyte solution by a membrane permeable only to small ions, a Donnan equilibrium establishes. The fixed charges on the macromolecule (concentration Cf, valence zf) require an unequal distribution of mobile ions to maintain electroneutrality in each phase. For a 1:1 salt (NaCl) the Donnan relations are

c+in · c-in = c+out · c-out = cs²

c+in – c-in = –zf Cf

where cs is the external salt concentration. Solving gives the internal ion concentrations and the Donnan potential Δψ = (RT/F) ln(c+in/c+out). The osmotic pressure difference across the membrane is

π = RT [ (c+in + c-in) – 2 cs + Cp ]

where Cp is the molar concentration of polymer chains (often negligible compared with the ion term). For highly charged polyelectrolytes, counter‑ion condensation (Manning theory) reduces the effective charge, modifying the Donnan prediction. In practice, Donnan equilibrium governs ion‑exchange chromatography, dialysis, and the swelling of hydrogel membranes. Examination questions often provide Cf, zf, and external salt concentration, asking for the internal Na⁺ concentration or the osmotic pressure. The key step is to write the electroneutrality condition inside the gel and combine it with the product‑rule for ion activities (assuming ideal dilute behaviour).

UNIFAC Group‑Contribution Method for Activity Coefficients in Multicomponent Mixtures

The UNIFAC (UNIQUAC Functional‑group Activity Coefficients) model predicts activity coefficients for mixtures where experimental binary parameters are unavailable. It splits the excess Gibbs energy into a combinatorial part (size/shape) and a residual part (group‑group interactions). Each molecule i is represented by a set of functional groups k with occurrence numbers νk(i). Group volume Rk and surface area Qk are tabulated (e.g. CH₃: R=0.9011, Q=0.848; OH: R=1.0000, Q=1.200). The combinatorial term uses the UNIQUAC expressions with molecular volume ri = Σ νk(i) Rk and surface area qi = Σ νk(i) Qk:

ln γiC = ln(φi/xi) + (z/2) qi ln(θi/φi) + li – (φi/xi) Σ xj lj

where φi = ri xi/Σ rj xj, θi = qi xi/Σ qj xj, li = (z/2)(ri–qi) – (ri–1), z = 10. The residual term is

ln γiR = Σ νk(i) [ ln Γk – ln Γk(i) ]

with group activity coefficient Γk = exp[ Qk (1 – ln Σ θm Ψmk – Σ (θm Ψkm/Σ θn Ψnm) ) ]. The interaction parameters Ψmn = exp(–amn/T) are regressed from binary VLE/LLE data; amn ≠ anm. UNIFAC is widely used for process‑simulation (distillation, extraction) because it requires only group‑level parameters. Limitations: missing groups for novel molecules, poor accuracy near critical regions, and no explicit pressure dependence (assumes low‑pressure vapour phase). In exams you may be asked to compute γ for a ternary mixture (e.g. water‑ethanol‑ethyl acetate) given the group composition and a table of amn values; the procedure is to calculate ri, qi, then φ, θ, then Γk, and finally γi.

  • Confusing the Setchenov constant ks (log₁₀ base) with a natural‑log version; always check the logarithm base used in the data table.
  • Applying the Gibbs‑Duhem area test without adding the endpoint contributions at x₁ = 0 and 1 where ln(γ₁/γ₂) = 0.
  • Assuming the Donnan equilibrium product rule holds for non‑ideal concentrated solutions; activity coefficients of the mobile ions must be included when I > 0.1 mol kg⁻¹.
  • Using UNIFAC group parameters for a functional group that is not present in the published parameter set (e.g. a novel fluorinated group) and treating the missing amn as zero.
  • Neglecting the pressure‑dependence of the azeotropic composition when designing a pressure‑swing distillation; the shift can be small for maximum‑boiling azeotropes.
  • Treating the combinatorial term of UNIFAC as identical to the Flory‑Huggins expression; the coordination number z = 10 and the li correction are specific to UNIQUAC/UNIFAC.
  • Forgetting that the Setchenov equation is empirical and its constant ks varies with temperature; using a 298 K value at 350 K introduces large error.

Kirkwood‑Buff Theory and Preferential Solvation

The Kirkwood‑Buff (KB) theory provides a rigorous statistical‑mechanical framework that connects microscopic pair‑correlation functions to macroscopic thermodynamic derivatives. For a multicomponent mixture the KB integrals are defined as Gij = ∫0∞ [gij(r) – 1] 4πr² dr, where gij(r) is the radial distribution function between species i and j. These integrals appear directly in the matrix of derivatives of chemical potentials with respect to composition at constant temperature and pressure: (∂μi/∂xj)T,P = RT (δij/xi – 1/ρ Gij), with ρ the total number density. Consequently, KB integrals determine partial molar volumes, isothermal compressibility, and the preferential solvation parameter.

Preferential solvation quantifies the local excess or deficit of a cosolvent around a solute relative to the bulk composition. For a ternary system (solute 3, solvent 1, cosolvent 2) the preferential solvation parameter is δx1,3 = x1 (G13 – G23) / [1 + ρ (x1G11 + x2G12 – x1G13 – x2G23)]. A positive δx1,3 indicates that the solute is preferentially surrounded by solvent 1. This quantity is central to interpreting cosolvent effects on protein stability, enzyme activity, and polymer conformation. Experimentally, KB integrals can be obtained from small‑angle scattering (SAXS/SANS) combined with concentration‑fluctuation analysis, or from molecular‑dynamics simulations where gij(r) are computed directly.

A frequent examination twist is to provide a set of KB integrals (or the underlying gij(r) data) and ask for the sign and magnitude of δx1,3 at a given composition. Students must remember that the denominator contains the Kirkwood‑Buff matrix determinant and that the factor ρ (number density) must be expressed in consistent units (mol m⁻³ or molecules Å⁻³). Another common error is confusing the KB preferential solvation parameter with the simpler “local mole fraction” definition x1,local = x1 (1 + ρ G13) / (1 + ρ Σ xk Gk3), which neglects the cross‑term G23 and is only valid for dilute cosolvent. The rigorous KB expression remains valid up to moderate concentrations and automatically satisfies the Gibbs‑Duhem relation.

In the context of protein solutions, KB theory links preferential hydration (negative δxcosolvent,protein) to the observed increase in chemical potential of the protein upon addition of stabilizing cosolvents such as trehalose or TMAO. The same formalism explains “salting‑out” where δxwater,protein becomes negative at high salt concentrations. Because KB integrals are thermodynamic quantities, they are independent of the microscopic model used to compute them, making the theory a powerful bridge between simulation and experiment.

Hildebrand and Hansen Solubility Parameters for Polymer‑Solvent Systems

The Hildebrand solubility parameter δ = √(ΔEvap/Vm) provides a single‑number estimate of the cohesive energy density of a material. For non‑polar systems the Flory‑Huggins interaction parameter χ can be approximated as χ ≈ Vseg(δ1 – δ2)²/RT, where Vseg is the molar volume of a polymer segment. This works well for hydrocarbon polymers in hydrocarbon solvents but fails when polar or hydrogen‑bonding interactions dominate.

Hansen extended the concept by splitting δ into three orthogonal contributions: dispersion (δd), polar (δp), and hydrogen‑bonding (δh). The distance between a polymer (subscript 1) and a solvent (subscript 2) in Hansen space is Ra = √[4(δd1 – δd2)² + (δp1 – δp2)² + (δh1 – δh2)²]. The factor 4 on the dispersion term reflects its larger influence on cohesive energy. Each polymer has an experimentally determined interaction radius R0; the relative energy difference RED = Ra/R0 predicts miscibility: RED < 1 indicates good solubility, RED ≈ 1 borderline, RED > 1 poor solubility.

Hansen parameters are tabulated for hundreds of solvents and polymers. For a new solvent, one can estimate its δ components from group‑contribution methods (e.g., Hoftyzer‑Van Krevelen) or from measured refractive index, dielectric constant, and enthalpy of vaporization. Temperature dependence is often neglected in introductory problems, but δ decreases with temperature because ΔEvap falls faster than Vm expands. In high‑precision work the temperature correction δ(T) = δ(298 K) [1 – α (T – 298)] with α ≈ 0.001 K⁻¹ is applied.

Examiners frequently test the ability to compute Ra and RED from a given table of Hansen parameters and to decide whether a polymer will dissolve. A typical trap is to forget the factor 4 on the dispersion term, which can change RED by 20–30 %. Another is to use Hildebrand δ alone for a polar polymer such as poly(vinyl alcohol) and incorrectly predict solubility in a non‑polar solvent. The Hansen approach also underpins solvent‑selection guides for polymer processing, membrane fabrication, and drug‑delivery vehicle design.

Worked Example: Hansen RED Calculation
A polymer has Hansen parameters δd = 18.0, δp = 5.0, δh = 8.0 MPa½ and an interaction radius R0 = 7.0 MPa½. A candidate solvent has δd = 16.0, δp = 4.0, δh = 6.0 MPa½. Determine RED and predict miscibility.
Compute the squared differences: Δδd = 2.0, Δδp = 1.0, Δδh = 2.0. Apply the Hansen distance formula: Ra = √[4(2.0)² + (1.0)² + (2.0)²] = √[4×4 + 1 + 4] = √[16 + 1 + 4] = √21 ≈ 4.58 MPa½. RED = Ra/R0 = 4.58/7.0 ≈ 0.65. Since RED < 1, the solvent is predicted to be a good solvent for the polymer.
Exam‑Day Strategy
Memorise the Hansen distance formula with the factor 4 on the dispersion term. Practice unit conversion: Hansen parameters are often given in (cal cm⁻³)½ or (J cm⁻³)½; 1 (cal cm⁻³)½ = 2.045 MPa½. When a problem supplies only Hildebrand δ, you cannot compute RED — you must state that Hansen parameters are required. For polymer‑solvent pairs, always check whether the polymer’s R0 is provided; if not, the question is incomplete.

Thermodynamics of Micellization and Critical Micelle Concentration

Micellization is the spontaneous aggregation of surfactant monomers into colloidal‑sized micelles above a characteristic concentration known as the critical micelle concentration (CMC). Two principal thermodynamic models are used: the phase‑separation model (treating micelles as a distinct phase) and the mass‑action model (treating micelle formation as a reversible equilibrium). For non‑ionic surfactants the phase‑separation model gives the standard Gibbs free energy of micellization per mole of monomer as ΔGm° = RT ln(CMC), where CMC is expressed in mole fraction or molarity (the standard state must be consistent). For ionic surfactants the counter‑ion binding degree α (fraction of surfactant charge neutralised by bound counter‑ions) modifies the expression to ΔGm° = (2 – α) RT ln(CMC). The factor (2 – α) arises because each micelle incorporates (1 – α) free counter‑ions per surfactant head group.

The standard enthalpy ΔHm° and entropy ΔSm° of micellization are obtained from the temperature dependence of the CMC via a van’t Hoff plot: ln(CMC) versus 1/T yields a slope of –ΔHm°/R. For many surfactants ΔHm° is small near room temperature, making micellization entropy‑driven (hydrophobic effect). The Krafft temperature TK is the temperature at which the surfactant solubility equals the CMC; below TK the surfactant precipitates before micelles can form. TK increases with hydrocarbon chain length and decreases with added electrolyte for ionic surfactants.

Counter‑ion binding α is commonly measured by conductivity: the ratio of the slope of conductivity versus concentration above the CMC to that below the CMC equals α. Typical values are α ≈ 0.2–0.3 for sodium alkyl sulfates and α ≈ 0.7–0.8 for cetyltrimethylammonium bromide. The degree of ionisation β = 1 – α appears in the expression for the micellar charge and influences the electrostatic contribution to ΔGm°. In mixed surfactant systems the CMC of the mixture can be predicted by Clint’s equation (ideal mixing) or Rubingh’s regular‑solution theory (non‑ideal mixing with interaction parameter βM).

Examination questions often provide CMC values at two temperatures and ask for ΔHm°, ΔSm°, and ΔGm° at a third temperature. A common pitfall is using the natural logarithm of CMC in mol L⁻¹ without converting to mole fraction, which introduces a constant offset in ΔGm° but cancels in ΔHm° and ΔSm°. Another trap is forgetting the (2 – α) factor for ionic surfactants, leading to a systematic underestimation of ΔGm° by ~30 %. Students should also recognise that the CMC is not a sharp phase transition but a narrow concentration range; the “CMC” reported in literature is usually the midpoint of that range.

Phase Equilibria in Ternary Liquid‑Liquid Systems: Tie‑lines and Plait Points

When three components form two liquid phases at constant temperature and pressure, the Gibbs phase rule gives F = C – P + 2 = 3 – 2 + 2 = 3 degrees of freedom. Fixing T and P leaves one compositional degree of freedom, which is represented by a tie‑line connecting the equilibrium compositions of the two conjugate phases on a ternary diagram. The ends of a tie‑line are the binodal (saturation) points; the locus of all binodal points forms the binodal curve. The plait point (or critical solution point) is the unique composition where the two phases become identical — the tie‑line length shrinks to zero and the binodal curve has a horizontal tangent in the appropriate projection.

The lever rule applies along each tie‑line: for an overall composition z lying on a tie‑line between phase α (composition xα) and phase β (composition xβ), the phase fractions are φα = (z – xβ)/(xα – xβ) and φβ = 1 – φα. This is valid for any conserved quantity (mass, mole fraction) provided the tie‑line is straight in the chosen coordinate system. In practice, ternary diagrams are plotted in mass‑fraction or mole‑fraction coordinates; tie‑lines are straight in both, but their slopes differ.

Experimental determination of the binodal curve uses the cloud‑point method: a homogeneous mixture is titrated with a third component until turbidity appears, marking the binodal. Tie‑line endpoints are found by equilibrating a mixture of known overall composition, separating the phases (e.g., by centrifugation), and analysing each phase (refractive index, density, chromatography). The plait point can be located by extrapolating tie‑line endpoints or by measuring the critical opalescence as the composition approaches the plait point.

In liquid‑liquid extraction, the distribution coefficient K = xα/xβ for a solute is constant along a tie‑line only if the system is dilute; otherwise K varies with composition. The selectivity of a solvent is assessed by the ratio of distribution coefficients for two solutes. Examiners often provide a ternary diagram with a few tie‑lines and ask for the phase compositions and amounts for a given feed composition. The key steps are: locate the feed point, identify the tie‑line it lies on (by interpolation between known tie‑lines), read the endpoint compositions, and apply the lever rule. A frequent error is using the wrong coordinate basis (mass vs. mole) for the lever rule, which yields incorrect phase fractions.

Krichevsky‑Kasarnovsky Equation for Gas Solubility at High Pressures

At pressures significantly above the gas saturation pressure, Henry’s constant is no longer constant; it varies with pressure because the fugacity of the dissolved gas depends on the total pressure. The Krichevsky‑Kasarnovsky (KK) equation provides the rigorous thermodynamic correction: ln(HP/H0) = (V̄g∞/RT) (P – Psat), where HP is the Henry constant at pressure P, H0 is the Henry constant at the gas saturation pressure Psat (often approximated as 1 bar for gases well below their critical temperature), and V̄g∞ is the partial molar volume of the gas at infinite dilution in the liquid. The derivation starts from the equality of chemical potentials of the gas in the vapour and liquid phases, μgv = μgl, and uses the definition of fugacity and the Gibbs‑Duhem relation for the liquid phase.

V̄g∞ is typically positive (10–50 cm³ mol⁻¹ for common gases in water at 298 K) and can be estimated from the gas’s molar volume in the liquid state or from volumetric measurements. For supercritical gases (e.g., CO₂ at 313 K) the KK equation remains valid provided the gas‑phase fugacity coefficient φg is used: ln(φg P / H0 xg) = (V̄g∞/RT) (P – Psat). This form is the basis for correlating high‑pressure gas solubility data in supercritical fluid extraction.

A common examination problem supplies H0 (or the Henry constant at 1 bar), V̄g∞, temperature, and a target pressure (e.g., 100 bar), and asks for the mole fraction solubility xg. The student must compute the pressure correction factor exp[V̄g∞(P – 1 bar)/RT] and multiply H0 by it to obtain HP, then use xg = φg P / HP. If the gas is far from critical, φg ≈ 1. A frequent mistake is to use the gas molar volume instead of the partial molar volume at infinite dilution, which can differ by a factor of 2–3. Another trap is neglecting the Psat term; for gases with high vapour pressure (e.g., propane at 298 K, Psat ≈ 8 bar) the correction (P – Psat) is essential.

The KK equation also underpins the thermodynamic consistency test for high‑pressure VLE data: a plot of ln(HP) versus P should be linear with slope V̄g∞/RT. Deviations indicate experimental error or the onset of specific chemical interactions (e.g., gas‑induced association). In the limit of low pressure the KK equation reduces to Henry’s law, confirming its consistency with the dilute‑solution framework.

  • Confusing the Kirkwood‑Buff preferential solvation parameter δx1,3 with the simpler local mole fraction definition; the latter omits the cross‑term G23 and fails at moderate cosolvent concentrations.
  • Forgetting the factor 4 on the dispersion term in the Hansen distance formula, which leads to systematic underestimation of Ra and erroneous miscibility predictions.
  • Using the natural logarithm of CMC in mol L⁻¹ without converting to mole fraction when calculating ΔGm°; the offset cancels for ΔHm° and ΔSm° but not for ΔGm°.
  • Omitting the (2 – α) factor for ionic surfactants in the micellization free‑energy expression, causing a 20–30 % error in ΔGm°.
  • Applying the lever rule in mass‑fraction coordinates while the tie‑line endpoints are given in mole fractions (or vice‑versa), which yields incorrect phase fractions.
  • Replacing the partial molar volume at infinite dilution V̄g∞ with the pure‑gas molar volume in the Krichevsky‑Kasarnovsky equation, introducing large errors in high‑pressure solubility calculations.
  • Neglecting the gas saturation pressure Psat in the KK pressure correction for gases with appreciable vapour pressure at the system temperature.

Eutectic and Peritectic Phase Diagrams in Binary Solid‑Liquid Systems

Binary solid‑liquid phase diagrams are constructed from the equality of chemical potentials of each component in the co‑existing solid and liquid phases. For a simple eutectic system the liquidus curves are given by the van’t Hoff relation ln x₁ = –ΔH_fus,1/R (1/T – 1/T_fus,1) and the analogous expression for component 2, where ΔH_fus is the molar enthalpy of fusion and T_fus the pure‑component melting temperature. The eutectic point (T_eut, x_eut) is the intersection of the two liquidus curves; at this composition the liquid solidifies into a mixture of two pure solid phases of fixed composition. The lever rule applied on a horizontal tie‑line at any temperature between the liquidus and the solidus gives the mass (or mole) fractions of liquid and solid: f_liquid = (x_solid – x_overall)/(x_solid – x_liquid). In a peritectic system one of the solid phases is a stoichiometric compound (AB) that melts incongruently. The peritectic reaction L + α → β occurs at a fixed temperature T_peri; the liquidus of the compound meets the solidus of the primary phase α at the peritectic composition. The phase diagram therefore contains three two‑phase regions (L+α, L+β, α+β) and a single three‑phase horizontal line at T_peri. Examination questions frequently provide a diagram with only the liquidus curves drawn and ask for the eutectic composition, the temperature at which a given alloy becomes completely solid, or the relative amounts of phases at a specified temperature. A common mistake is to apply the lever rule using weight fractions when the diagram axes are in mole fractions, or to treat the peritectic horizontal as a simple eutectic tie‑line.

Solubility of Gases in Mixed Solvents: Cosolvency and Anti‑cosolvency

When a gas dissolves in a binary solvent mixture the Henry constant H_mix is not a linear combination of the pure‑solvent constants. The Setchenov‑type relation ln(H_mix/H₁) = k_s x₂ (where x₂ is the mole fraction of the co‑solvent and k_s the Setchenov coefficient) describes the first‑order effect, but real systems often show a pronounced minimum or maximum in gas solubility as a function of solvent composition. Cosolvency occurs when the gas is more soluble in the mixture than in either pure solvent; anti‑cosolvency is the opposite. The phenomenon originates from preferential solvation: the local mole fraction of the more “gas‑philic” solvent around the solute differs from the bulk composition. Kirkwood‑Buff integrals G_ij quantify this preferential solvation; the local mole fraction of solvent 1 around the gas is x₁^local = x₁ + x₂ ρ (G₁₃ – G₂₃), where ρ is the number density and the subscript 3 denotes the gas. A positive (G₁₃ – G₂₃) indicates enrichment of solvent 1 near the gas, leading to cosolvency if solvent 1 has a lower pure‑solvent Henry constant. Experimental determination of H_mix at several compositions allows extraction of k_s and, with density data, the Kirkwood‑Buff integrals. In examinations, a typical problem supplies Henry constants for the pure solvents, the Setchenov coefficient, and a target mixture composition; the student must compute H_mix and then the equilibrium mole fraction of dissolved gas at a given partial pressure using x_gas = P_gas/H_mix. A frequent trap is to ignore the pressure dependence of the gas‑phase fugacity coefficient when the total pressure exceeds ~10 bar, or to assume ideal mixing of Henry constants (i.e., H_mix = x₁H₁ + x₂H₂), which is only valid for very dilute co‑solvent concentrations.

Worked Example: Cosolvency Calculation
At 298 K the Henry constants for O₂ in water and in ethanol are H_w = 4.4 × 10⁴ bar and H_e = 1.2 × 10⁴ bar. The Setchenov coefficient for ethanol in water is k_s = –0.85 (negative sign indicates cosolvency). For a solvent mixture containing 0.30 mole fraction ethanol, calculate the equilibrium mole fraction of O₂ when the gas phase partial pressure is 0.21 bar.
First compute the mixture Henry constant using the Setchenov relation: ln(H_mix/H_w) = k_s x_e = –0.85 × 0.30 = –0.255. Hence H_mix = H_w exp(–0.255) = 4.4 × 10⁴ × 0.775 = 3.41 × 10⁴ bar. Then x_O₂ = P_O₂/H_mix = 0.21 / 3.41 × 10⁴ = 6.16 × 10⁻⁶. The result shows a higher O₂ solubility than in pure water (x = 0.21/4.4×10⁴ = 4.77×10⁻⁶), confirming cosolvency.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a problem gives both pure‑solvent Henry constants and a Setchenov coefficient, always use the exponential form ln(H_mix/H₁)=k_s x₂ rather than a linear interpolation. Check the sign of k_s: negative → cosolvency, positive → anti‑cosolvency. If the total pressure is above 10 bar, convert the gas partial pressure to fugacity using a suitable equation of state before applying Henry’s law.

Electrolyte Solutions: Debye‑Hückel Limiting Law and Extended Models

The Debye‑Hückel limiting law (DHLL) gives the mean ionic activity coefficient γ± for a dilute electrolyte of valence type z₊z₋ as log₁₀ γ± = –A |z₊z₋| √I, where I = ½ Σ m_i z_i² is the ionic strength (mol kg⁻¹) and A = 0.509 kg½ mol⁻½ at 298 K in water. The law is strictly valid only for I < 0.01 mol kg⁻¹. For higher concentrations the extended Debye‑Hückel equation adds a linear term in I: log₁₀ γ± = –A |z₊z₋| √I / (1 + B a √I) + b I, where B = 0.328 Å⁻¹ kg½ mol⁻½, a is the ion‑size parameter (typically 3–9 Å), and b is an empirical coefficient. The Davies equation, widely used in geochemistry, replaces the denominator with (1 + √I) and adds a 0.3 I term: log₁₀ γ± = –A |z₊z₋| (√I/(1+√I) – 0.3 I). For very concentrated solutions (I > 1 mol kg⁻¹) specific ion‑interaction models such as Pitzer’s equations or the e‑NRTL model are required; they introduce binary (β⁽⁰⁾, β⁽¹⁾) and ternary (Cφ) interaction parameters fitted to osmotic coefficient and activity data. Examination questions often ask to compute γ± for a 0.01 m NaCl solution using DHLL, then to comment on the deviation when the same calculation is performed at 0.1 m using the Davies equation. A typical trap is to use the molar concentration (mol L⁻¹) instead of molality in the ionic strength expression, which introduces a density error of ~2 % at 0.1 m. Another common error is to forget the absolute value of the charge product |z₊z₋|, leading to a sign mistake in the logarithm.

Phase Separation in Polymer Solutions: Spinodal and Binodal Curves

In polymer‑solvent systems the Flory‑Huggins free energy of mixing per lattice site is ΔG_mix/RT = φ_p ln φ_p + (1–φ_p) ln(1–φ_p) + χ φ_p(1–φ_p), where φ_p is the polymer volume fraction and χ the interaction parameter. The binodal (coexistence) curve is obtained from the common‑tangent construction on ΔG_mix(φ_p), i.e., the compositions φ_p^α and φ_p^β that satisfy equality of chemical potentials and osmotic pressure: μ_p(φ_p^α) = μ_p(φ_p^β) and Π(φ_p^α) = Π(φ_p^β). The spinodal curve marks the limit of metastability and is defined by the inflection point of the free energy: ∂²(ΔG_mix/RT)/∂φ_p² = 0, which yields 1/(N φ_p) + 1/(1–φ_p) – 2χ = 0 for a polymer of degree of polymerization N. For high N the critical point occurs at φ_p,crit ≈ N⁻¹ᐟ² and χ_crit ≈ ½ + N⁻¹ᐟ². Inside the spinodal region the mixture is unstable and phase separates by spinodal decomposition (continuous composition fluctuations); between the spinodal and binodal it is metastable and separates by nucleation and growth. Light‑scattering experiments measure the scattering intensity I(q) ∝ 1/(∂²ΔG/∂φ_p² + κq²), providing direct access to the spinodal. In examinations, a typical problem gives χ = 0.55, N = 10⁴, and asks for the critical polymer volume fraction and the binodal compositions at a temperature where χ = 0.52. The student must solve the spinodal equation for φ_p,crit, then use the common‑tangent condition (often solved numerically) for the binodal. A frequent mistake is to treat the polymer as a monomer (N = 1) when applying the Flory‑Huggins expressions, which shifts the critical χ by a factor of two.

Thermodynamics of Hydrate Formation

Gas hydrates are inclusion compounds in which water forms a hydrogen‑bonded lattice that traps guest molecules (e.g., CH₄, CO₂, N₂). The phase equilibrium between hydrate, water (or ice), and the gas phase is described by the van der Waals‑Platteeuw model. The chemical potential difference of water in the empty hydrate lattice (β) and in the filled hydrate is Δμ_w^β = RT Σ_i ν_i ln(1 – θ_i), where ν_i is the number of cages of type i per water molecule and θ_i the fractional occupancy of cage i. The occupancy follows a Langmuir isotherm: θ_i = C_i f / (1 + C_i f), with C_i the Langmuir constant (function of temperature) and f the fugacity of the guest gas. At equilibrium the chemical potential of water in the hydrate equals that in the coexisting water/ice phase, giving the hydrate dissociation pressure P_diss at a given temperature. The Clapeyron slope dP/dT = ΔH_diss / (T ΔV) is positive for most hydrates, so increasing pressure stabilises the hydrate. In practice, the model requires the Langmuir constants (often correlated by the Kihara potential) and the empty‑lattice chemical potential difference Δμ_w^β (obtained from experimental dissociation data). Examination questions may provide C_i values at two temperatures and ask to estimate the dissociation pressure at an intermediate temperature using the van’t Hoff relation for C_i: ln C_i = –ΔH_ads,i / RT + const. A common trap is to confuse the gas fugacity with the total system pressure; at pressures above ~30 bar the fugacity coefficient φ deviates significantly from unity and must be calculated from an appropriate equation of state (e.g., Peng‑Robinson). Another error is to neglect the contribution of the empty‑lattice term Δμ_w^β, which is essential for quantitative prediction of the three‑phase equilibrium line.

Common Traps

  • Applying the lever rule with weight fractions on a mole‑fraction phase diagram, or vice‑versa.
  • Assuming linear mixing of Henry constants for gas solubility in mixed solvents instead of using the Setchenov or Kirkwood‑Buff formalism.
  • Using molar concentration instead of molality when calculating ionic strength for Debye‑Hückel or Davies equations.
  • Forgetting the absolute value of the charge product |z₊z₋| in the Debye‑Hückel limiting law.
  • Treating a high‑molecular‑weight polymer as a monomer (N = 1) in Flory‑Huggins spinodal/binodal calculations.
  • Neglecting the gas‑phase fugacity coefficient when converting total pressure to fugacity for hydrate equilibrium at moderate to high pressures.
  • Omitting the empty‑lattice chemical potential term Δμ_w^β in the van der Waals‑Platteeuw hydrate model.

Mastering these advanced topics and their associated pitfalls will give you a decisive edge in any competitive chemistry examination.

Specific Ion Interaction Theory and the Bromley Model for Concentrated Electrolyte Solutions

The Debye–Hückel limiting law and its extended forms lose quantitative accuracy above ionic strengths of roughly 0.1 mol kg⁻¹ because they treat ions as point charges in a continuous dielectric. Two widely used semi‑empirical frameworks that extend the usable range to 5–6 mol kg⁻¹ are the Specific Ion Interaction Theory (SIT) and the Bromley model. Both retain the Debye–Hückel term for long‑range electrostatic forces and add short‑range binary interaction parameters that are specific to each ion pair.

In SIT the activity coefficient of ion i is expressed as

log₁₀ γᵢ = –zᵢ² A √I / (1 + 1.5 √I) + Σⱼ ε(i,j) mⱼ

where A is the Debye–Hückel constant (0.509 kg¹ᐟ² mol⁻¹ᐟ² at 25 °C for water), I is the molal ionic strength, ε(i,j) are the SIT interaction coefficients (units kg mol⁻¹) for the pair i–j, and mⱼ is the molality of ion j. The summation runs over all other ions present. The ε parameters are tabulated for many common ion pairs (e.g., ε(Na⁺,Cl⁻) = 0.03 kg mol⁻¹, ε(Mg²⁺,SO₄²⁻) = 0.25 kg mol⁻¹) and are assumed to be independent of ionic strength. SIT is particularly convenient for geochemical speciation codes because the binary parameters are additive and the model remains thermodynamically consistent when the Gibbs–Duhem relation is applied.

The Bromley equation takes a slightly different functional form:

log₁₀ γ± = –A |z₊z₋| √I / (1 + √I) + (0.06 + 0.6 B) I + Σₖ Bₖ mₖ

Here γ± is the mean activity coefficient of the electrolyte, B is a parameter that depends on the charge type (e.g., B ≈ 0.1 for 1:1 electrolytes, 0.2 for 2:1, 0.3 for 2:2), and Bₖ are binary interaction coefficients for the electrolyte–ion pairs. The term (0.06 + 0.6 B)I accounts for the linear increase of log γ with I observed experimentally. Bromley parameters are available for hundreds of electrolytes and are often used in industrial process simulators for brine chemistry.

Typical examination problems supply a set of ε or Bₖ values and ask for the mean activity coefficient of a mixed electrolyte at I = 3 mol kg⁻¹. A common mistake is to apply the Debye–Hückel extended law (with a fixed ion‑size parameter å) beyond its validity range, which underestimates γ by 20–40 %. Another trap is to neglect the cross‑terms Σ ε(i,j)mⱼ when more than two ions are present, effectively treating the solution as a single‑electrolyte system.

Thermodynamics of Protein Solubility: Hofmeister Series and Preferential Interaction Parameters

Protein solubility is governed by a delicate balance between protein–protein, protein–solvent, and solvent–solvent interactions. The Hofmeister series ranks ions according to their ability to precipitate (salting‑out) or enhance (salting‑in) protein solubility. The classical order for anions is SO₄²⁻ > HPO₄²⁻ > CH₃COO⁻ > Cl⁻ > NO₃⁻ > ClO₄⁻ > SCN⁻, while for cations it is NH₄⁺ > K⁺ > Na⁺ > Li⁺ > Mg²⁺ > Ca²⁺. Modern interpretation uses the preferential interaction parameter Γ₂₃ (also written Γ₃₂), defined as the excess number of cosolvent (ion) molecules in the vicinity of the protein relative to bulk solvent:

Γ₂₃ = (∂μ₂/∂μ₃)ₜ,ₚ,ₙ₂

where μ₂ is the chemical potential of the protein and μ₃ that of the cosolvent. A negative Γ₂₃ indicates preferential exclusion (salting‑out), while a positive value signals preferential binding (salting‑in). The linkage between Γ₂₃ and the solubility S is given by the Wyman–Tanford relation:

(∂ln S/∂μ₃)ₜ,ₚ = –Γ₂₃ / RT

Experimentally, Γ₂₃ is obtained from densimetry, vapor‑pressure osmometry, or isothermal titration calorimetry. For many globular proteins, Γ₂₃ varies linearly with salt concentration at low I, allowing the extraction of a salting‑out constant kₛ from the Setchenov‑type equation log S = log S₀ – kₛ I. The magnitude of kₛ correlates with the Hofmeister position: sulfate has kₛ ≈ 0.8 M⁻¹ for lysozyme, whereas thiocyanate gives a negative kₛ (salting‑in).

Examination questions often provide a table of Γ₂₃ values at several salt concentrations and ask to compute the change in solubility when the salt concentration is doubled. The student must integrate the Wyman–Tanford relation assuming Γ₂₃ is constant over the interval, yielding ln(S₂/S₁) = –Γ₂₃ Δμ₃/RT. A frequent error is to confuse the sign of Γ₂₃: preferential exclusion (negative Γ₂₃) leads to decreased solubility, so the logarithm of the solubility ratio becomes positive when Δμ₃ is positive. Another pitfall is to treat the Hofmeister series as universal; specific protein surface chemistry can invert the order for certain ion pairs.

Worked Example: Bromley Activity Coefficient for NaCl at High Ionic Strength
Calculate the mean activity coefficient γ± of NaCl in a 3.0 mol kg⁻¹ NaCl solution at 25 °C using the Bromley model. Use A = 0.509 kg¹ᐟ² mol⁻¹ᐟ², B = 0.1 for 1:1 electrolytes, and the binary interaction coefficient B_NaCl = 0.035 kg mol⁻¹.

Step 1: Compute ionic strength I. For a single 1:1 electrolyte, I = m = 3.0 mol kg⁻¹.

Step 2: Evaluate the Debye–Hückel term: –A|z₊z₋|√I/(1+√I) = –0.509×1×√3/(1+√3) = –0.509×1.732/2.732 = –0.323.

Step 3: Evaluate the linear term: (0.06+0.6B)I = (0.06+0.6×0.1)×3.0 = (0.06+0.06)×3.0 = 0.12×3.0 = 0.36.

Step 4: Evaluate the binary interaction term: Σ Bₖ mₖ = B_NaCl × m = 0.035 × 3.0 = 0.105.

Step 5: Sum contributions: log₁₀ γ± = –0.323 + 0.36 + 0.105 = 0.142.

Step 6: Convert to γ±: γ± = 100.142 = 1.39.

Thus the mean activity coefficient of NaCl at 3 mol kg⁻¹ is approximately 1.39, indicating strong deviation from ideality.

Exam‑Day Strategy
When a problem asks for an activity coefficient above 1 mol kg⁻¹, first check whether the Debye–Hückel extended law is explicitly required. If not, default to SIT or Bromley because they are the standard models for concentrated brines. Memorise the typical B values for 1:1, 2:1, and 2:2 electrolytes (0.1, 0.2, 0.3) and the form of the linear term (0.06+0.6B)I. For protein solubility, remember that Γ₂₃ < 0 means salting‑out; the Setchenov constant kₛ is positive for salting‑out ions. Always verify the sign convention in the given equation before integrating.

Solubility of Solids in Mixed Solvents: The Jouyban–Acree Model and Cosolvency Phenomena

Predicting the solubility of a solid solute in a binary solvent mixture is a recurring challenge in pharmaceutical and chemical engineering examinations. The Jouyban–Acree model provides a compact, thermodynamically grounded correlation that captures both ideal mixing and specific cosolvency (synergistic) effects. For a solute at temperature T, the logarithm of the molar solubility x₂ in a mixture of solvents 1 and 3 is expressed as

log₁₀ x₂ = w₁ log₁₀ x₂¹ + w₃ log₁₀ x₂³ + w₁ w₃ Σᵢ₌₀ⁿ Jᵢ (w₁ – w₃)ⁱ

where w₁ and w₃ are the mass fractions of the pure solvents, x₂¹ and x₂³ are the solubilities in the pure solvents, and Jᵢ are temperature‑dependent interaction parameters (usually n = 1 or 2 suffices). The first two terms represent the log‑linear ideal mixing rule; the summation accounts for non‑ideal cosolvency. When J₀ is positive, the mixture exhibits positive cosolvency (solubility higher than either pure solvent); a negative J₀ indicates anti‑cosolvency.

The model can be extended to ternary solvent systems by adding a third mass fraction and pairwise interaction terms. At 25 °C, typical J₀ values for drug–water–ethanol systems range from –0.5 to +2.0. For example, the solubility of ibuprofen in water–ethanol shows a pronounced maximum near 0.3 mass fraction ethanol, with J₀ ≈ 1.2 and J₁ ≈ –0.3. The model parameters are obtained by regressing experimental solubility data at a minimum of three mixture compositions.

Examination problems often supply solubility data in pure solvents and at one or two mixture compositions, then ask to determine J₀ and J₁ and predict the solubility at a new composition. A common trap is to use mole fractions instead of mass fractions in the Jouyban–Acree equation; the original formulation is strictly mass‑fraction based. Another error is to assume the log‑linear rule (Jᵢ = 0) when the data clearly show a maximum or minimum, leading to large systematic deviations. Students should also remember that the model is empirical; extrapolation beyond the composition range used for parameter fitting is unreliable.

Phase Equilibria in Multicomponent Systems: Gibbs Phase Rule, Residue Curve Maps, and Azeotropic Distillation

The Gibbs phase rule for a non‑reactive system at constant temperature and pressure reduces to F = C – P + 1, where F is the number of degrees of freedom, C the number of components, and P the number of phases. In distillation design, the rule governs the topology of residue curve maps (RCMs) for ternary and higher mixtures. A residue curve traces the liquid composition trajectory during a simple batch distillation at constant pressure; its direction is given by the difference between vapor and liquid compositions (y – x). Fixed points of the RCM correspond to pure components, binary azeotropes, and ternary azeotropes. The stability of these fixed points is classified by the eigenvalues of the Jacobian matrix ∂(y–x)/∂x: stable nodes (pure components with lowest boiling point), unstable nodes (highest boiling point), and saddle points (intermediate boiling azeotropes).

For a ternary system exhibiting a minimum‑boiling binary azeotrope between components 1 and 2, the RCM shows a separatrix that divides the composition triangle into two distillation regions. No continuous distillation column can cross a separatrix; therefore, the choice of entrainer for extractive or azeotropic distillation must place the feed composition in the same region as the desired product. The feasibility of separation is assessed by the “distillation boundary” criterion: if the feed and product lie on opposite sides of a boundary, a single column cannot achieve the split.
