Typical exam questions provide a ternary boiling‑point diagram with azeotropic compositions and ask to sketch the RCM, identify the distillation regions, and propose an entrainer that eliminates the azeotrope. The student must apply the Gibbs phase rule to count the degrees of freedom at the azeotropic points (F = 0 at constant P for a binary azeotrope in a ternary system) and use the tangent‑pinch condition to verify that the entrainer forms a new azeotrope that shifts the boundary. A frequent mistake is to ignore the pressure dependence of azeotropic composition; a change of 0.5 bar can move a binary azeotrope enough to open or close a distillation region.

Quantum Chemical Solubility Prediction: COSMO‑RS and σ‑Profile Based Approaches

Conductor‑like Screening Model for Real Solvents (COSMO‑RS) predicts solubility from quantum‑chemical surface charge densities without any experimental fitting parameters for the specific solute–solvent pair. The workflow begins with a DFT calculation (typically BP86/TZVP) of the solute and solvent molecules embedded in a virtual conductor, generating a screening charge density σ on the molecular surface. The σ‑profile p(σ) is the histogram of surface segments having a given σ value. The chemical potential of a compound i in a mixture is then

μᵢ = μᵢ* + RT ln xᵢ + RT ln γᵢ

with the activity coefficient ln γᵢ obtained from the statistical thermodynamics of interacting surface segments:

ln γᵢ = (1/RT) ∫ pᵢ(σ) [μₛ(σ) – μₛ*(σ)] dσ

where μₛ(σ) is the chemical potential of a surface segment with charge density σ in the mixture, and μₛ*(σ) is that in the pure compound. The segment chemical potentials are solved self‑consistently from the pairwise interaction energies Eₘᵢₙ(σ,σ') = –α (σ+σ')² / (σ²+σ'²) (with α a universal constant). Once γᵢ is known for the solute in the solvent mixture, the solubility x₂ at temperature T follows from the equality of chemical potentials of the solid and dissolved solute:

μ₂^solid(T) = μ₂^liquid(T, x₂) = μ₂* + RT ln x₂ + RT ln γ₂

The solid‑phase chemical potential μ₂^solid is approximated by the sublimation free energy or, more accurately, by the melting free energy ΔH_fus – TΔS_fus corrected for the heat‑capacity difference between solid and liquid.

COSMO‑RS has been shown to predict aqueous solubilities of drug‑like molecules within 0.5–1.0 log units. Examination questions may provide σ‑profiles for a solute and two solvents and ask to estimate the relative solubility trend. The student must recognise that a strong hydrogen‑bond donor peak (σ > 0.01 e/Å²) on the solute interacts favorably with a hydrogen‑bond acceptor peak (σ < –0.01 e/Å²) on the solvent, leading to lower γ and higher solubility. A common trap is to confuse the σ‑profile of the solute with that of the solvent; the integral is over the solute’s pᵢ(σ) weighted by the mixture’s μₛ(σ). Another error is to neglect the solid‑phase free energy term, which can dominate the temperature dependence of solubility.

  • Using the Debye–Hückel extended law for ionic strengths above 0.5 mol kg⁻¹ instead of SIT or Bromley.
  • Misinterpreting the sign of the preferential interaction parameter Γ₂₃ in protein solubility calculations.
  • Applying the Jouyban–Acree equation with mole fractions rather than mass fractions.
  • Assuming the log‑linear mixing rule holds when experimental data show a clear solubility maximum.
  • Ignoring the pressure dependence of azeotropic compositions when sketching residue curve maps.
  • Confusing the solute and solvent σ‑profiles in COSMO‑RS activity‑coefficient integrals.
  • Omitting the solid‑phase free‑energy contribution when equating chemical potentials for solubility prediction.

Thermodynamic Modeling of Gas Solubility in Ionic Liquids Using the e‑NRTL Equation

Ionic liquids (ILs) exhibit negligible vapor pressure, high thermal stability, and tunable polarity, making them attractive solvents for gas capture. The electrolyte Non‑Random Two‑Liquid (e‑NRTL) model extends the classical NRTL framework by adding a long‑range electrostatic term derived from the Debye‑Hückel theory and a short‑range binary interaction term for ion‑pair and ion‑neutral pairs. The excess Gibbs energy is written as Gex = Gex,LR + Gex,SR, where the long‑range contribution depends on the ionic strength I and the dielectric constant of the mixture, while the short‑range part uses the usual NRTL τ and α parameters for each binary pair (cation‑anion, cation‑gas, anion‑gas). For a gas g dissolving in an IL composed of cation c and anion a, the Henry constant Hg at temperature T and pressure P follows from the equality of fugacities: fggas = xg γg Hg0. The activity coefficient γg is obtained from the derivative of Gex with respect to the mole number of the gas. Because the gas is present at infinite dilution, the short‑range τ parameters for gas‑ion pairs dominate the solubility prediction. Experimental Henry constants for CO₂, CH₄, and N₂ in imidazolium‑based ILs are typically correlated with a temperature‑dependent τg‑c = A + B/T + C ln T. The model reproduces the observed increase in CO₂ solubility with increasing alkyl chain length on the cation, reflecting enhanced free‑volume and weaker cation‑anion electrostatic interactions. A key practical point: the e‑NRTL parameters are fitted to a limited set of binary gas‑IL data; extrapolation to multicomponent gas mixtures (e.g., flue gas) requires the assumption of pairwise additivity, which may break down at high pressures where gas‑gas interactions in the liquid phase become non‑negligible.

Critical Solution Behavior in Binary Liquid Mixtures: Scaling Laws and Universality Classes

Near the critical solution temperature (CST) of a binary liquid mixture, thermodynamic properties exhibit power‑law singularities characterized by universal critical exponents that depend only on the dimensionality and symmetry of the order parameter, not on molecular details. For a liquid‑liquid critical point belonging to the Ising universality class (scalar order parameter, three dimensions), the coexistence curve diameter follows ρl – ρc ∝ |T – Tc|β with β ≈ 0.326, the correlation length diverges as ξ ∝ |T – Tc|–ν with ν ≈ 0.630, and the osmotic compressibility χT ∝ |T – Tc|–γ with γ ≈ 1.239. The scaling law γ = β(δ – 1) and the hyperscaling relation 2 – α = d ν (with α ≈ 0.110 for the specific heat exponent) provide internal consistency checks. In practice, the critical composition xc is often not exactly 0.5; asymmetry leads to a non‑zero diameter exponent βd ≈ 0.5 (mean‑field) crossing over to the Ising value very close to Tc. Experimental determination of exponents requires high‑precision turbidity or light‑scattering data within 10–3 K of Tc. A common pitfall is fitting data over too wide a temperature range, which yields effective exponents that drift toward mean‑field values (β = 0.5, γ = 1). The renormalization‑group approach predicts correction‑to‑scaling terms of the form |T – Tc|Δ with Δ ≈ 0.5, which must be included for accurate extrapolation. For polymer‑solvent systems, the critical point shifts to very low polymer volume fractions and the universality class remains Ising, but the Ginzburg criterion shows that the critical region narrows dramatically as the degree of polymerization increases, often rendering the asymptotic scaling experimentally inaccessible.

Worked Example: Estimating the Critical Exponent β from Coexistence Data
A binary mixture of nitroethane and 3‑methylpentane shows the following liquid‑phase compositions (mole fraction of nitroethane) at temperatures approaching the upper critical solution temperature Tc = 298.15 K: (297.90 K, 0.42), (297.95 K, 0.44), (298.00 K, 0.46), (298.05 K, 0.48). Assuming the critical composition xc = 0.50, estimate the exponent β by a log‑log fit of Δx = xc – x versus ΔT = Tc – T.
Compute ΔT and Δx: (0.25 K, 0.08), (0.20 K, 0.06), (0.15 K, 0.04), (0.10 K, 0.02). Take natural logs: ln ΔT = (–1.386, –1.609, –1.897, –2.303); ln Δx = (–2.526, –2.813, –3.219, –3.912). Linear regression of ln Δx on ln ΔT gives slope β ≈ 1.02. The value is far from the Ising β ≈ 0.326 because the data are too far from Tc (ΔT > 0.1 K) and the critical region is not reached. The exercise illustrates the danger of extracting asymptotic exponents from non‑asymptotic data.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for a critical exponent, first check whether the data provided lie within the asymptotic scaling region (typically ΔT/Tc < 10–3). If not, state that the effective exponent will be larger than the universal value and explain the crossover to mean‑field behavior. Cite the Ginzburg criterion to justify the width of the critical region for the given system.

Solid Solution Thermodynamics: Regular Solution Model, Ordering, and Miscibility Gaps

Solid solutions are crystalline phases in which two or more components share a common lattice. The regular solution model, originally developed for liquids, is frequently applied to substitutional solid solutions by replacing the coordination number z with the number of nearest neighbours in the crystal (e.g., 12 for fcc, 8 for bcc). The excess enthalpy of mixing is ΔHmix = Ω xA xB, where Ω = z (εAB – ½(εAA + εBB)) is the interaction parameter. A positive Ω favors phase separation, leading to a miscibility gap with a consolute point at Tc = Ω / (2R). For Ω < 0, ordering occurs: the system lowers its energy by arranging unlike neighbours preferentially, producing superlattice structures such as B2 (CsCl‑type) or L1₀ (CuAu‑type). The Bragg‑Williams approximation gives the long‑range order parameter η = (xAα – xAβ) / (xAα + xAβ) for a two‑sublattice model, with the order‑disorder transition temperature Tord = –Ω / (2R) for a 50‑50 composition. Real systems deviate from the mean‑field prediction because of short‑range order (SRO) above Tord, quantified by the Warren‑Cowley SRO parameter α₁ = 1 – (PAB / xB), where PAB is the conditional probability of finding a B atom next to an A atom. Diffuse scattering experiments measure α₁, which approaches zero only far above Tord. In multicomponent alloys (e.g., high‑entropy alloys), the regular solution model is extended by summing pairwise Ωij terms, but the configurational entropy ΔSconf = –R Σ xi ln xi can stabilize a single‑phase solid solution even when many Ωij are positive, provided the number of components is large enough (the “entropy‑stabilization” criterion). A practical exam trap: confusing the regular solution interaction parameter Ω (units J mol⁻¹) with the dimensionless Flory‑Huggins χ parameter used for polymer solutions; they are related by χ = Ω / (RT) only for athermal polymer blends.

Osmotic Virial Expansion for Polymer Solutions: Second Virial Coefficient and Theta Conditions

The osmotic pressure Π of a polymer solution can be expressed as a virial expansion in the polymer mass concentration c (kg m⁻³): Π / (RT) = c / Mn + A₂ c² + A₃ c³ + …, where Mn is the number‑average molar mass and A₂ is the second osmotic virial coefficient (units m³ kg⁻² mol). A₂ reflects pairwise polymer‑polymer interactions mediated by the solvent; it is related to the excluded volume v by A₂ = v / (2 Mn²). In a good solvent, v > 0 and A₂ > 0; in a poor solvent, v < 0 and A₂ < 0, leading to phase separation. The theta (θ) condition is defined by A₂ = 0, where the polymer chain behaves ideally (Gaussian statistics) because attractive polymer‑segment interactions exactly cancel excluded‑volume repulsion. The theta temperature θ is the temperature at which A₂ vanishes for a given polymer‑solvent pair; it can be determined experimentally by measuring Π at several concentrations and extrapolating Π/c to zero concentration. The Flory‑Huggins theory predicts A₂ = (½ – χ) V1 / Mn², where χ is the polymer‑solvent interaction parameter and V1 is the molar volume of the solvent. At the theta point, χ = ½. Higher virial coefficients (A₃, A₄) become important near the critical point of polymer solutions, where the correlation length diverges and the scaling form Π = c R T f(c ξ³) applies. Light‑scattering measurements provide an independent route to A₂ via the Rayleigh ratio Rθ = K c / (1 / Mw + 2 A₂ c + …), where Mw is the weight‑average molar mass and K is the optical constant. A common mistake is to use mass concentration c in the osmotic virial equation but mole fraction in the Flory‑Huggins expression without converting units, leading to an apparent discrepancy between A₂ and χ.

Thermodynamic Consistency Tests for Activity Coefficient Models: Herington and Van Ness Methods

Experimental vapor‑liquid equilibrium (VLE) data for binary mixtures must satisfy the Gibbs‑Duhem equation at constant temperature and pressure: x₁ d ln γ₁ + x₂ d ln γ₂ = 0. Two widely used numerical consistency tests are the Herington test and the Van Ness test. The Herington test evaluates the integrals ∫₀¹ ln(γ₁/γ₂) dx₁ and ∫₀¹ (ln γ₁ – ln γ₂) dx₁; for thermodynamically consistent data both integrals must be zero within experimental error. In practice, the integrals are approximated by the trapezoidal rule using the measured x₁, y₁, P data and the relation ln γ₁ = ln(y₁ P / x₁ P₁sat). The Van Ness test fits the data to a chosen activity‑coefficient model (e.g., Wilson, NRTL, UNIQUAC) by minimizing the objective function Σ (Pexp – Pcalc)² + Σ (y₁exp – y₁calc)². The root‑mean‑square deviations in pressure (δP) and vapor composition (δy) are then compared with the experimental uncertainties. If δP and δy are significantly larger than the reported uncertainties, the data are deemed inconsistent. A subtle point: the Herington test is model‑free but sensitive to systematic errors in the pure‑component vapor pressures Pisat. The Van Ness test can mask inconsistencies if the model has enough adjustable parameters to fit erroneous data; therefore, the number of parameters should be kept minimal (e.g., two‑parameter Wilson rather than three‑parameter NRTL) when testing consistency. In exam questions, you may be given a table of x₁, y₁, P at constant T and asked to compute the Herington integrals manually for a few points. Remember to use the same reference pressure for both components and to check that the sum of the two integrals is zero (the area test). A frequent trap is forgetting that the integrals are taken over the mole fraction of component 1 from 0 to 1, not over the experimental range only; extrapolation to the pure‑component limits is required and introduces uncertainty.

  • Assuming the e‑NRTL model parameters for a single gas‑IL pair are transferable to mixed‑gas systems without accounting for gas‑gas interactions in the liquid phase.
  • Fitting critical‑exponent data over a temperature range that lies outside the asymptotic scaling region, yielding mean‑field instead of Ising exponents.
  • Confusing the regular solution interaction parameter Ω (J mol⁻¹) with the Flory‑Huggins χ parameter (dimensionless) when analyzing solid solutions versus polymer blends.
  • Using mass concentration in the osmotic virial expansion but mole fraction in the Flory‑Huggins expression without unit conversion, leading to erroneous A₂ values.
  • Applying the Van Ness consistency test with an over‑parameterized activity‑coefficient model, which can hide systematic errors in the VLE data.
  • Neglecting the extrapolation to pure‑component limits when evaluating the Herington integrals, causing the area test to fail even for consistent data.
  • Treating the theta temperature as a fixed property of the polymer alone, ignoring its dependence on solvent molar volume and polymer molecular weight distribution.

Vapor‑Liquid Equilibrium of Associating Fluids: The SAFT Equation of State

The Statistical Associating Fluid Theory (SAFT) provides a molecular‑based equation of state that explicitly accounts for chain formation, dispersion forces, and hydrogen‑bonding association. In the original SAFT formulation, each molecule is represented as a chain of spherical segments of diameter σ that interact via a Lennard‑Jones‑type potential. Association sites (e.g., the two donor and two acceptor sites on water) are added to the segments; the association contribution to the Helmholtz free energy is derived from Wertheim’s first‑order thermodynamic perturbation theory. The total residual Helmholtz energy is written as a sum of hard‑chain, dispersion, and association terms: Ares = Ahc + Adisp + Aassoc. The hard‑chain term uses the Carnahan‑Starling expression for hard‑sphere fluids extended to chains of length m. The dispersion term is usually taken from a perturbation expansion (e.g., the Barker‑Henderson or the more recent Mie‑potential based versions). The association term depends on the association energy εAB and volume κAB for each pair of sites A and B, and on the fraction of non‑bonded sites XA, which are obtained by solving the mass‑action equations XA = 1 / (1 + ΣB ρ XB ΔAB), where ΔAB is the association strength.

SAFT has been extended to numerous variants: SAFT‑VR (variable range), SAFT‑γ Mie (group‑contribution), PC‑SAFT (perturbed‑chain SAFT), and the Cubic‑Plus‑Association (CPA) model that combines a cubic EOS (SRK or PR) with the SAFT association term. PC‑SAFT is widely used in industry because it requires only three pure‑component parameters (segment number m, segment diameter σ, dispersion energy ε/k) plus association parameters for associating compounds. For mixtures, binary interaction parameters kij are introduced in the dispersion term; association cross‑parameters are usually combined with geometric mean rules. The model predicts vapor‑liquid equilibria (VLE), liquid‑liquid equilibria (LLE), and supercritical solubilities with high accuracy for systems containing water, alcohols, acids, and refrigerants.

A key advantage of SAFT over cubic EOS is its ability to represent the strong non‑ideality caused by hydrogen bonding without resorting to large temperature‑dependent binary parameters. However, the model is sensitive to the choice of association scheme (e.g., 2B, 3B, 4C for water) and to the quality of pure‑component parameters, which are typically fitted to vapor pressure and saturated liquid density data. In exam questions you may be asked to write the expression for the association contribution to the chemical potential, μiassoc = ΣA (ln XiA – XiA/2 + 1/2), or to explain why the association term vanishes for non‑associating components. Remember that the total pressure is obtained from P = ρ² (∂(Ares/RT)/∂ρ)T + ρRT, and that the fugacity coefficient follows from ln φi = (∂(n Ares/RT)/∂ni)T,V,nj≠i.

Solubility of Solids in Supercritical Fluids: The Chrastil Equation and Density‑Based Correlations

Supercritical fluid extraction (SFE) relies on the dramatic increase in solute solubility near the critical point of the solvent (most commonly CO₂). The Chrastil equation is an empirical density‑based correlation that relates the equilibrium solubility (expressed as mass fraction w or molar concentration c) to the fluid density ρ and temperature T: ln w = k ln ρ + a/T + b, where k, a, and b are solute‑specific constants. The parameter k reflects the number of solvent molecules in the solvation shell (the “association number”), a is related to the enthalpy of solvation, and b accounts for entropic contributions. Because the density of a supercritical fluid is a strong function of pressure and temperature, the Chrastil equation effectively captures the crossover from gas‑like to liquid‑like solvating power.

More rigorous thermodynamic models express the solid‑fluid equilibrium through equality of fugacities: fsolid = ffluid. The solid fugacity is approximated by the sublimation pressure Psub corrected by the Poynting factor exp[ Vs(P – Psub)/RT ], where Vs is the molar volume of the solid. The fluid‑phase fugacity is y φ P, where y is the mole fraction solubility and φ is the fugacity coefficient obtained from an EOS (e.g., Peng‑Robinson with Wong‑Sandler mixing rules or PC‑SAFT). The Chrastil equation can be derived by assuming that the fugacity coefficient varies linearly with density and that the Poynting factor is negligible at moderate pressures. In practice, the three Chrastil parameters are obtained by linear regression of experimental solubility data plotted as ln w versus ln ρ and 1/T. The model works well for non‑polar and moderately polar solutes (naphthalene, anthracene, caffeine) but fails for strongly associating solutes where specific solute‑solvent interactions (hydrogen bonding, Lewis acid‑base) dominate.

Extensions of the Chrastil approach include the Méndez‑Santiago–Teja (MST) correlation, which adds a term proportional to the solvent’s reduced temperature, and the Bartle‑Clifford‑Jafar‑Mansoori (BCJM) model that incorporates the solute’s critical properties. For exam purposes, you should be able to (i) linearize the Chrastil equation for parameter estimation, (ii) calculate the solubility at a given P,T by first obtaining ρ from an EOS or from tabulated data, and (iii) discuss the physical meaning of the association number k and its typical range (2–10 for aromatic solids in CO₂). A common trap is to use the ideal‑gas density ρ = PM/RT instead of the real fluid density, which leads to large errors above 100 bar.

Worked Example: Estimating Solubility of Naphthalene in Supercritical CO₂ Using the Chrastil Equation
Experimental Chrastil parameters for naphthalene in CO₂ are k = 5.12, a = –1850 K, b = –12.3 (w in kg/kg, ρ in kg/m³). At T = 313 K and P = 150 bar, the density of CO₂ is 780 kg/m³. Compute the mass‑fraction solubility w.
Step 1: Write the Chrastil equation: ln w = k ln ρ + a/T + b.
Step 2: Insert values: ln w = 5.12 × ln(780) + (–1850)/313 + (–12.3).
ln(780) ≈ 6.659. 5.12 × 6.659 ≈ 34.09.
a/T = –1850/313 ≈ –5.91.
Sum: 34.09 – 5.91 – 12.3 = 15.88.
Step 3: w = exp(15.88) ≈ 7.9 × 10⁶ kg/kg. This absurdly large number signals that the parameters are given for w in g/kg or that the density unit is g/cm³. Re‑checking the original source shows ρ in g/cm³ (0.78 g/cm³) and w in g/kg. Using ρ = 0.78: ln(0.78) = –0.248; 5.12 × (–0.248) = –1.27. Then ln w = –1.27 – 5.91 – 12.3 = –19.48. w = exp(–19.48) ≈ 3.5 × 10⁻⁹ kg/kg = 3.5 µg/kg, which is consistent with literature values (~3–5 µg/kg). The key lesson: always verify the units of ρ and w before applying the correlation.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a supercritical solubility problem appears, first identify whether the question expects the empirical Chrastil route or the rigorous fugacity‑equality route. If Chrastil parameters are given, plug them in directly but double‑check the units of density and solubility (often g/cm³ and g/kg). If no parameters are given, set up the fugacity equality: y φ P = Psub exp[ Vs(P – Psub)/RT ]. Use a cubic EOS (Peng‑Robinson) to obtain φ and ρ. Remember that the Poynting correction is usually < 5 % for pressures up to 200 bar and can be omitted if the problem states “moderate pressure”. Write the final expression for y and indicate the required iterative solution for φ(y).

Phase Equilibria in Polymer Blends: Flory‑Huggins Theory for Binary Polymer Mixtures

The Flory‑Huggins lattice model, originally developed for polymer solutions, extends naturally to binary polymer blends. In a blend of polymer A (degree of polymerization NA) and polymer B (NB), the combinatorial entropy of mixing per lattice site is ΔSmix/k = –[ φA ln φA/NA + φB ln φB/NB ], where φi are volume fractions. The enthalpy of mixing is ΔHmix = χ φA φB RT per lattice site, with χ the segment‑segment interaction parameter (often assumed temperature dependent: χ = A + B/T). The Gibbs free energy of mixing per site is ΔGmix/RT = φA ln φA/NA + φB ln φB/NB + χ φA φB. Phase separation occurs when the second derivative of ΔGmix with respect to composition becomes negative (spinodal) or when the common‑tangent construction yields two coexisting phases (binodal). The critical point satisfies ∂²ΔG/∂φ² = 0 and ∂³ΔG/∂φ³ = 0, giving χc = ½ (1/√NA + 1/√NB)² and φAc = √NB / (√NA + √NB). For symmetric blends (NA = NB = N), χc = 2/N and φc = 0.5.

Because N is typically 10³–10⁵, χc is very small (10⁻³–10⁻⁵), meaning that even weakly unfavorable interactions drive phase separation. This explains why most polymer pairs are immiscible. Miscibility can be achieved by introducing specific interactions (hydrogen bonding, dipole‑dipole) that make χ negative or by using copolymers that reduce the effective χ. The Flory‑Huggins model also predicts the interfacial tension γ between coexisting phases: γ ≈ (kT/a²) √(χ/6) (φA – φB)², where a is the segment length. In exam questions you may be asked to (i) derive the spinodal condition χs = ½ (1/(NA φA) + 1/(NB φB)), (ii) calculate the critical χ for given N values, or (iii) discuss how the addition of a low‑molecular‑weight solvent (plasticizer) shifts the binodal by effectively reducing N and altering χ. A frequent mistake is to use the polymer‑solution expression χc = 0.5 (1 + 1/√N)² for blends; the correct blend expression contains both NA and NB.

Thermodynamics of Electrolyte Solutions: The Mean Spherical Approximation and Ion Association Models

The Mean Spherical Approximation (MSA) is a closure of the Ornstein‑Zernike integral equation that yields analytical expressions for the correlation functions of charged hard spheres. For a primitive model electrolyte (ions as charged hard spheres in a dielectric continuum), the MSA gives the excess internal energy Uex = –(1/2) Σi ρi zi² e² / (ε ε₀) × (Γ / (1 + Γ ai)), where Γ is the screening parameter satisfying Γ² = (4π e² / ε ε₀ kT) Σ ρi zi² / (1 + Γ ai)². The activity coefficient follows from ln γi = –zi² e² Γ / (2 ε ε₀ kT (1 + Γ ai)). In the limit of point ions (a → 0), Γ reduces to the Debye‑Hückel κ and the MSA reproduces the limiting law. For finite ion sizes, the MSA remains accurate up to concentrations of ~3 mol kg⁻¹ for 1:1 electrolytes.

Ion association (formation of ion pairs, triplets) is incorporated by adding a chemical equilibrium between free ions and associated species. The Bjerrum association model defines a critical distance q = z+z– e² / (8π ε ε₀ kT); ions closer than q are considered paired. The association constant KA = 4π NA q³ / 3 × exp( z+z– e² / (ε ε₀ kT q) ). In the MSA‑association framework (e.g., the MSA‑Bjerrum or the extended MSA of Blum and Høye), the free‑ion concentrations are obtained by solving mass‑balance equations together with the MSA screening parameter. This approach yields activity and osmotic coefficients that match experimental data for 2:2 and 3:3 electrolytes where simple Debye‑Hückel fails. For exam purposes, you should be able to (i) write the MSA expression for ln γ± of a symmetric electrolyte, (ii) explain the physical meaning of the screening parameter Γ and how it differs from the Debye length κ⁻¹, and (iii) describe how ion pairing reduces the effective ionic strength and thus increases the mean activity coefficient at high concentration. A common trap is to apply the Debye‑Hückel limiting law beyond 0.01 mol kg⁻¹ for multivalent electrolytes; the MSA or Pitzer model must be used instead.

pH‑Dependent Solubility of Weak Electrolytes: Henderson‑Hasselbalch, Intrinsic Dissolution Rate, and the pH‑Solubility Profile

The equilibrium solubility of a weak acid (HA) or weak base (B) varies dramatically with pH because only the neutral form partitions into the solid phase. For a monoprotic acid with intrinsic solubility S₀ (mol L⁻¹ of the neutral species) and acid dissociation constant Ka, the total dissolved concentration Stot at a given pH is Stot = S₀ (1 + Ka / [H⁺]) = S₀ (1 + 10pH – pKa). For a weak base with Kb, Stot = S₀ (1 + [H⁺] / Ka) = S₀ (1 + 10pKa – pH). Plotting log Stot versus pH yields the characteristic pH‑solubility profile: a horizontal plateau at low pH for acids (where the neutral form dominates) and a slope of +1 above pKa; the reverse for bases. The intersection of the two linear asymptotes occurs at pH = pKa, where Stot = 2 S₀.

In pharmaceutical development, the intrinsic dissolution rate (IDR) is measured under sink conditions at constant pH using a rotating disk apparatus. The Noyes‑Whitney equation gives IDR = (D A / h) (Stot – Cb), where D is the diffusion coefficient, A the surface area, h the diffusion layer thickness, and Cb the bulk concentration (≈0 under sink). Because Stot is pH dependent, the IDR inherits the same pH profile. For salts of weak acids (e.g., sodium ibuprofen), the solid phase is the ionized form, and the solubility product Ksp = [Na⁺][A⁻] governs the equilibrium; the pH‑solubility relationship then involves the common‑ion effect and the acid‑base equilibrium of the counter‑ion.

Exam questions often ask you to (i) derive the pH‑solubility equation from mass‑balance and charge‑balance, (ii) calculate the pH at which a given dose of drug will precipitate (dose/solubility ratio), or (iii) explain why the Henderson‑Hasselbalch equation (pH = pKa + log([A⁻]/[HA])) is valid only when activity coefficients are unity and the neutral species concentration equals S₀. A frequent trap is to ignore the contribution of the counter‑ion in salt solubility, leading to an over‑prediction of solubility at low pH. Another is to treat the pH‑solubility profile as linear over the entire range; the curvature near pKa is significant and must be accounted for in precise calculations.

  • Using the Debye‑Hückel limiting law for multivalent electrolytes above 0.01 mol kg⁻¹, where ion‑size and association effects dominate.
  • Applying the polymer‑solution critical χ expression to binary polymer blends instead of the blend‑specific formula χc = ½ (1/√NA + 1/√NB)².
  • Neglecting unit consistency in the Chrastil equation (density in g cm⁻³ vs kg m⁻³, solubility in g kg⁻¹ vs kg kg⁻¹) which produces orders‑of‑magnitude errors.
  • Assuming the Henderson‑Hasselbalch equation holds at high ionic strength without activity‑coefficient corrections.
  • Forgetting that the MSA screening parameter Γ satisfies a non‑linear equation and cannot be replaced by the Debye length κ⁻¹ for concentrated solutions.
  • Overlooking the common‑ion effect when calculating the solubility of a weak‑acid salt in a buffer of fixed pH.
  • Treating the Flory‑Huggins χ parameter as temperature‑independent when the problem explicitly gives χ = A + B/T.

Thermodynamics of Aqueous Two‑Phase Systems for Biomolecule Partitioning

Aqueous two‑phase systems (ATPS) form when two mutually incompatible hydrophilic polymers, or a polymer and a salt, are mixed above critical concentrations. The most studied pair is polyethylene glycol (PEG) and dextran, but PEG–phosphate, PEG–sulfate, and alcohol–salt systems are also common. Each phase is water‑rich but differs in polymer/salt composition, creating distinct micro‑environments that partition proteins, nucleic acids, and cell organelles according to surface hydrophobicity, charge, and size.

Phase Diagram and Binodal Curve

The binodal curve separates the one‑phase region from the two‑phase region. It is obtained experimentally by titration and fitted to the empirical Merchuk equation: w₁ = a exp(b w₂0.5 + c w₂), where w₁ and w₂ are mass fractions of the two phase‑forming components. The critical point lies at the apex of the binodal; tie‑lines connect compositions of coexisting phases and are determined by the lever rule. The slope of a tie‑line reflects the relative affinity of a solute for the top (polymer‑rich) versus bottom (salt‑rich) phase.

Partition Coefficient and Thermodynamic Model

The partition coefficient K = C_top / C_bottom is related to the standard Gibbs energy of transfer ΔG°_tr = –RT ln K. In the Flory–Huggins framework extended to ATPS, the chemical potential of a solute i in phase α is μ_i^α = μ_i0 + RT ln φ_i^α + RT ∑_j χ_ij φ_j^α, where φ are volume fractions and χ_ij are interaction parameters. For proteins, the dominant contributions are excluded‑volume (size) and electrostatic (charge) terms. The excluded‑volume term scales with the ratio of protein radius to polymer coil radius; the electrostatic term follows a Debye–Hückel‑type dependence on ionic strength and protein net charge.

Effect of pH, Ionic Strength, and Temperature

Changing pH alters protein charge and thus the electrostatic contribution, often shifting K by orders of magnitude. Adding neutral salts (e.g., NaCl) screens charges and reduces the electrostatic term, while also moving the binodal. Temperature influences both the binodal position (usually shifting it to lower polymer concentrations at higher T) and the χ parameters. In practice, a systematic optimization varies pH, tie‑line length (TLL), and temperature to achieve a target K for a given biomolecule.

Applications and Limitations

ATPS are used for large‑scale purification of antibodies, enzymes, and plasmid DNA because they are biocompatible, scalable, and allow continuous operation. Limitations include relatively low resolution for proteins with similar surface properties, and the cost of high‑purity polymers. Recent advances incorporate affinity ligands (e.g., PEG‑biotin) into one phase to impart selectivity, and the use of thermoseparating polymers (e.g., EO‑PO copolymers) that enable phase separation by temperature swing.

Counterion Condensation Theory and Osmotic Pressure of Polyelectrolyte Solutions

Polyelectrolytes such as DNA, poly(acrylic acid), and sulfonated polystyrene carry a high linear charge density. In aqueous solution, a fraction of the counterions condense onto the polymer backbone, reducing the effective charge and dramatically altering colligative properties. Manning’s counterion condensation theory provides a quantitative criterion: condensation occurs when the dimensionless charge density parameter ξ = e²/(ε kT b) exceeds unity, where b is the average distance between charges along the chain.

Manning Parameter and Condensed Fraction

For a monovalent counterion, the fraction of free counterions is f = 1/ξ when ξ > 1; the remaining (1 – 1/ξ) are condensed and move with the polymer. For multivalent counterions, the theory predicts a stronger condensation, and the effective valence of the free ions is reduced. The condensed ions do not contribute to the osmotic pressure because they are thermodynamically bound to the polyion.

Osmotic Pressure and the van’t Hoff Factor

The osmotic pressure Π of a polyelectrolyte solution can be expressed as Π = i c RT, where c is the molar concentration of polymer repeat units and i is the effective van’t Hoff factor. In the Manning regime, i ≈ 1/ξ + ν, where ν accounts for co‑ions and any added salt. At low polymer concentrations, the Donnan equilibrium with added salt dominates; at high concentrations, the condensed counterions lower i toward unity, making the solution behave almost like a neutral polymer solution.

Experimental Verification

Osmometry, conductivity, and electrophoresis measurements confirm the theory. For example, the osmotic coefficient φ = Π/(c RT) of sodium poly(styrene sulfonate) in 0.1 M NaCl drops from ~0.9 at low concentration to ~0.2 at 0.5 M repeat units, consistent with ξ ≈ 4.5 for this polymer. The theory also predicts the limiting conductivity of polyelectrolyte solutions, which scales with the free counterion fraction.

Limitations and Extensions

Manning’s model assumes an infinitely long, rigid rod and neglects ion‑size effects, dielectric saturation, and chain flexibility. Extensions such as the Poisson–Boltzmann cell model and Monte Carlo simulations incorporate finite chain length, excluded volume, and specific ion binding. These refinements are essential for quantitative prediction of osmotic pressure in physiological media where mixed salts and crowding agents are present.

Worked Example: Partition Coefficient in PEG–Dextran ATPS
A protein with molecular weight 45 kDa is partitioned in a PEG 8000 (12 wt %) – dextran T500 (8 wt %) system at 25 °C. The tie‑line length is 15 wt % and the protein’s net charge at the working pH is –5. The Flory–Huggins interaction parameters are χ_protein‑PEG = 0.35, χ_protein‑dextran = 0.55, and the protein volume fraction in the top phase is 2 × 10⁻⁴. Estimate the partition coefficient K assuming ideal mixing and negligible electrostatic contribution.
The chemical potential equality μ_top = μ_bottom gives ln K = ln(φ_top/φ_bottom) + ∑_j χ_protein‑j (φ_j^top – φ_j^bottom). For a dilute protein, φ_bottom ≈ φ_top/K. The polymer volume fractions on the tie‑line are φ_PEG^top ≈ 0.12, φ_dextran^top ≈ 0.02; φ_PEG^bottom ≈ 0.02, φ_dextran^bottom ≈ 0.10. Substituting: ln K ≈ ln K + χ_protein‑PEG(0.12–0.02) + χ_protein‑dextran(0.02–0.10). Rearranging: 0 = 0.35×0.10 + 0.55×(–0.08) = 0.035 – 0.044 = –0.009. The small negative value indicates K ≈ 1 (slight preference for the bottom phase). Including the electrostatic term (negative charge favors the more hydrated dextran‑rich phase) would increase K_bottom, consistent with experimental observation that acidic proteins partition to the dextran phase.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the partition coefficient in an ATPS, first identify the tie‑line composition from the binodal data. Write the chemical‑potential balance using the Flory–Huggins expression; the protein volume fractions cancel if you assume dilute limit. Remember that the electrostatic contribution is proportional to the square of the protein net charge and inversely proportional to the Debye length. If the problem gives χ parameters, plug them directly; if not, use the empirical correlation log K = a + b TLL + c pH. Always check whether the protein is near its isoelectric point — at pI the electrostatic term vanishes and partitioning is governed solely by hydrophobicity and size.

The van der Waals–Platteeuw Model for Gas Hydrate Equilibria

Gas hydrates are inclusion compounds where water molecules form hydrogen‑bonded cages that trap guest molecules such as methane, carbon dioxide, or hydrogen sulfide. The van der Waals–Platteeuw (vdWP) model provides a statistical‑thermodynamic description of hydrate stability by treating the water lattice as a fixed framework and the guest occupancy as a Langmuir‑type adsorption process.

Chemical Potential of Water in the Hydrate Phase

The difference in chemical potential of water between the empty hydrate lattice (β) and the filled hydrate (H) is Δμ_w = μ_w^β – μ_w^H = RT ∑_m ν_m ln(1 – ∑_i θi,m), where ν_m is the number of cages of type m per water molecule, and θi,m is the fractional occupancy of guest i in cage m. The occupancy follows the Langmuir isotherm θi,m = Ci,m f_i / (1 + ∑_j Cj,m f_j), with Ci,m the Langmuir constant and f_i the fugacity of guest i in the vapor phase.

Langmuir Constants from Intermolecular Potentials

The constants Ci,m are obtained by integrating the guest–water interaction potential over the cage volume: Ci,m = (4π/kT) ∫0R_m exp[–w(r)/kT] r² dr, where w(r) is the spherically averaged Kihara or Lennard‑Jones potential. For spherical guests, the Kihara potential with a hard‑core radius σ and well depth ε reproduces experimental C values within 10 %. The model predicts that larger cages (e.g., 5¹²6⁴ in structure I) have higher C for larger guests, explaining the preferential occupancy of methane in small cages and ethane in large cages.

Phase Equilibrium Condition

At equilibrium, the chemical potential of water in the hydrate equals that in the coexisting aqueous phase (liquid water or ice). For the liquid water phase, μ_w^L = μ_w0 + RT ln a_w, where a_w is the water activity. The equilibrium condition becomes RT ln a_w = –Δμ_w. This equation is solved iteratively for the dissociation pressure at a given temperature, or for the temperature at a given pressure. The model accurately predicts the three‑phase (hydrate–liquid water–vapor) equilibrium curve for pure methane hydrate within 2 % of experimental data up to 100 MPa.

Inhibitor Effects and Electrolyte Solutions

Thermodynamic inhibitors (methanol, ethylene glycol, salts) lower water activity a_w, shifting the equilibrium to higher pressures or lower temperatures. In the vdWP framework, the inhibitor effect enters solely through a_w, which can be calculated from an activity‑coefficient model such as Pitzer or e‑NRTL for electrolyte solutions. Kinetic inhibitors and anti‑agglomerants, by contrast, do not alter the thermodynamic equilibrium and are not captured by the vdWP model.

Limitations and Modern Extensions

The original vdWP model assumes a rigid lattice, no guest‑guest interactions, and ideal mixing of guests in the cages. Extensions include the inclusion of lattice distortion (the “cell potential” method), guest‑guest interactions via a virial expansion in the cage, and the use of molecular dynamics to compute Ci,m directly. These improvements are essential for mixed‑guest hydrates (e.g., natural gas with CO₂ and H₂S) and for predicting hydrate formation in porous media where capillary effects modify the water activity.

Reactive Phase Equilibria: Thermodynamics of Esterification in Liquid‑Liquid Systems

Esterification of a carboxylic acid with an alcohol is a reversible reaction that often occurs in a two‑phase liquid‑liquid system, especially when water is removed to drive conversion. The equilibrium composition is governed by simultaneous chemical‑reaction equilibrium and phase‑distribution equilibrium. The reaction quotient Q = (a_ester a_water)/(a_acid a_alcohol) must equal the equilibrium constant K_r at the system temperature.

Coupled Equilibrium Equations

For a system with an organic phase (rich in ester, acid, alcohol) and an aqueous phase (rich in water, dissolved acid/alcohol), the mass balances for each component i are: z_i = x_i^org φ_org + x_i^aq φ_aq, where z_i is the overall mole fraction, x_i^α the mole fraction in phase α, and φ_α the phase mole fraction. The phase‑equilibrium condition for each component is γ_i^org x_i^org P_i^sat = γ_i^aq x_i^aq P_i^sat (assuming equal total pressure). The reaction equilibrium is imposed in each phase where the reaction occurs; typically the organic phase is the reactive zone, so K_r = (γ_ester^org x_ester^org γ_water^org x_water^org)/(γ_acid^org x_acid^org γ_alcohol^org x_alcohol^org).

Activity‑Coefficient Models

Because the mixture is highly non‑ideal, activity coefficients are calculated with models such as UNIFAC, NRTL, or UNIQUAC. For esterification systems, the UNIFAC‑Dortmund version with temperature‑dependent parameters gives reliable predictions of both phase split and reaction equilibrium. The water activity in the organic phase is critical: even trace water (x_water^org ≈ 0.01) strongly suppresses conversion because it appears in the numerator of K_r.

Effect of Water Removal

In industrial practice, water is continuously removed by azeotropic distillation (e.g., using toluene) or by pervaporation. Thermodynamically, this lowers the overall water mole fraction z_water, shifting the reaction equilibrium toward products. The phase diagram shows that as water is removed, the two‑phase region shrinks and eventually disappears, yielding a single homogeneous organic phase where the reaction proceeds to near‑completion.

Design Implications

Reactive distillation columns combine reaction and separation in one unit. The design requires solving the coupled equilibrium equations stage‑by‑stage, often using the “reaction‑equilibrium” flash calculation. A key design variable is the reflux ratio, which controls the water concentration in the reactive zone. Sensitivity analysis shows that a 10 % increase in reflux can raise conversion from 85 % to >95 % for typical ethyl acetate synthesis.

Solubility of Solids in Subcritical Water: Temperature and Pressure Effects

Subcritical water (also called hot compressed water) exists at temperatures between 100 °C and 374 °C and pressures sufficient to maintain the liquid phase (typically 10–25 MPa). In this region, the dielectric constant of water drops from ~80 at 25 °C to ~20 at 300 °C, dramatically increasing the solubility of non‑polar and weakly polar organic solids while decreasing the solubility of ionic salts.

Thermodynamic Framework

The solubility of a solid solute is governed by the equality of fugacities: f_solid = f_solute^liq. For the solid, f_solid = P_sub exp[∫P_sub^P V_solid/RT dP] ≈ P_sub exp[V_solid(P – P_sub)/RT], where P_sub is the sublimation pressure. For the solute in water, f_solute^liq = x_solute γ_solute φ_solute^sat P_sat exp[∫P_sat^P V_solute^∞/RT dP]. The Poynting correction (the exponential term) is often negligible at moderate pressures but becomes significant above 20 MPa.

Dielectric Constant and Solvation

The temperature dependence of the dielectric constant ε(T,P) controls the solvation free energy of polar solutes. The Born equation gives ΔG_solv ∝ –(1 – 1/ε) z²/r. As ε decreases, the solvation of ions becomes less favorable, reducing salt solubility. Conversely, the solubility of neutral organics correlates with the Hildebrand solubility parameter δ = √(ΔU_vap/V_m), which for water decreases with temperature, approaching the δ values of many organic compounds and thus enhancing miscibility.

Empirical Correlations

For many organic solids, the solubility in subcritical water follows the Chrastil‑type density correlation: ln x = k ln ρ + a/T + b, where ρ is the water density (kg m⁻³) at the system T and P. The parameters k, a, b are fitted to experimental data. This correlation captures the combined effect of temperature and pressure through density. For ionic solids, the solubility product K_sp must be corrected for activity coefficients using the Pitzer model at the high ionic strengths encountered near the critical point.

Applications and Practical Limits

Subcritical water extraction (SWE) is used for essential oils, polyphenols, and environmental remediation of PAH‑contaminated soils. The main operational limit is the onset of thermal degradation of the target analyte above ~300 °C. Pressure is maintained by a back‑pressure regulator; typical extraction times are 5–30 min. The method is considered green because it avoids organic solvents, but the energy cost of heating and pressurizing water must be accounted for in life‑cycle assessments.

  • Assuming the binodal curve of an ATPS is symmetric; in reality the critical point is often skewed toward the polymer‑rich side, affecting tie‑line slopes.
  • Neglecting the condensed counterion fraction when calculating the osmotic pressure of a polyelectrolyte; this leads to an over‑estimate of the van’t Hoff factor by up to an order of magnitude.
  • Using the ideal‑gas fugacity for guest molecules in the vdWP model at pressures above 10 MPa; real‑gas fugacity coefficients from a cubic EOS are required.
  • Treating the reaction equilibrium constant K_r as pressure‑independent in reactive liquid‑liquid equilibrium; the standard‑state volume change ΔV° can shift K_r appreciably at >20 MPa.
  • Applying the Chrastil correlation with water density in g cm⁻³ while the fitted parameters expect kg m⁻³, producing solubility errors of 10³‑fold.
  • Ignoring the Poynting correction for solid solutes at pressures >15 MPa; the exponential term can change the fugacity by 20‑30 %.
  • Assuming water activity in the organic phase of an esterification system is negligible; even 0.5 mol % water can reduce conversion by 15‑20 % at typical K_r values.

These advanced topics illustrate how thermodynamic models must be adapted to the specific molecular interactions and phase behavior encountered in modern separation and reaction engineering.

Thermodynamics of Deep Eutectic Solvents: Formation, Phase Behavior, and Solubility Applications

Deep eutectic solvents (DESs) are formed by mixing a hydrogen‑bond acceptor (HBA), typically a quaternary ammonium salt such as choline chloride, with a hydrogen‑bond donor (HBD) like urea, glycerol, or ethylene glycol. The eutectic point occurs at a molar ratio where the melting temperature of the mixture is dramatically lower than that of either pure component, often below 100 °C. The thermodynamic driving force is the large negative excess enthalpy arising from extensive hydrogen‑bond networks that disrupt the crystal lattice of the HBA. The Gibbs free energy of mixing ΔG_mix = ΔH_mix – TΔS_mix becomes negative over a wide composition range because ΔH_mix is strongly exothermic (–10 to –30 kJ mol⁻¹) while the entropy term remains modest.

Phase behavior of DESs is commonly described by the Schröder–van Laar equation for each component i: ln(x_i γ_i) = ΔH_fus,i/R (1/T_fus,i – 1/T). Here x_i is the mole fraction, γ_i the activity coefficient, ΔH_fus,i the enthalpy of fusion, and T_fus,i the melting temperature of the pure component. Because DESs are highly non‑ideal, γ_i deviates strongly from unity; models such as NRTL or UNIQUAC with temperature‑dependent binary interaction parameters are fitted to experimental liquidus data. The eutectic composition corresponds to the intersection of the two liquidus curves, and the eutectic temperature is the lowest temperature at which a single liquid phase exists.

Solubility of solids in DESs is governed by the same equality of fugacities used for conventional solvents: f_solid = x_solute γ_solute φ_solute^sat P_sat exp[∫V_solute^∞/RT dP]. The activity coefficient γ_solute captures specific interactions (hydrogen bonding, π‑π stacking, van der Waals) between solute and the DES components. Empirical correlations such as the modified Apelblat equation ln x = A + B/T + C ln T + D x_HBD are widely used to correlate solubility as a function of temperature and DES composition. For many pharmaceuticals, solubilities in DESs exceed those in water by one to three orders of magnitude, a consequence of the tunable polarity and hydrogen‑bond basicity/acidity of the solvent.

Industrial relevance stems from the low volatility, non‑flammability, and biodegradability of many DESs. They are employed in metal electrodeposition, biomass fractionation, and as reaction media for biocatalysis. However, high viscosity (often 50–500 mPa·s at 25 °C) limits mass transfer; addition of a small amount of water (5–10 wt %) can reduce viscosity by an order of magnitude while preserving the eutectic character. Thermodynamic models must therefore incorporate water as a third component, using ternary NRTL or UNIQUAC parameters to predict phase diagrams and solute activities accurately.

Phase Equilibria in Quaternary Liquid‑Liquid Systems: Tie‑Line Construction and Plait Points

Quaternary liquid‑liquid equilibrium (LLE) involves four components distributing between two immiscible liquid phases at constant temperature and pressure. The Gibbs phase rule gives F = C – P + 2 = 4 – 2 + 2 = 4 degrees of freedom; fixing T and P leaves two independent composition variables per phase. Experimental data are usually presented as tie‑lines connecting the equilibrium compositions of the conjugate phases on a tetrahedral (or projected triangular) diagram. Each tie‑line satisfies equality of component chemical potentials: μ_i^α = μ_i^β for i = 1…4, which translates to γ_i^α x_i^α = γ_i^β x_i^β when the same standard state is used.

Construction of tie‑lines from experimental data follows the Bachman or Othmer–Tobias correlations. For a quaternary system A‑B‑C‑D, the distribution coefficient K_i = x_i^α / x_i^β is plotted against the solvent‑free composition of the other phase. Linear relationships indicate thermodynamic consistency. The plait point (critical solution point) is the composition where the two phases become identical; it lies on the binodal curve where the tie‑line length shrinks to zero. At the plait point, the determinant of the Hessian matrix of the Gibbs energy with respect to composition vanishes, and the spinodal curve meets the binodal.

Activity‑coefficient models for quaternary LLE include NRTL, UNIQUAC, and UNIFAC. NRTL requires binary interaction parameters τ_ij and non‑randomness factors α_ij for each of the six binary pairs. These parameters are usually regressed from binary and ternary LLE data, then used to predict quaternary behavior. UNIFAC offers a predictive alternative by decomposing molecules into functional groups; however, group‑interaction parameters for ionic or highly associating components may be lacking. The reliability of predictions is assessed by the root‑mean‑square deviation (RMSD) of calculated versus experimental tie‑line endpoints; values below 0.02 mole fraction are considered excellent.

Applications include solvent extraction of multicomponent mixtures (e.g., separation of aromatics from aliphatics using sulfolane‑water‑alkane‑alcohol systems), design of liquid‑liquid extraction columns, and formulation of multiphase reaction media. In extraction column design, the tie‑line data feed the McCabe–Thiele or Kremser stage‑wise calculations. The plait point determines the maximum achievable separation; operating too close to it leads to excessive stage requirements and poor phase disengagement. Accurate quaternary LLE modeling therefore directly impacts capital and operating costs.

Worked Example: Tie‑Line Consistency Check
A quaternary system water (1) – ethanol (2) – toluene (3) – n‑hexane (4) at 298 K yields the following equilibrium compositions (mole fractions) for the two liquid phases: Phase α: x₁=0.45, x₂=0.30, x₃=0.15, x₄=0.10; Phase β: x₁=0.10, x₂=0.05, x₃=0.40, x₄=0.45. Verify the Othmer–Tobias correlation for component 1 using component 2 as reference.
The Othmer–Tobias plot uses (1–x₁^α)/x₁^α versus (1–x₂^α)/x₂^α for the α‑phase and similarly for β. For phase α: (1–0.45)/0.45 = 1.222; (1–0.30)/0.30 = 2.333. For phase β: (1–0.10)/0.10 = 9.0; (1–0.05)/0.05 = 19.0. Plotting the two points (2.333, 1.222) and (19.0, 9.0) gives a straight line through the origin with slope ≈0.47, confirming consistency. The linear relationship indicates that the experimental tie‑line satisfies the Othmer–Tobias correlation, supporting thermodynamic validity of the data.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the number of independent variables in a quaternary LLE at fixed T and P, remember the phase rule: F = C – P + 2. With C=4, P=2, and T,P fixed, the remaining degrees of freedom are 2. For tie‑line construction, always check that the sum of mole fractions in each phase equals unity; a common slip is to omit the fourth component. In consistency tests, the Othmer–Tobias and Bachman plots should be linear; if they are not, the data likely contain experimental error or the system exhibits a plait point within the measured range.

Solubility of Gases in Molten Salts: Thermodynamic Models and Industrial Relevance

Molten salts (ionic liquids with melting points below 300 °C) serve as high‑temperature solvents for gas absorption in processes such as nuclear fuel reprocessing, solar‑thermal energy storage, and syngas purification. The solubility of a gas G in a molten salt is expressed by Henry’s law in the form f_G = x_G γ_G^∞ H_G, where f_G is the fugacity of the gas, x_G its mole fraction in the melt, γ_G^∞ the infinite‑dilution activity coefficient, and H_G the Henry’s constant (pressure units). Because molten salts are highly structured ionic media, γ_G^∞ deviates strongly from unity and depends on the cation/anion combination, temperature, and pressure.

Thermodynamic models for γ_G^∞ include the regular solution theory adapted for ionic solvents, the scaled‑particle theory (SPT), and the more rigorous statistical‑mechanical approaches such as the Mean Spherical Approximation (MSA) combined with a Born solvation term. In the regular solution framework, ln γ_G^∞ = V_G (δ_salt – δ_G)²/RT, where V_G is the molar volume of the gas, δ_salt the solubility parameter of the molten salt (estimated from cohesive energy density), and δ_G that of the gas. SPT accounts for cavity formation in a hard‑sphere fluid: ln γ_G^∞ = –ln(1–η) + (3η/(1–η))(r_G/r_s) + (3η²/(1–η)²)(r_G/r_s)², where η is the packing fraction of the molten salt, r_G and r_s are the radii of gas and salt ions.

Experimental Henry’s constants for CO₂, H₂, CH₄, and H₂S in common molten salts (e.g., LiCl‑KCl eutectic, NaNO₃‑KNO₃, FLiBe) show that CO₂ solubility is typically 10–100 times higher than that of H₂ at 600 °C, reflecting stronger quadrupole–ion interactions. The temperature dependence follows van’t Hoff behavior: d(ln H_G)/d(1/T) = –ΔH_sol/R, where ΔH_sol is the enthalpy of solution (often exothermic for CO₂, endothermic for H₂). Pressure effects are incorporated via the Poynting correction: f_G = φ_G P exp[∫V_G^∞/RT dP]; at pressures above 10 MPa the exponential term can increase fugacity by 20–30 %.

Industrial design of gas‑molten salt contactors (packed columns, bubble columns, or membrane contactors) requires accurate prediction of loading capacity (mol gas kg⁻¹ salt) and mass‑transfer coefficients. The loading capacity is directly proportional to x_G at the operating partial pressure. For CO₂ capture in a LiCl‑KCl melt at 600 °C and 1 MPa CO₂, typical loadings are 0.02–0.05 mol kg⁻¹. Corrosion of structural alloys by molten salts imposes material constraints; thermodynamic models also predict the stability of protective oxide layers via the Ellingham diagram, linking gas solubility to materials selection.

The CPA Equation of State for Associating Fluids: Parameter Estimation and VLE Predictions

The Cubic‑Plus‑Association (CPA) equation of state combines a Soave‑Redlich‑Kwong (SRK) cubic term with a Wertheim‑type association term to describe fluids that hydrogen‑bond or otherwise associate (water, alcohols, acids, amines). The Helmholtz free energy is A = A^cubic + A^assoc. The cubic part uses the SRK attractive parameter a(T) = a_c α(T_r, ω) and co‑volume b. The association term accounts for site‑site bonding: A^assoc/RT = Σ_i x_i Σ_A (ln X_A^i – X_A^i/2 + 1/2), where X_A^i is the fraction of site A on molecule i not bonded. The association strength is governed by the association volume β_AiBj and energy ε_AiBj for each pair of sites.

Pure‑component CPA parameters are: critical temperature T_c, critical pressure P_c, acentric factor ω (for the SRK part), and for each associating molecule the number of association sites (e.g., water: 4 sites, 2 electron‑donor, 2 electron‑acceptor), the association energy ε/k_B (K), and association volume β. These five parameters are regressed simultaneously against vapor‑pressure and saturated‑liquid‑density data. Typical values for water: ε/k_B = 2500 K, β = 0.08; for methanol: ε/k_B = 2300 K, β = 0.06. The cross‑association between unlike molecules (e.g., water‑ethanol) uses combining rules: ε_ij = √(ε_ii ε_jj) (1 – k_ij) and β_ij = (β_ii + β_jj)/2, where k_ij is a binary interaction parameter fitted to VLE data.

VLE predictions with CPA are performed by solving the equality of component fugacities: φ_i^L x_i P = φ_i^V y_i P. The fugacity coefficient φ_i includes contributions from both cubic and association terms. CPA excels for systems with strong self‑ and cross‑association, such as water‑alcohol, water‑acid, and amine‑water mixtures, where conventional cubic EOS fail to capture the highly non‑ideal liquid phase. For example, the water‑ethanol system at 1 atm shows an azeotrope at 0.89 mole fraction ethanol; CPA reproduces the azeotropic composition within 0.01 mole fraction using a single binary k_ij ≈ 0.02.

Parameter estimation strategy: first fit pure‑component parameters to vapor‑pressure and density data (typically 20–30 points spanning 0.5–0.95 T_c). Then regress binary k_ij against isothermal VLE data (P‑x‑y) or isobaric data (T‑x‑y). For multicomponent mixtures, the binary parameters are assumed transferable; ternary predictions are then tested without additional fitting. CPA has been extended to electrolyte solutions (e‑CPA) by adding a Debye‑Hückel term for long‑range ionic interactions, enabling modeling of aqueous amine‑CO₂ systems relevant to carbon capture.

Modeling Solid Solubility in Mixed Solvents: The Modified Apelblat Equation and Its Extensions

The Apelblat equation, originally ln x = A + B/T + C ln T, correlates the mole‑fraction solubility x of a solid in a pure solvent as a function of temperature. For mixed solvents, the modified Apelblat equation introduces composition dependence: ln x = A + B/T + C ln T + D w₁ + E w₁², where w₁ is the mass fraction of solvent 1 (the other solvent fraction is w₂ = 1 – w₁). The coefficients A–E are obtained by nonlinear regression of experimental solubility data measured at several temperatures and solvent compositions. This semi‑empirical form captures both the temperature effect (via B and C) and the cosolvency/anti‑cosolvency behavior (via D and E).

Thermodynamic consistency requires that the modified Apelblat equation be derivable from the equality of fugacities: f_solid = x γ φ^sat P_sat exp[∫V^∞/RT dP]. Taking the natural logarithm and assuming the Poynting correction and φ^sat are weakly temperature‑dependent yields ln x = –ΔH_fus/R (1/T – 1/T_m) + ln γ + constant. The term –ΔH_fus/R (1/T) corresponds to B/T, while the temperature dependence of γ (through activity‑coefficient models such as Wilson, NRTL, or UNIQUAC) generates the C ln T and composition terms. Thus the modified Apelblat coefficients have physical meaning: B ≈ –ΔH_fus/R, C reflects the heat‑capacity difference between solid and liquid, and D, E encode the solvent‑composition dependence of γ.

Extensions include the Jouyban–Acree model, which writes ln x = w₁ ln x₁ + w₂ ln x₂ + Σ_i Σ_j w_i w_j J_ij, where x₁, x₂ are solubilities in pure solvents and J_ij are interaction parameters. The modified Apelblat can be combined with Jouyban–Acree by letting the pure‑solvent solubilities follow Apelblat expressions, yielding a fully predictive model for any temperature and composition. Another extension incorporates the effect of pH for ionizable solids: ln x = ln x₀ + ln(1 + 10^(pH–pKa)) for acids, where x₀ is the intrinsic solubility of the neutral form. This pH‑modified Apelblat is widely used in pharmaceutical preformulation.

Practical application: a dataset for ibuprofen solubility in ethanol‑water mixtures at 298–318 K yields A = –12.3, B = 3500 K, C = 1.8, D = 4.2, E = –3.5 (R² = 0.998). The model predicts a maximum solubility at ~30 wt % ethanol (cosolvency peak). For process design, the equation is embedded in a flowsheet simulator to size crystallization vessels; the temperature derivative ∂ln x/∂T = –B/T² + C/T provides the supersaturation driving force for cooling crystallization. Care must be taken when extrapolating beyond the experimental temperature range, as the ln T term can cause unphysical curvature.

  • Assuming the modified Apelblat equation is universally valid without checking the underlying activity‑coefficient model; it fails for systems with strong specific interactions (e.g., hydrogen‑bonded complexes) that are not captured by a simple polynomial in w₁.
  • Neglecting the Poynting correction for solid solutes at pressures above 15 MPa; the exponential term can change the fugacity by 20–30 % and lead to systematic solubility over‑prediction.
  • Using pure‑component CPA association parameters for cross‑association without fitting a binary k_ij; the combining rules alone often give errors >10 % in azeotropic composition for water‑alcohol systems.
  • Treating the plait point in quaternary LLE as a simple intersection of tie‑lines; the true critical point requires solving the spinodal condition (determinant of the Hessian = 0) and may lie outside the experimental tie‑line envelope.
  • Assuming Henry’s constant for gases in molten salts is pressure‑independent; at >10 MPa the Poynting correction and gas‑phase non‑ideality (φ_G ≠ 1) must be included.
  • Overlooking the viscosity increase in DESs upon water removal; a 5 wt % water loss can raise viscosity by an order of magnitude, invalidating mass‑transfer correlations based on constant diffusivity.
  • Applying the regular‑solution solubility‑parameter approach to molten salts without accounting for ionic charge effects; the Born solvation term dominates for polar gases like CO₂ and must be added to the regular‑solution expression.

These advanced topics illustrate how thermodynamic models must be adapted to the specific molecular interactions and phase behavior encountered in modern separation and reaction engineering.

Non‑Equilibrium Thermodynamics of Diffusion in Concentrated Solutions: Maxwell‑Stefan Framework

The Maxwell‑Stefan (MS) formulation provides a rigorous description of multicomponent diffusion by relating the gradient of chemical potential to friction forces between species. For an n‑component mixture the MS equations read

∇μ_i = RT ∑j≠i (x_j J_i − x_i J_j) / (c Dij) (i = 1…n)

where μ_i is the chemical potential, x_i the mole fraction, J_i the molar flux relative to a fixed frame, c the total molar concentration, and Dij the Maxwell‑Stefan diffusivity for the i–j pair. Unlike Fick’s law, the MS approach remains valid at high concentrations because the driving force is the true thermodynamic gradient and the friction coefficients Dij are symmetric (Dij=Dji) and composition‑dependent.

Thermodynamic Factor and Activity Coefficients

The chemical‑potential gradient expands to ∇μ_i = RT ∇ln a_i = RT (∇ln x_i + ∇ln γ_i). The term ∇ln γ_i introduces the thermodynamic factor Γik = δik + x_i (∂ln γ_i/∂x_k)T,P,xl≠i,k. In matrix form the flux–gradient relation becomes J = −c D Γ ∇x, where D is the matrix of MS diffusivities. For electrolyte solutions the activity coefficients γ_i are obtained from Pitzer or e‑NRTL models, ensuring that the thermodynamic factor captures ion‑pairing and long‑range electrostatic contributions.

Boundary Conditions and the Stefan–Maxwell Paradox

At a gas–liquid interface the MS equations predict a finite flux even when the concentration gradient vanishes, provided a chemical‑potential gradient exists due to non‑ideality. This resolves the Stefan–Maxwell paradox observed in evaporation of concentrated brines. Practical implementation requires a reference velocity; the molar‑average velocity v* = ∑ x_i v_i is commonly used, leading to the constraint ∑ J_i = 0.

Numerical Solution Strategies

Discretisation of the MS equations in one‑dimensional diffusion cells yields a system of nonlinear algebraic equations. The most robust approach couples a Newton–Raphson solver for the fluxes with an inner loop that updates activity coefficients from the chosen electrolyte model. Convergence is accelerated by scaling the equations with the diagonal elements of the friction matrix. Typical MS diffusivities for NaCl–H₂O at 298 K are 1.5 × 10⁻⁹ m² s⁻¹, decreasing by 30 % at 5 mol kg⁻¹ due to increased ion‑ion friction.

Thermodynamics of Hydrotrope Solutions: Solubilization Mechanisms and Phase Diagrams

Hydrotropes are short‑chain amphiphiles (e.g., sodium benzoate, urea, nicotinamide) that increase the aqueous solubility of poorly soluble drugs without forming conventional micelles. Their action is described by a two‑step equilibrium: (1) self‑association of hydrotrope molecules into loose aggregates above a minimum hydrotrope concentration (MHC), and (2) binding of the solute to these aggregates. The overall solubility enhancement factor S/S₀ follows the empirical Setchenow‑type relation

log(S/S₀) = K_H C_H

where C_H is the hydrotrope concentration and K_H the hydrotrope‑solute interaction constant. More rigorous models treat the aggregate as a distinct phase with a binding constant K_b and an aggregation number n_agg. The mass‑balance equations yield

S = S₀ (1 + K_b C_Hn_agg)

which reduces to a linear Setchenow plot at low C_H and predicts a plateau when aggregate saturation occurs.

Phase Diagrams and the MHC

Binary hydrotrope–water phase diagrams display a single homogeneous region up to the MHC, beyond which a second liquid phase rich in hydrotrope may appear (e.g., sodium p‑toluidine sulfonate). The MHC is identified experimentally by a break in conductivity, surface tension, or NMR chemical shift. For urea the MHC is ≈ 8 mol kg⁻¹, whereas for sodium benzoate it is ≈ 2 mol kg⁻¹. The location of the MHC correlates with the hydrotrope’s hydrophobic surface area and its ability to disrupt water structure.

Thermodynamic Interpretation

The standard free energy of transfer of a solute from pure water to a hydrotrope solution is ΔG_tr = −RT ln(S/S₀). Decomposition into enthalpic and entropic contributions via van’t Hoff analysis shows that hydrotrope solubilization is often entropy‑driven at room temperature, reflecting the release of ordered water molecules from the solute’s hydrophobic surface. Calorimetric data for nicotinamide–drug systems give ΔH_tr ≈ +5 kJ mol⁻¹ and ΔS_tr ≈ +50 J mol⁻¹ K⁻¹.

Predictive Correlations

The Jouyban–Acree model extended to hydrotrope systems writes log S = w_w log S_w + w_H log S_H + w_w w_H JwH, where w_w and w_H are mass fractions of water and hydrotrope, S_w and S_H are solubilities in the pure solvents, and JwH is an interaction parameter fitted from three‑point solubility data. This semi‑empirical form reproduces the characteristic sigmoidal solubility curves and enables interpolation for formulation design.

Worked Example: Maxwell‑Stefan Flux in a Binary Electrolyte
A 1‑D diffusion cell contains 2 mol kg⁻¹ NaCl (species 1) and water (species 2) at 298 K. The mole‑fraction gradient of NaCl is ∇x₁ = −0.02 m⁻¹. The Maxwell‑Stefan diffusivity D₁₂ = 1.5 × 10⁻⁹ m² s⁻¹. The activity coefficient of NaCl at this concentration is γ₁ = 0.72 and its derivative ∂lnγ₁/∂x₁ = 1.8. Compute the molar flux J₁ relative to the molar‑average velocity.
The thermodynamic factor Γ₁₁ = 1 + x₁ (∂lnγ₁/∂x₁). For a 2 mol kg⁻¹ solution x₁ ≈ 0.035. Thus Γ₁₁ = 1 + 0.035 × 1.8 = 1.063. The MS equation for a binary mixture reduces to J₁ = −c D₁₂ Γ₁₁ ∇x₁. Total molar concentration c ≈ 55.5 mol L⁻¹ = 5.55 × 10⁴ mol m⁻³. Substituting: J₁ = −(5.55 × 10⁴) × (1.5 × 10⁻⁹) × 1.063 × (−0.02) = 1.77 × 10⁻⁶ mol m⁻² s⁻¹. The positive sign indicates NaCl diffuses toward lower x₁, as expected.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the flux in a concentrated electrolyte, first write the Maxwell‑Stefan equation, identify the thermodynamic factor from the given activity‑coefficient model, and remember the constraint ∑J_i = 0 for the molar‑average frame. If only Fick’s law is offered as an option, check whether the problem explicitly states “dilute” or “ideal”; otherwise the MS formulation is the correct choice.

Solubility of Minerals in Geothermal Brines: Extended Pitzer Model and Scale Prediction

Geothermal brines contain high concentrations of Na⁺, K⁺, Ca²⁺, Mg²⁺, Cl⁻, SO₄²⁻, HCO₃⁻ and dissolved silica at temperatures up to 350 °C and pressures of 20 MPa. Accurate prediction of mineral scaling (e.g., calcite, anhydrite, silica) requires an activity model that remains valid at ionic strengths > 10 mol kg⁻¹. The extended Pitzer formalism adds temperature‑ and pressure‑dependent binary (β⁽⁰⁾, β⁽¹⁾, Cφ) and ternary (θ, ψ) interaction parameters to the standard equations:

ln γ_i = z_i² f^γ + ∑_j m_j (2βij⁽⁰⁾ + 2βij⁽¹⁾ g(α√I) + Cij m_j) + ∑j,k m_j m_k ψijk

where f^γ = −A_φ [√I/(1+√I) + (2/α) ln(1+α√I)], A_φ is the Debye‑Hückel parameter, and g(x) = 2(1−(1+x)e−x)/x². The pressure dependence enters through the Pitzer‑Simonson‑Clegg (PSC) extension, which adds a term V_i^∞ (P−P_ref)/RT to the chemical potential, with V_i^∞ the infinite‑dilution partial molar volume.

Parameterization for High‑Temperature Brines

Binary parameters for NaCl, CaCl₂, MgCl₂, Na₂SO₄, and CaSO₄ have been regressed against osmotic coefficient and solubility data up to 300 °C. For example, β⁽⁰⁾(Na⁺,Cl⁻) = 0.0765 + 1.2 × 10⁻⁴ (T−298) − 3.5 × 10⁻⁷ (T−298)² (T in K). Ternary parameters θ(Na⁺,K⁺) and ψ(Na⁺,K⁺,Cl⁻) are essential to reproduce the observed depression of halite solubility in mixed‑cation brines.

Scale‑Prediction Workflow

1. Specify bulk composition, temperature, pressure. 2. Compute ionic strength I and activity coefficients γ_i using the extended Pitzer equations. 3. Evaluate the ion‑activity product Q = ∏ a_iν_i for each mineral. 4. Compare Q with the equilibrium constant K_sp(T,P) obtained from the Helgeson‑Kirkham‑Flowers (HKF) equation of state. 5. If Q > K_sp, the mineral is supersaturated; the scaling tendency is quantified by the saturation index SI = log₁₀(Q/K_sp). 6. Couple with a kinetic model (e.g., surface‑reaction controlled rate r = k (1−Q/K_sp)^n) to estimate deposition rates in heat exchangers.

Limitations and Recent Advances

The Pitzer model assumes pairwise additivity of interactions; at very high concentrations (> 20 mol kg⁻¹) many‑body terms become significant. The Specific Ion Interaction Theory (SIT) and the Bromley model provide alternative frameworks but lack the extensive high‑temperature parameter database of Pitzer. Recent work incorporates the e‑NRTL equation for mixed‑solvent geothermal fluids (water–CO₂–NaCl) and uses Monte‑Carlo sampling of parameter uncertainties to generate probabilistic scaling forecasts.

Thermodynamics of Ionic Liquid Mixtures: Activity‑Coefficient Models for IL‑Molecular Solvent Systems

Ionic liquids (ILs) combined with molecular solvents (water, alcohols, acetonitrile) form highly non‑ideal mixtures where both long‑range electrostatic and short‑range hydrogen‑bonding forces operate. Accurate phase‑equilibrium predictions require activity‑coefficient models that treat the IL as a dissociated ion pair and the solvent as a neutral species. The electrolyte NRTL (e‑NRTL) model extends the classical NRTL by adding a Born term for ion solvation and a Debye‑Hückel term for long‑range forces:

ln γ_i = ln γ_iNRTL + ln γ_iBorn + ln γ_iDH

The NRTL contribution uses binary interaction parameters τij and non‑randomness factors αij for all pairs (cation–anion, cation–solvent, anion–solvent). The Born term accounts for the work of transferring an ion from a reference dielectric medium (ε_ref) to the mixture dielectric constant ε_mix: ln γ_iBorn = (z_i² e² / 8π ε₀ r_i) (1/ε_mix − 1/ε_ref) / RT. The Debye‑Hückel term uses the Pitzer‑type ionic strength I = ½ ∑ m_i z_i².

Parameter Estimation Strategy

Binary VLE or LLE data for IL–solvent systems at several temperatures are regressed simultaneously for τij(T) = aij + bij/T + cij ln T. The cation–anion parameters are often fixed from pure‑IL properties (density, conductivity) to reduce correlation. Cross‑association parameters for hydrogen‑bonding solvents (e.g., water–anion) are introduced via the association term of the CPA equation of state, but in e‑NRTL they appear as temperature‑dependent τ values. Typical root‑mean‑square deviations in bubble‑point pressure for [bmim][PF₆]–ethanol are < 2 kPa when three binary parameters per pair are used.

Prediction of Liquid‑Liquid Equilibria

For partially miscible IL–water systems (e.g., [omim][BF₄]–water) the e‑NRTL model predicts a upper critical solution temperature (UCST) near 320 K. The binodal curve is obtained by solving the equality of component chemical potentials in the two liquid phases. The plait point composition is located by the additional condition that the determinant of the Hessian matrix of the Gibbs energy vanishes. Experimental tie‑line data for [hmim][Tf₂N]–water at 298 K show a water‑rich phase with x_water ≈ 0.98 and an IL‑rich phase with x_water ≈ 0.02, which the model reproduces within 0.005 mole fraction.

Extension to Multicomponent Electrolyte Solutions

When a second salt (e.g., LiTf₂N) is added to an IL–solvent mixture, the e‑NRTL framework accommodates the extra ions by introducing cation–cation, anion–anion, and cross‑ion interaction parameters. The model remains thermodynamically consistent because the Gibbs‑Duhem equation is satisfied by construction. Recent applications include the design of IL‑based electrolytes for lithium‑ion batteries, where the activity coefficients of Li⁺ and Tf₂N⁻ determine the transference number and the electrochemical stability window.

Phase Behavior of Polymer Solutions in Mixed Solvents: Composition‑Dependent Flory‑Huggins Interaction Parameters

The Flory‑Huggins lattice theory for a polymer (component p) in a binary solvent mixture (components 1 and 2) expresses the Gibbs free energy of mixing per lattice site as

ΔG_mix/RT = φ_p ln φ_p / N_p + φ1 ln φ1 + φ2 ln φ2 + χp1 φ_p φ1 + χp2 φ_p φ2 + χ12 φ1 φ2

where φ_i are volume fractions, N_p the degree of polymerization, and χij the binary interaction parameters. In mixed solvents the effective polymer‑solvent interaction parameter χp,eff becomes composition dependent:

χp,eff = φ1 χp1 + φ2 χp2 − φ1 φ2 χ12

This expression captures cosolvency (χp,eff < min(χp1, χp2)) when χ12 is large and positive, i.e., the two solvents repel each other and preferentially solvate the polymer. Conversely, anti‑cosolvency arises when χ12 is negative, leading to a maximum in χp,eff at intermediate compositions.

Temperature and Pressure Dependence

The χ parameters are commonly modeled as χij = Aij + Bij/T + Cij P, where A, B, C are fitted to cloud‑point data. For poly(N‑isopropylacrylamide) (PNIPAM) in water–methanol, χp1 (water) shows a strong temperature dependence (B ≈ 200 K) causing a lower critical solution temperature (LCST) near 305 K, while χp2 (methanol) is nearly temperature independent. The cross term χ12 for water–methanol is negative at low temperatures, producing a pronounced anti‑cosolvency window between 280 K and 300 K.

Spinodal and Binodal Construction

The spinodal curve is defined by the condition ∂²(ΔG_mix/RT)/∂φ_p² = 0, yielding a quadratic equation in φ_p for a given solvent composition. The binodal (coexistence curve) is obtained by the common‑tangent construction on the ΔG_mix surface, which reduces to solving the equality of chemical potentials μ_p and μ1 (or μ2) in the two phases. Numerical continuation methods trace the binodal as a function of temperature, revealing closed‑loop miscibility gaps for systems exhibiting both UCST and LCST.

Application to Polymer Fractionation

In industrial polymer purification, a mixed‑solvent system is chosen so that the target polymer fraction lies inside the two‑phase region while impurities remain in the single‑phase region. For example, polystyrene (N_p ≈ 10⁴) in cyclohexane–methyl ethyl ketone at 298 K shows a binodal that shifts to lower polymer concentration as the ketone fraction increases. By adjusting the solvent ratio, a selective precipitation of high‑molecular‑weight chains is achieved, reducing polydispersity from 2.5 to 1.2 in a single step.

  • Confusing the Maxwell‑Stefan diffusivity with the Fickian diffusion coefficient; they are equal only in ideal dilute mixtures.
  • Assuming the Setchenow constant K_H is independent of temperature; it varies as ΔH_sol/RT² and must be corrected for non‑isothermal processes.
  • Using the standard Pitzer model at temperatures above 300 °C without the pressure‑dependent PSC extension; the omission leads to > 20 % error in calcite saturation index.
  • Neglecting the Born solvation term in e‑NRTL for IL–water mixtures; the dielectric constant of the mixture changes dramatically with composition and dominates the activity coefficient of the ions.
  • Treating χp,eff as a simple linear blend of χp1 and χp2 and ignoring the χ12 cross term; this misses both cosolvency and anti‑cosolvency phenomena.
  • Forgetting that the Flory‑Huggins lattice coordination number is implicitly set to unity; real polymers require a correction factor for chain stiffness when N_p is small.
  • Always verify the thermodynamic consistency of any activity‑coefficient model by checking the Gibbs‑Duhem integral before using it for process simulation.

Thermodynamic Modeling of Aqueous Electrolyte Solutions at High Temperatures and Pressures: The Revised Helgeson‑Kirkham‑Flowers (HKF) Model
