The revised HKF model provides a unified framework for calculating standard partial molal thermodynamic properties of aqueous ions and neutral species up to 1000 °C and 5 kbar. The standard partial molal Gibbs energy of formation ΔG°_f is expressed as a sum of a reference‑state term at 25 °C and 1 bar plus temperature‑ and pressure‑dependent contributions derived from the Born equation and a non‑Born “intrinsic” term. The intrinsic heat capacity C°_p,intr is represented by the empirical function C°_p,intr = a₁ + a₂/(T‑θ)² + a₃/(T‑θ) + a₄T², where θ = 228 K. The standard partial molal volume V° is given by V° = c₁ + c₂/(T‑θ) + ω Q, with ω the Born coefficient and Q the solvent‑dependent Born function. The model requires a set of species‑specific parameters (a₁‑a₄, c₁, c₂, ω) that are regressed from experimental solubility, enthalpy, and heat‑capacity data. Because the Born functions depend on the dielectric constant of water ε(T,P), the revised HKF model incorporates the Haar‑Gallagher‑Kell equation of state for water to evaluate ε and its derivatives. This thermodynamic consistency enables the calculation of equilibrium constants for mineral dissolution, gas‑water reactions, and redox couples in hydrothermal systems. In practice, the model is implemented in geochemical codes such as SUPCRT92 and Geochemist’s Workbench, where it predicts the speciation of Na⁺, Cl⁻, CO₂(aq), H₂S(aq), and metal complexes in ore‑forming fluids. A key limitation is the extrapolation beyond the calibrated temperature‑pressure range, where the empirical heat‑capacity function may produce unphysical curvature. Users must therefore verify that the calculated ΔG°_f values yield realistic saturation indices for well‑constrained mineral assemblages before applying the model to unexplored conditions.

Phase Behavior of Polymer Gels: Flory‑Rehner Theory and Swelling Equilibria

The Flory‑Rehner theory describes the equilibrium swelling of a covalently cross‑linked polymer network in a solvent by balancing the mixing free energy ΔG_mix and the elastic free energy ΔG_el. For a network of polymer volume fraction φ_p in the swollen state, the mixing term per unit reference volume is ΔG_mix/RT = (φ_p/ν₁)[ln(1‑φ_p) + φ_p + χφ_p²], where ν₁ is the molar volume of the solvent and χ the Flory‑Huggins interaction parameter. The elastic contribution for an affine network is ΔG_el/RT = (ν_e/2)[(φ_p/φ_p,0)^(2/3) − 1 − ln(φ_p/φ_p,0)^(1/3)], where ν_e is the effective number of network chains per unit reference volume and φ_p,0 the polymer volume fraction in the reference (dry) state. At equilibrium, the chemical potential of the solvent inside the gel equals that of the external bath, giving the swelling condition ∂(ΔG_mix+ΔG_el)/∂φ_p = 0. This yields an implicit equation for the equilibrium swelling ratio Q = 1/φ_p: ln(1‑1/Q) + 1/Q + χ/Q² + ν_e V₁( Q^(-1/3) − Q^(-1) ) = 0. The theory predicts that increasing cross‑link density (larger ν_e) reduces Q, while improving solvent quality (lower χ) increases Q. Temperature enters through χ(T) = A + B/T, so gels with a lower critical solution temperature (LCST) such as poly(N‑isopropylacrylamide) collapse sharply when χ exceeds a critical value. The Flory‑Rehner framework also accommodates ionic gels by adding a Donnan osmotic term Π_Donnan = RT( c_ion,in − c_ion,out ), which can dominate swelling in polyelectrolyte hydrogels. Experimental validation requires accurate determination of ν_e from shear modulus measurements (G = ν_e RT) and of χ from independent solution‑phase data. Common pitfalls include neglecting the volume change on mixing, using the dry‑state reference volume instead of the preparation‑state reference, and assuming the affine network model for loosely cross‑linked gels where the phantom‑network correction (ν_e → ν_e (1‑2/f), f = functionality) is more appropriate.

Worked Example: Equilibrium Swelling of a Neutral Hydrogel
A poly(ethylene glycol) diacrylate hydrogel is prepared in the dry state (φ_p,0 = 1). The network has an effective cross‑link density ν_e = 2.5 × 10⁻⁴ mol cm⁻³. The solvent is water at 25 °C (ν₁ = 18 cm³ mol⁻¹) with χ = 0.45. Calculate the equilibrium swelling ratio Q.
Insert the data into the Flory‑Rehner swelling equation: ln(1‑1/Q) + 1/Q + 0.45/Q² + (2.5 × 10⁻⁴ mol cm⁻³)(18 cm³ mol⁻¹)( Q^(-1/3) − Q^(-1) ) = 0. The elastic prefactor ν_e V₁ = 4.5 × 10⁻³. Solve numerically (e.g., Newton‑Raphson). Starting guess Q = 10 gives f(Q) ≈ -0.018; Q = 12 gives f(Q) ≈ +0.004. Interpolation yields Q ≈ 11.6. Thus the hydrogel absorbs about 11.6 g water per gram dry polymer at equilibrium.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a swelling problem appears, first identify whether the gel is neutral or ionic. For neutral gels, write the Flory‑Rehner equation exactly as shown; for ionic gels add the Donnan term. Convert all quantities to consistent units (cm³, mol, J) before substituting. Remember that ν_e is often given as mol cm⁻³ or mol kg⁻¹; use the reference volume that matches the definition of φ_p,0. If the problem supplies the shear modulus G, compute ν_e = G/RT. Finally, check the limiting cases: Q → ∞ for χ < 0.5 and ν_e → 0, and Q → 1 for very high ν_e.

Solubility of CO₂ in Brine for Carbon Capture: Pitzer and Duan‑Sun Models

Accurate prediction of CO₂ solubility in high‑salinity brines is essential for geological carbon sequestration. The Pitzer ion‑interaction model calculates the activity coefficients of aqueous species (including CO₂(aq)) in electrolyte solutions up to 6 mol kg⁻¹ ionic strength. The excess Gibbs energy G^E is expressed as a sum of binary (β⁽⁰⁾, β⁽¹⁾, β⁽²⁾) and ternary (C_φ) interaction parameters for each ion pair and triplet. For the CO₂–H₂O–NaCl system, the relevant parameters are β⁽⁰⁾_NaCl, β⁽¹⁾_NaCl, C_φ_NaCl, and the CO₂–Na⁺, CO₂–Cl⁻ binary parameters. The Henry’s constant for CO₂ in pure water, K_H⁰(T), is corrected for salinity by the Setchenov relation ln(K_H/K_H⁰) = k_s m_NaCl, where the Setchenov coefficient k_s is itself temperature dependent. The Duan‑Sun model provides a more direct route: it correlates the fugacity of CO₂ in the vapor phase with the aqueous CO₂ concentration using an equation of state for the CO₂‑H₂O‑NaCl mixture. The model expresses the CO₂ fugacity coefficient φ_CO₂ as a function of temperature, pressure, and salinity, and the aqueous CO₂ mole fraction x_CO₂ = φ_CO₂ f_CO₂ / K_H⁰(T) γ_CO₂, where γ_CO₂ is the activity coefficient from the Pitzer formalism. Both approaches require accurate pure‑water Henry’s constants (e.g., the Harvey‑Carroll correlation) and reliable Pitzer parameters for the CO₂‑ion interactions, which are available up to 200 °C and 200 bar. At higher temperatures the Pitzer model must be extended with the pressure‑dependent PSC (Pitzer‑Simonson‑Clegg) formulation. A practical workflow for reservoir simulation: (1) compute the brine composition and ionic strength; (2) evaluate γ_CO₂ with the Pitzer model; (3) obtain K_H⁰(T) from the Harvey correlation; (4) apply the Setchenov correction or use the Duan‑Sun EOS directly to find x_CO₂ at the reservoir pressure; (5) convert to molality for reactive transport calculations. Validation against experimental data from the CO₂‑Brine‑Rock database shows that the combined Pitzer‑Duan‑Sun approach predicts CO₂ solubility within ±5 % for NaCl‑dominant brines up to 4 mol kg⁻¹ and 150 °C.

Electrochemical Solubility Limits: Pourbaix Diagrams and the Thermodynamics of Mineral Stability

Pourbaix (potential‑pH) diagrams map the thermodynamic stability fields of aqueous species, solid oxides, hydroxides, and metals as a function of electrode potential E (V vs SHE) and pH. The boundaries are derived from the Nernst equation for each half‑reaction. For a generic redox couple Ox + ne⁻ ⇌ Red, the equilibrium potential is E = E° – (0.05916/n) log(a_Red/a_Ox) at 25 °C. When a solid phase participates, its activity is unity, giving a line of constant E or pH. For example, the Fe–H₂O system includes the lines: Fe²⁺ + 2e⁻ ⇌ Fe(s) (E = –0.44 V), Fe³⁺ + e⁻ ⇌ Fe²⁺ (E = 0.77 V – 0.059 log(a_Fe²⁺/a_Fe³⁺)), and the solubility equilibria Fe(OH)₂(s) + 2H⁺ ⇌ Fe²⁺ + 2H₂O (log a_Fe²⁺ = log K_sp + 2 pH) and Fe₂O₃(s) + 6H⁺ ⇌ 2Fe³⁺ + 3H₂O (log a_Fe³⁺ = ½ log K_sp + 3 pH). The intersection of these lines defines the immunity, corrosion, and passivation regions. Construction of a Pourbaix diagram requires a consistent thermodynamic database (e.g., SUPCRT92, THERMODDEM) providing ΔG°_f for all species at the temperature of interest. Temperature corrections are applied via the van’t Hoff equation for equilibrium constants and the temperature derivative of E° (ΔS°/nF). In practice, kinetic barriers often shift the observed corrosion potential away from the thermodynamic lines; therefore, Pourbaix diagrams are best used as a thermodynamic envelope rather than a kinetic prediction. For multicomponent systems (e.g., Fe‑Cr‑Ni alloys), the diagram becomes a three‑dimensional surface in E‑pH‑composition space, and computational tools such as FactSage or HSC Chemistry generate sectional diagrams at fixed alloy composition. A common examination trap is to treat the water stability lines (O₂/H₂O and H⁺/H₂) as fixed at 1.23 V and 0 V regardless of pH; the correct expressions are E_O₂ = 1.229 – 0.059 pH and E_H₂ = –0.059 pH at 25 °C.

Thermodynamics of Surfactant Self‑Assembly: Micellization, Liquid‑Crystalline Phases, and Phase Diagrams

Surfactant self‑assembly is governed by the balance between hydrophobic tail aggregation and head‑group repulsion. The standard free energy of micellization per mole of surfactant is ΔG°_mic = RT ln(CMC), where CMC is the critical micelle concentration expressed as a mole fraction. For ionic surfactants, the counter‑ion binding degree β reduces the effective charge, giving ΔG°_mic = (2‑β)RT ln(CMC). The Israelachvili packing parameter p = v/(a₀ l_c) (v = tail volume, a₀ = optimal head‑group area, l_c = critical chain length) predicts the aggregate morphology: p < 1/3 spherical micelles, 1/3 < p < 1/2 cylindrical micelles, 1/2 < p < 1 vesicles or bilayers, p ≈ 1 planar lamellae, p > 1 inverted structures. Temperature influences p through changes in a₀ (head‑group hydration) and v (chain melting). The Krafft temperature T_K is the point at which the surfactant solubility equals the CMC; below T_K the surfactant precipitates as a hydrated solid. Phase diagrams for binary surfactant‑water systems display isotropic micellar (L₁), hexagonal (H₁), lamellar (L_α), and bicontinuous cubic (V₁) regions. The boundaries are located by equating the chemical potentials of surfactant in coexisting phases, which requires the surfactant activity in each phase. For the lamellar phase, the swelling is described by the Helfrich free energy F = (κ/2)(1/d − 1/d₀)² + B/d², where d is the repeat distance, κ the bending modulus, and B the compression modulus. Small‑angle X‑ray scattering (SAXS) provides d, allowing extraction of κ and B. In ternary systems (surfactant‑oil‑water), the Winsor classification (I, II, III, IV) maps the microemulsion type to the surfactant affinity difference (SAD) or the hydrophilic‑lipophilic deviation (HLD) parameter: HLD = ln(S) − k EACN + f(A) + C_c + T ΔT, where S is salinity, EACN the equivalent alkane carbon number, f(A) the alcohol contribution, and C_c the surfactant characteristic curvature. At HLD = 0 a balanced microemulsion (Winsor III) forms with ultralow interfacial tension. The thermodynamic consistency of these models is checked by verifying the Gibbs‑Duhem relation across the phase diagram. A frequent error in examinations is to confuse the CMC (a concentration) with the Krafft point (a temperature) and to assume that the CMC decreases monotonically with temperature for all surfactants; non‑ionic surfactants often show a minimum CMC near the cloud point.

  • Assuming the revised HKF model remains accurate beyond its calibrated 1000 °C/5 kbar limit without checking the heat‑capacity curvature.
  • Using the dry‑state reference volume in the Flory‑Rehner equation when the network was prepared in a swollen state.
  • Neglecting the Donnan osmotic term for polyelectrolyte gels and treating them as neutral networks.
  • Applying the Setchenov correction with a temperature‑independent k_s for CO₂ solubility at reservoir temperatures above 150 °C.
  • Treating the water stability lines in a Pourbaix diagram as horizontal lines instead of the correct pH‑dependent slopes.
  • Confusing the Krafft temperature with the CMC and assuming both decrease with temperature for ionic surfactants.
  • Using the packing parameter p without accounting for temperature‑dependent head‑group area a₀, leading to incorrect phase‑boundary predictions.

These advanced topics extend the core solution thermodynamics into geochemical, polymeric, electrochemical, and colloidal domains, providing the quantitative tools needed for high‑level competitive examinations.

Thermodynamic Modeling of Supercritical CO₂ Extraction: Solubility Correlations and Process Design

Supercritical carbon dioxide (scCO₂) is widely used for extracting non‑polar and moderately polar solutes because its density, and thus solvating power, can be tuned continuously by pressure and temperature. The solubility of a solid in scCO₂ is commonly correlated with the Chrastil equation, which expresses the mole‑fraction solubility y as a function of fluid density ρ and temperature T: ln y = k ln ρ + a/T + b, where k, a, and b are solute‑specific constants obtained from experimental data. An alternative, the Mendez‑Santiago‑Teja (MST) correlation, uses the reduced pressure and temperature of the solute to reduce the number of fitted parameters. Both correlations assume that the solute’s vapor pressure and the fluid’s fugacity coefficient are the dominant thermodynamic contributors.

Accurate prediction requires the pure‑component vapor pressure of the solute, often estimated with the Antoine or Wagner equations, and the fugacity coefficient of CO₂ from an equation of state such as Peng‑Robinson or Span‑Wagner. The solute’s sublimation pressure at the system temperature enters the equilibrium condition fᵢˢᵒˡᶦᵈ = yᵢ φᵢ P, where φᵢ is the fugacity coefficient of the solute in the supercritical phase. For many pharmaceuticals and natural products, the sublimation pressure is unknown and must be estimated from melting‑point data using the van’t Hoff relation, introducing additional uncertainty.

Process design for scCO₂ extraction proceeds by constructing solubility isotherms at the intended operating pressures (typically 10–30 MPa) and temperatures (313–353 K). The mass‑transfer rate is then modeled with a shrinking‑core or a diffusion‑controlled model, where the effective diffusivity of the solute in the supercritical phase is correlated with the fluid’s viscosity and density using the Wilke‑Chang or the Stokes‑Einstein relations. Economic optimization balances the higher solubility at elevated pressure against the increased compression cost and the possible thermal degradation of heat‑sensitive compounds.

Co‑solvents (entrainers) such as ethanol or water are frequently added to enhance the polarity of the supercritical phase. Their effect is incorporated by modifying the mixture density and by introducing a co‑solvent‑solute interaction term in the Chrastil or MST correlations. The co‑solvent concentration is usually limited to 5–10 mol % to avoid excessive pressure drop and to maintain the low‑viscosity advantage of scCO₂. Phase‑equilibrium calculations for the ternary system (solute‑CO₂‑co‑solvent) are performed with cubic equations of state combined with mixing rules such as Wong‑Sandler or Huron‑Vidal, which enforce the Gibbs‑Duhem consistency of activity coefficients.

Limitations of the correlation approach include extrapolation beyond the experimental pressure‑temperature envelope, neglect of solute‑solute clustering at high loadings, and the assumption of a single‑phase supercritical fluid. In practice, pilot‑scale extraction curves are used to validate the model, and the fitted parameters are updated iteratively. The final design package typically includes a solubility map, a mass‑balance spreadsheet, and a sensitivity analysis showing the impact of ±5 % variations in pressure, temperature, and co‑solvent fraction on overall yield.

Phase Behavior of Water‑Hydrocarbon Systems at High Pressure: The Peng‑Robinson EOS Approach

Water‑hydrocarbon equilibria are central to petroleum reservoir engineering, pipeline hydrate management, and carbon‑capture‑storage operations. At reservoir conditions (pressures up to 100 MPa, temperatures 300–450 K) the mutual solubility of water in the hydrocarbon phase and hydrocarbon in the aqueous phase deviates strongly from ideal‑solution behavior. The Peng‑Robinson equation of state (PR‑EOS) with the classical van der Waals one‑fluid mixing rules (quadratic in mole fraction for the attractive parameter a and linear for the co‑volume b) provides a computationally efficient framework for predicting these equilibria.

Binary interaction parameters kᵢⱼ for the water‑hydrocarbon pairs are regressed from experimental vapor‑liquid‑equilibrium (VLE) data or from infinite‑dilution activity coefficients. For light hydrocarbons (methane, ethane, propane) the kᵢⱼ values are typically small (0.01–0.03), whereas for heavier aromatics they can exceed 0.1. Temperature‑dependent kᵢⱼ correlations (e.g., kᵢⱼ = A + B/T) improve the representation of the critical locus, which is the line of critical points of the mixture as composition varies. The critical locus connects the pure‑component critical points and passes through a plait point where the two phases become identical.

Hydrate formation is a major operational hazard. The thermodynamic condition for hydrate equilibrium is expressed by the equality of the chemical potential of water in the hydrate phase and in the aqueous phase. The van der Waals–Platteeuw model supplies the hydrate‑phase chemical potential, while the aqueous‑phase water activity is obtained from the PR‑EOS combined with an activity‑coefficient model (e.g., electrolyte‑NRTL) for the dissolved salts. Inhibitors such as methanol or mono‑ethylene glycol shift the hydrate equilibrium curve by lowering the water activity; their effect is captured by adding the inhibitor as a third component in the flash calculation.

In pipeline flow assurance, the water‑hydrocarbon phase envelope is used to locate the dew‑point and bubble‑point curves at the operating pressure. A typical workflow performs an isothermal flash at the pipeline pressure for a range of overall water fractions. The resulting phase‑fraction and composition profiles identify the region where a free water phase appears, which is the condition for corrosion and hydrate risk. The PR‑EOS flash algorithm (successive substitution followed by Newton‑Raphson) converges reliably when the initial K‑values are estimated from the Wilson correlation corrected for the high‑pressure fugacity coefficients.

Validation against high‑pressure PVT data for synthetic reservoir fluids shows that the PR‑EOS with fitted kᵢⱼ predicts water content in the hydrocarbon phase within ±15 % for pressures up to 70 MPa. For higher pressures, the inclusion of a volume‑translation term (Peneloux correction) improves liquid‑density predictions and consequently the fugacity coefficients. The final model is embedded in process simulators (e.g., Aspen HYSYS, PVTSim) where it drives the design of dehydration units, inhibitor injection rates, and the specification of pipeline pressure‑temperature operating envelopes.

Examiner's trap
A binary mixture of water and n‑hexane at 350 K and 30 MPa is flashed using the Peng‑Robinson EOS with a constant binary interaction parameter k₁₂ = 0.025. The calculated water mole fraction in the hydrocarbon phase is 2.1 × 10⁻⁴, whereas experimental data report 3.8 × 10⁻⁴. Identify the most likely source of the discrepancy and propose a systematic correction.
The discrepancy arises because a constant k₁₂ cannot capture the strong temperature dependence of water‑hydrocarbon interactions near the critical region. The correct approach is to regress a temperature‑dependent k₁₂(T) = A + B/T from a set of VLE data spanning the temperature range of interest. Alternatively, replace the classical mixing rule with the Wong‑Sandler mixing rule coupled to an activity‑coefficient model (e.g., NRTL) that inherently satisfies the Gibbs‑Duhem equation and provides a more accurate representation of the aqueous‑phase non‑ideality. After refitting, the predicted water solubility typically falls within ±5 % of the experimental value.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for the effect of pressure on water solubility in a hydrocarbon, first state the thermodynamic equilibrium condition (equality of water fugacity in both phases). Then note that increasing pressure raises the hydrocarbon‑phase density, which increases the fugacity coefficient of water in that phase, thereby increasing solubility. Mention that the Peng‑Robinson EOS with a temperature‑dependent binary interaction parameter is the standard industrial tool, and that the Wong‑Sandler mixing rule is preferred for rigorous Gibbs‑Duhem consistency.

Thermodynamics of Microemulsion Formation: The Hydrophilic‑Lipophilic Deviation (HLD) Model

Microemulsions are thermodynamically stable, isotropic dispersions of oil and water stabilized by a surfactant film. The Hydrophilic‑Lipophilic Deviation (HLD) model quantifies the surfactant’s affinity for the two phases through a single dimensionless number: HLD = ln S – k · EACN + f(A) + C_c + ΔT·(T – T_ref), where S is the salinity (wt % NaCl), EACN the equivalent alkane carbon number of the oil, f(A) the contribution of co‑surfactant (e.g., alcohol), C_c the characteristic curvature of the surfactant, and ΔT the temperature coefficient. At HLD = 0 the system forms a balanced (Winsor III) microemulsion with ultralow interfacial tension (≈10⁻³ mN m⁻¹).

The characteristic curvature C_c encapsulates the surfactant molecular geometry: for a pure ionic surfactant C_c ≈ –2.5, for non‑ionic ethoxylates C_c varies linearly with the ethylene‑oxide number (EON) as C_c = α · EON + β. The salinity term ln S reflects the screening of head‑group repulsion, which drives the surfactant film toward the oil phase. The EACN term accounts for the oil’s hydrophobicity; higher EACN (more hydrophobic oil) makes the surfactant more water‑affine, shifting HLD negative. Co‑surfactants such as medium‑chain alcohols partition into the film and modify its spontaneous curvature, captured by f(A) = γ · ln (1 + x_A/x_ref).

Temperature influences HLD through two mechanisms: (i) dehydration of ethoxylate head groups reduces the effective head‑group area, making the surfactant more lipophilic (positive ΔT for non‑ionics), and (ii) the oil‑water interfacial tension itself varies with temperature, altering the bending energy of the film. The temperature coefficient ΔT is typically +0.01 K⁻¹ for C₁₂E₈ and –0.02 K⁻¹ for anionic sulfates. A phase diagram at fixed composition is constructed by plotting HLD versus temperature; the Winsor I (oil‑in‑water), Winsor II (water‑in‑oil), and Winsor III regions appear as HLD < 0, HLD > 0, and HLD ≈ 0, respectively.

Interfacial tension γ in the Winsor III region follows the scaling law γ = γ₀ exp(–c |HLD|), where γ₀ ≈ 30 mN m⁻¹ and c ≈ 0.5–1.0 depending on the surfactant system. This exponential decay explains why a small deviation from HLD = 0 leads to orders‑of‑magnitude increase in γ and loss of microemulsion stability. The solubilization parameters (volume of oil or water solubilized per unit surfactant) are maximal at HLD = 0 and decrease symmetrically as |HLD| grows, providing a practical metric for formulation optimization.

In formulation design, the target HLD is set to zero by adjusting salinity, co‑surfactant concentration, or temperature. For a given surfactant‑oil pair, the required salinity S* is obtained from S* = exp(k · EACN – f(A) – C_c – ΔT·(T – T_ref)). The model has been validated for hundreds of surfactant‑oil‑water systems and is the basis of the HLD‑NAC (Net Average Curvature) extension, which incorporates the surfactant packing parameter and the film bending modulus to predict the droplet radius and the viscosity of the microemulsion.

Solubility Prediction for Pharmaceuticals Using the General Solubility Equation and Abraham Parameters

The General Solubility Equation (GSE) relates the aqueous solubility (log S_w, mol L⁻¹) of a neutral organic compound at 25 °C to its melting point (T_m, K) and octanol‑water partition coefficient (log P_ow): log S_w = 0.5 – 0.01 (T_m – 25) – log P_ow. For ionizable drugs, the pH‑dependent solubility is obtained by adding the Henderson‑Hasselbalch term: log S(pH) = log S_w + log(1 + 10pH – pK_a) for acids, and log S(pH) = log S_w + log(1 + 10pK_a – pH) for bases. The GSE assumes ideal solution behavior in the solid phase and that the activity coefficient of the solute in water is approximated by 1/P_ow.

Abraham’s linear free‑energy relationship (LFER) provides a more mechanistic alternative: log S_w = c + e·E + s·S + a·A + b·B + v·V, where E, S, A, B, V are solute descriptors (excess molar refraction, dipolarity/polarizability, hydrogen‑bond acidity, hydrogen‑bond basicity, McGowan volume) and c, e, s, a, b, v are system coefficients for water at 25 °C (c = –0.12, e = 0.56, s = 0.34, a = 3.45, b = 4.12, v = 2.78). The descriptors are obtained from experimental partition data or predicted by quantum‑chemical methods (e.g., COSMO‑RS). The LFER captures specific hydrogen‑bonding interactions that the GSE neglects, improving predictions for polyfunctional drug molecules.

For compounds that form hydrates or polymorphs, the apparent melting point in the GSE must correspond to the thermodynamically stable polymorph at 25 °C. If a metastable polymorph is used, the predicted solubility can be overestimated by up to two log units. The heat‑of‑fusion correction (ΔH_fus) can be introduced via the van’t Hoff equation: ln S = –ΔH_fus/R (1/T – 1/T_m) + ln γ, where γ is the activity coefficient. When ΔH_fus is unavailable, it can be estimated from the melting point using the empirical relation ΔH_fus ≈ 0.025 T_m (kJ mol⁻¹).

In mixed‑solvent systems (e.g., water‑ethanol, water‑PEG), the GSE is extended by replacing log P_ow with the log P of the solute between the mixed solvent and a reference phase, or by using the Jouyban‑Acree model: log S_mix = x₁ log S₁ + x₂ log S₂ + Σ J_i x₁^i x₂n‑i, where x₁, x₂ are solvent mole fractions and J_i are interaction parameters fitted from a minimum of three solubility measurements. The Abraham LFER can also be adapted by determining system coefficients for each solvent mixture, which are often linearly interpolated between the pure‑solvent coefficients.

Validation on the FDA’s Biopharmaceutics Classification System (BCS) dataset shows that the GSE predicts log S_w within ±0.7 log units for 70 % of neutral drugs, while the Abraham LFER reduces the mean absolute error to ±0.4 log units. For ionizable compounds, incorporating the pH‑solubility profile (pH 1–7.5) and the intrinsic dissolution rate (IDR) measured by the rotating‑disk method provides a complete developability assessment. The combined GSE/LFER approach is now standard in early‑stage formulation screening and in the setting of dissolution specifications for generic drug approval.

Thermodynamic Consistency of Activity Coefficient Models: The Gibbs‑Duhem Integration Test

Any activity‑coefficient model (Wilson, NRTL, UNIQUAC, UNIFAC) used for vapor‑liquid‑equilibrium (VLE) calculations must satisfy the Gibbs‑Duhem equation at constant temperature and pressure: Σ x_i d ln γ_i = 0. For a binary system this reduces to x₁ d ln γ₁ + x₂ d ln γ₂ = 0, which can be integrated numerically along the composition axis to test the internal consistency of experimental γ‑data or of a fitted model. The Herington test evaluates the integral ∫₀¹ (ln γ₁ – ln γ₂) dx₁; a consistent dataset yields a value close to zero (typically < 0.01). The Van Ness test uses the area between the experimental ln γ₁ and ln γ₂ curves and the model‑predicted curves; the ratio of the two areas should be unity for a thermodynamically consistent model.

In practice, the test is performed by first smoothing the experimental activity coefficients with a low‑order polynomial or spline, then computing the derivative d ln γ_i/dx₁ analytically. The integral is evaluated using Simpson’s rule with at least 20 composition points. If the Herington integral exceeds 0.02, the data are flagged for possible systematic errors such as pressure‑transducer drift, impurity in the feed, or incorrect pure‑component vapor pressures. The Van Ness area ratio is more sensitive to local deviations and can pinpoint composition regions where the model fails (e.g., near azeotropes).

For multicomponent systems, the Gibbs‑Duhem equation becomes a set of (c – 1) independent relations. The consistency test is applied to each binary subsystem extracted from the multicomponent data, and a global test uses the vector form Σ x_i ∇ ln γ_i = 0. Modern process simulators embed an automatic consistency check during parameter regression: the objective function includes a penalty term proportional to the squared Herington integral, forcing the optimizer toward thermodynamically admissible parameters. This approach prevents the common pitfall of obtaining a good fit to VLE data while violating the Gibbs‑Duhem relation, which would lead to erroneous extrapolation to unmeasured compositions.

When using group‑contribution methods such as UNIFAC, the consistency test is applied to the predicted activity coefficients rather than to experimental data. Because UNIFAC parameters are derived from a large database of binary VLE, the Herington integral for most binaries is < 0.005. However, for highly asymmetric systems (e.g., water‑hydrocarbon, polymer‑solvent) the standard UNIFAC groups may not capture the strong non‑ideality, and the integral can exceed 0.05. In such cases, a modified UNIFAC version (e.g., UNIFAC‑Dortmund, UNIFAC‑IL) with temperature‑dependent group interaction parameters is employed, and the consistency test is repeated until the integral falls below the acceptance threshold.

A frequent examination trap is to assume that a low root‑mean‑square deviation (RMSD) in pressure or composition between model and experiment guarantees thermodynamic consistency. The correct statement is: a model can have a low RMSD yet violate the Gibbs‑Duhem equation, leading to incorrect predictions of derivative properties such as the excess enthalpy or the azeotropic composition shift with pressure. Therefore, any VLE modeling exercise must report both the RMSD and the Herington/Van Ness consistency metrics. The final deliverable in a design project includes a consistency‑test table for each binary pair, the fitted binary parameters, and a statement of the maximum Herington integral observed.

  • Assuming the Chrastil correlation constants are universal for a solute across all pressure‑temperature ranges.
  • Using a constant binary interaction parameter kᵢⱼ in the Peng‑Robinson EOS for water‑hydrocarbon systems at high pressure.
  • Confusing the HLD zero point with the cloud point of non‑ionic surfactants.
  • Applying the General Solubility Equation to ionizable drugs without the Henderson‑Hasselbalch correction.
  • Equating a low RMSD in VLE regression with thermodynamic consistency of the activity‑coefficient model.
  • Neglecting the temperature dependence of the surfactant characteristic curvature C_c in the HLD model.
  • Using the melting point of a metastable polymorph in the GSE and reporting the result as the equilibrium solubility.

These advanced topics extend the core solution thermodynamics into supercritical extraction, high‑pressure phase behavior, microemulsion formulation, pharmaceutical solubility prediction, and rigorous model validation, providing the quantitative tools needed for high‑level competitive examinations.

Thermodynamics of Ionic Liquid–Water Binary Mixtures: Activity Coefficients, Excess Enthalpy, and Liquid–Liquid Equilibrium

Ionic liquids (ILs) paired with water form a class of binary mixtures that display strong non‑ideality because of hydrogen‑bonding networks, ion‑dipole interactions, and the large size asymmetry between the bulky organic cation/anion and the small water molecule. The excess Gibbs energy GE of these systems is commonly correlated with the Non‑Random Two‑Liquid (NRTL) or the electrolyte‑NRTL (e‑NRTL) model, where the binary interaction parameters τij and non‑randomness factor αij are fitted to experimental vapor‑liquid equilibrium (VLE) or liquid‑liquid equilibrium (LLE) data. For many imidazolium‑based ILs (e.g., [C4mim][BF4], [C4mim][PF6]) the water‑rich phase shows a negative deviation from Raoult’s law at low water mole fractions (xw < 0.2) because water molecules are strongly solvated by the anion, while the IL‑rich phase exhibits a positive deviation at high xw due to disruption of the ionic network.

The excess enthalpy HE measured by isothermal titration calorimetry typically shows a pronounced exothermic minimum near xw ≈ 0.3, reflecting the formation of water‑anion hydrogen‑bonded clusters. This minimum coincides with the composition where the activity coefficient of water γw reaches its lowest value (often < 0.2). The temperature dependence of τij is captured by a linear function τij = aij + bij/T, allowing prediction of VLE at temperatures up to 450 K. When the IL is hydrophobic (e.g., [C8mim][NTf2]), a miscibility gap appears below a critical solution temperature (CST) of ~ 320 K; the binodal curve is accurately reproduced by the e‑NRTL model with a single set of parameters for both VLE and LLE.

Practical implications include the design of IL‑based extraction processes for water removal from organic streams and the prediction of water activity in IL electrolytes for electrochemical devices. A common examiner twist is to ask for the sign of HE at infinite dilution of water in a hydrophobic IL; the correct answer is positive because water‑water hydrogen bonds are broken without sufficient compensation from ion‑water interactions.

Gas Solubility in Deep Eutectic Solvents: Henry’s Law Constants, Temperature Dependence, and Correlation Methods

Deep eutectic solvents (DESs) formed from a hydrogen‑bond donor (HBD) such as urea or glycerol and a hydrogen‑bond acceptor (HBA) like choline chloride exhibit tunable polarity and negligible vapor pressure, making them attractive for gas capture. The solubility of gases (CO2, CH4, H2S, N2) in DESs is usually expressed through Henry’s law constant kH (Pa · m3 · mol−1) defined as fg = kH xg, where fg is the fugacity of the gas and xg its mole fraction in the liquid. Experimental kH values for CO2 in the choline chloride‑urea (1:2) DES at 298 K are around 1.2 × 106 Pa · m3 · mol−1, roughly an order of magnitude lower than in conventional amine solvents, indicating higher CO2 affinity.

The temperature dependence follows the van’t Hoff relation ln kH = A + B/T, where B = −ΔHsol/R. For CO2 in the same DES, ΔHsol ≈ −25 kJ mol−1, confirming exothermic absorption. Correlation of kH across DES families is achieved with the Abraham linear free‑energy relationship (LFER): log kH = c + e E + s S + a A + b B + v V, where E, S, A, B, V are solute descriptors. The LFER reduces the average absolute deviation to ±0.15 log units for a dataset of 30 DES‑gas pairs.

An alternative density‑based correlation is the Chrastil equation: ln xg = k ln ρ + a/T + b, where ρ is the DES density. Because DES density varies strongly with temperature and composition, the Chrastil model captures the combined effect of free‑volume and energetic interactions. In exam questions, students are often asked to convert a reported Henry’s constant in mol · kg−1 · bar−1 to the mole‑fraction basis; the conversion factor is the molar mass of the DES divided by 1000 kg · mol−1.

Worked Example: Henry’s Constant Conversion for a DES
A literature source gives the Henry’s constant for CO2 in choline chloride‑glycerol (1:2) DES at 298 K as 0.025 mol · kg−1 · bar−1. The DES molar mass is 210 g · mol−1. Calculate the Henry’s constant on a mole‑fraction basis (kH in Pa · m3 · mol−1).
1. Convert the given constant to SI: 0.025 mol · kg−1 · bar−1 = 0.025 mol · kg−1 · (105 Pa)−1 = 2.5 × 10−7 mol · kg−1 · Pa−1. 2. Relate mole fraction x to molality m: x ≈ m · MDES / 1000, where MDES = 0.210 kg · mol−1. 3. Henry’s law on mole‑fraction basis: f = kH x. Substituting x gives f = kH · m · MDES/1000. Equate to the molality form f = kH,molal · m. Hence kH = kH,molal · 1000 / MDES. 4. Insert numbers: kH = (2.5 × 10−7 mol · kg−1 · Pa−1) · 1000 / 0.210 = 1.19 × 10−3 Pa−1. 5. Invert to obtain the conventional Henry’s constant kH (Pa · m3 · mol−1) = 1 / (1.19 × 10−3) ≈ 8.4 × 102 Pa · m3 · mol−1. The result is consistent with reported values for CO2 in glycerol‑based DESs.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a problem supplies Henry’s constant in mixed units, always write the definition used by the source (molality, mole fraction, or concentration) before converting. Keep a conversion table in your notes: 1 bar = 105 Pa; 1 mol · kg−1 = Msolvent/1000 mol · mol−1. This prevents unit‑mismatch errors that cost easy marks.

Pressure Effects on Aqueous Electrolyte Activity Coefficients: Pitzer and e‑NRTL Comparisons

At pressures above 10 MPa the activity coefficients of ions in aqueous solutions deviate noticeably from their 0.1 MPa values because the dielectric constant of water decreases and ion‑pair formation is enhanced. The Pitzer model incorporates pressure through the virial coefficients β(0), β(1), and Cφ, each expressed as a function of temperature and pressure: β(P,T) = β0(T) + βP(T) · (P − 0.1 MPa). The pressure derivatives βP are obtained from high‑pressure osmotic coefficient measurements (e.g., NaCl up to 100 MPa at 323 K). For 1 mol · kg−1 NaCl at 323 K, increasing pressure from 0.1 to 50 MPa raises the mean activity coefficient γ± from 0.66 to 0.71, a 7 % change that is significant for geochemical modeling.

The electrolyte‑NRTL (e‑NRTL) model treats pressure implicitly via the temperature‑dependent binary parameters τij(T) and the Born term for the electrostatic contribution. The Born term includes the solvent dielectric constant ε(P,T), which is correlated with the Bradley‑Pitzer equation. When the same high‑pressure dataset is regressed with e‑NRTL, the average absolute deviation in ln γ± is 0.018 versus 0.012 for Pitzer, indicating that Pitzer’s explicit pressure virial terms capture the compression effect more parsimoniously for simple 1:1 electrolytes. However, for mixed‑electrolyte systems (e.g., NaCl‑MgCl2‑H2O) e‑NRTL’s multicomponent framework avoids the combinatorial explosion of Pitzer mixing parameters, making it the preferred choice in process simulators for pressures up to 30 MPa.

A typical examination question asks to estimate the change in the osmotic coefficient φ of a 2 mol · kg−1 Na2SO4 solution when pressure is raised from 1 to 20 MPa at 298 K. Using the Pitzer pressure derivative βP = −1.2 × 10−4 MPa−1 for the Na+‑SO42− pair, the calculated Δφ ≈ +0.004, a small but measurable shift that influences solubility predictions for sparingly soluble salts under reservoir conditions.

Crystal Growth Kinetics in Supersaturated Solutions: The Burton–Cabrera–Frank Model and Industrial Implications

The Burton–Cabrera–Frank (BCF) theory describes the advancement of a crystal surface by the lateral motion of steps generated at screw dislocations. The normal growth rate R (m · s−1) for a single screw dislocation is R = (Ω D σ / λ) · tanh(λ / 2xs), where Ω is the molecular volume, D the surface diffusion coefficient, σ the relative supersaturation (c − csat)/csat, λ the mean diffusion length of adatoms, and xs the step spacing. At low supersaturation (σ < 0.01) the tanh term ≈ λ/2xs and R ∝ σ2 (parabolic law). At high supersaturation the tanh saturates to 1 and R ∝ σ (linear law). The transition supersaturation σc ≈ 2xs/λ is typically 0.02–0.05 for inorganic salts such as KCl and (NH4)2SO4.

In industrial crystallizers, the overall growth rate is the sum over all active dislocations: Rtot = ρd R, where ρd is the dislocation density (cm−2). Typical values for well‑grown single crystals are 103–105 cm−2; for poorly seeded slurries ρd can exceed 107 cm−2, leading to excessive fines. The BCF model also predicts the effect of impurities that adsorb at step edges, reducing the step velocity by a factor (1 − θ), where θ is the surface coverage given by a Langmuir isotherm. This mechanism explains why ppm‑level additives (e.g., polyacrylic acid in CaCO3 crystallization) can change the habit from rhombohedral to scalenohedral.

Examiners often test the ability to distinguish between surface‑integration‑controlled growth (BCF) and mass‑transfer‑controlled growth. The latter follows R = kL(c − csat) with a linear dependence on supersaturation at all levels. A diagnostic plot of ln R versus ln σ yields a slope of 2 for BCF at low σ and 1 for mass‑transfer control; experimental data for K2SO4 in a mixed‑suspension‑mixed‑product‑removal (MSMPR) crystallizer show a slope of 1.8 at σ = 0.01, confirming BCF dominance.

Solid–Liquid Equilibria in Multicomponent Pharmaceutical Formulations: Combined van’t Hoff, Apelblat, and Jouyban–Acree Approaches

Predicting the equilibrium solubility of a drug in a multicomponent solvent mixture (e.g., water‑ethanol‑PEG 400) requires a model that captures both temperature and composition effects. The van’t Hoff equation ln x = −ΔHfus/R(1/T − 1/Tm) provides a baseline for pure solvents but neglects specific solute‑solvent interactions. The Apelblat equation extends this by adding empirical terms: ln x = A + B/T + C ln T, where A, B, C are fitted for each binary solvent system. For ternary mixtures, the Jouyban–Acree model combines the binary solubilities x1, x2, x3 with interaction parameters Jij: ln xmix = ∑wi ln xi + ∑wiwj Jij/T, where wi are mass fractions of the solvents.

A practical workflow for a formulation scientist: (1) measure solubility in each pure solvent at three temperatures (e.g., 298, 308, 318 K); (2) fit Apelblat parameters for each binary pair; (3) determine Jij from a minimum of three ternary compositions at a single temperature; (4) validate the model by predicting solubility at a fourth composition and temperature. For ibuprofen in water‑ethanol‑PEG 400, this protocol yields a root‑mean‑square error of 0.018 log x units across the 298–318 K range, which meets the ICH Q6A specification for dissolution profile prediction.

Common pitfalls include using the van’t Hoff equation beyond the temperature range where ΔHfus is constant (typically ±20 K around Tm) and ignoring the temperature dependence of Jij. In exam questions, candidates are asked to derive the expression for the partial molar enthalpy of solution from the Jouyban–Acree model: ΔHsol = −R ∂(ln xmix)/∂(1/T) = ∑wi ΔHsol,i + ∑wiwj Jij. This highlights the additive nature of enthalpic contributions and the role of cross‑interaction parameters.

  • Assuming that the Pitzer pressure derivatives are negligible for all electrolytes above 10 MPa.
  • Using a single Henry’s constant for a gas in a DES without accounting for temperature‑dependent density changes in the Chrastil correlation.
  • Applying the BCF parabolic growth law at supersaturations where the linear regime already dominates.
  • Fitting the Jouyban–Acree model with only binary data and omitting the ternary interaction parameters Jij.
  • Neglecting the Born term pressure dependence in e‑NRTL when modeling high‑pressure VLE of aqueous electrolytes.
  • Treating the excess enthalpy of an ionic‑liquid‑water mixture as composition‑independent.
  • Correct interpretation of model‑consistency metrics is essential; a low RMSD does not guarantee thermodynamic admissibility.

Thermodynamics of Microemulsions: Phase Behavior, Interfacial Tension, and Solubilization

Microemulsions are thermodynamically stable, isotropic dispersions of oil and water stabilized by a surfactant film, with domain sizes typically 10–100 nm. Unlike macroemulsions, which are kinetically metastable, microemulsions form spontaneously when the interfacial tension γ between oil and water is reduced to ultralow values (10−3–10−6 mN·m−1) by the adsorbed surfactant monolayer. The thermodynamic stability criterion is ΔGf = γΔA − TΔSconf < 0, where ΔA is the interfacial area created and ΔSconf is the configurational entropy of the dispersed droplets or bicontinuous domains. Three principal structural types exist: oil-in-water (O/W) droplets, water-in-oil (W/O) droplets, and bicontinuous structures where both phases percolate. The phase behavior is commonly mapped on a ternary phase diagram (oil–water–surfactant) at fixed temperature and pressure, showing single-phase microemulsion regions (often labeled L1, L2, or L3 for bicontinuous) separated from two-phase and three-phase regions by binodal curves. The Winsor classification describes four equilibrium types: Winsor I (O/W microemulsion + excess oil), Winsor II (W/O microemulsion + excess water), Winsor III (bicontinuous microemulsion + excess oil + excess water), and Winsor IV (single-phase microemulsion). The transition from Winsor I to III to II as surfactant hydrophile–lipophile balance (HLB) or salinity is varied corresponds to the spontaneous curvature H0 of the surfactant film passing through zero. The hydrophilic–lipophilic deviation (HLD) equation, HLD = ln S − k·EACN + f(A) + Cc·T + φ, quantifies this: HLD < 0 favors O/W, HLD > 0 favors W/O, and HLD ≈ 0 gives the bicontinuous Winsor III phase with minimum interfacial tension. Solubilization capacity (volume of oil or water solubilized per unit surfactant) peaks near HLD = 0 and scales with the surfactant packing parameter p = v/(a0lc), where v is tail volume, a0 headgroup area, and lc critical chain length. For p ≤ 1/3 spherical O/W droplets form; 1/3 < p ≤ 1/2 gives cylindrical/rodlike micelles; 1/2 < p ≤ 1 yields flexible bilayers/bicontinuous structures; p > 1 favors W/O reverse micelles. The bending elasticity of the surfactant film is described by the Helfrich free energy F = ∫[2κ(H − H0)2 + κ̄K]dA, where κ is the bending modulus, κ̄ the saddle-splay modulus, H the mean curvature, and K the Gaussian curvature. Low κ (∼kBT) permits thermal fluctuations that stabilize the bicontinuous phase. Microemulsions find application in enhanced oil recovery (EOR), where ultralow γ mobilizes trapped oil; in nanoparticle synthesis, where water pools act as nanoreactors; and in drug delivery, where the large interfacial area enhances solubilization of hydrophobic actives. The phase inversion temperature (PIT) method exploits the temperature dependence of nonionic surfactant HLB to produce fine emulsions by cooling through the PIT.

Solution Calorimetry: Experimental Determination of Enthalpies of Solution, Dilution, and Mixing

Solution calorimetry measures the heat effect q accompanying the dissolution of a solute in a solvent, the dilution of a solution, or the mixing of two liquids, providing direct access to enthalpy changes ΔHsol, ΔHdil, and ΔHmix. The fundamental relation is q = CcalΔT + qloss, where Ccal is the calorimeter heat capacity (determined by electrical calibration or a standard reaction such as TRIS/HCl), ΔT the corrected temperature rise, and qloss the heat leak correction obtained from the pre- and post-reaction temperature drift. Modern isothermal titration calorimeters (ITC) and heat-flow calorimeters (Tian–Calvet, Picker) achieve precision of ±0.1% in ΔH. For a solid solute, the integral enthalpy of solution at concentration m is ΔHsol(m) = [H(solution) − H(pure solid) − H(pure solvent)]/nsolute. The standard enthalpy of solution ΔHsol⦵ corresponds to the infinite-dilution limit (m → 0). The differential enthalpy of solution ΔHsol,diff = (∂H/∂n2)T,P,n1 is related to the integral quantity by ΔHsol,diff = ΔHsol,int + n2(∂ΔHsol,int/∂n2). For electrolytes, ΔHsol⦵ = ΔHlatt + ΔHhyd(cation) + ΔHhyd(anion), where ΔHlatt is the lattice enthalpy (endothermic) and ΔHhyd the hydration enthalpies (exothermic). The Born model estimates ΔHhyd ≈ −(NAz2e2/8πε0r)(1 − 1/εr), showing the strong dependence on ionic radius r and solvent dielectric constant εr. Enthalpy of dilution ΔHdil(m1 → m2) = ΔHsol(m2) − ΔHsol(m1) measures the heat released or absorbed when a solution of molality m1 is diluted to m2. For strong electrolytes, ΔHdil is exothermic at low concentrations (ion–solvent interactions dominate) and may become endothermic at high concentrations (ion–ion interactions). The enthalpy of mixing ΔHmix for binary liquids is measured by mixing known masses in the calorimeter; it is related to the excess molar enthalpy HE = ΔHmix by HE = x1x2∑kBk(x1 − x2)k (Redlich–Kister expansion). For aqueous electrolyte solutions, the Pitzer model expresses HE in terms of β(0), β(1), Cφ parameters and their temperature derivatives. Calorimetric data are essential for validating activity-coefficient models via the Gibbs–Helmholtz relation: (∂(GE/T)/∂T)P,x = −HE/T2. In pharmaceutical development, solution calorimetry of drug–cyclodextrin complexes yields binding enthalpies ΔHbind and stoichiometries via the Wiseman isotherm. For polymers, the enthalpy of mixing with solvents determines the Flory–Huggins χ parameter: χ = (ΔHmix − TΔSmix)/RTφ1φ2. Careful blank corrections (heat of stirring, ampoule breakage, vaporization) and buoyancy corrections for mass measurements are critical for high-accuracy work.

Worked Example: Enthalpy of Solution from Calorimetric Data
A solution calorimeter with heat capacity Ccal = 12.50 kJ·K−1 is charged with 100.0 g of water at 298.15 K. After adding 5.000 g of KCl (M = 74.55 g·mol−1), the temperature rises by 0.342 K. The heat leak correction is negligible. Calculate the integral enthalpy of solution ΔHsol,int in kJ·mol−1 at the resulting molality. Is the process endothermic or exothermic?
Heat released by dissolution: q = CcalΔT = (12.50 kJ·K−1) × (0.342 K) = 4.275 kJ. Since temperature rises, heat flows from the reaction to the calorimeter; the dissolution is exothermic, so qrxn = −4.275 kJ. Moles of KCl = 5.000 g / 74.55 g·mol−1 = 0.06707 mol. Integral enthalpy of solution ΔHsol,int = qrxn / nKCl = −4.275 kJ / 0.06707 mol = −63.7 kJ·mol−1. The negative sign confirms an exothermic process. The resulting molality m = 0.06707 mol / 0.1000 kg = 0.6707 mol·kg−1. This value differs from the infinite-dilution standard enthalpy ΔHsol⦵ (≈ +17.2 kJ·mol−1 for KCl) because at this concentration ion–ion interactions contribute significantly; the integral enthalpy becomes less exothermic (more positive) as m → 0.
Exam Strategy: Calorimetry Sign Conventions
Always define the system (the solution) and surroundings (calorimeter). A temperature rise in the calorimeter means heat left the system: qsys = −CcalΔT. Exothermic ΔH < 0, endothermic ΔH > 0. In multiple-choice questions, the sign is the most common trap. Write the sign convention explicitly in your working to avoid losing marks.

Molecular Weight Determination of Polymers by Membrane Osmometry and Static Light Scattering

Absolute molecular weight determination of polymers requires colligative or scattering methods that do not rely on calibration standards. Membrane osmometry measures the osmotic pressure Π of a polymer solution separated from pure solvent by a semipermeable membrane (typically regenerated cellulose with MWCO 10–50 kg·mol−1). The van't Hoff equation Π = (RT/Mn)c + A2c2 + A3c3 + … is extrapolated to c → 0 to obtain the number-average molar mass Mn = limc→0(RTc/Π). The second virial coefficient A2 (mol·mL·g−2) quantifies polymer–solvent interactions: A2 > 0 for good solvents, A2 = 0 at the theta temperature θ, A2 < 0 for poor solvents. Modern high-speed membrane osmometers (e.g., Knauer, Gonotec) operate at 30–60°C with pressure resolution 0.1 Pa, measuring Mn in the range 103–106 g·mol−1. Key corrections include: (1) membrane imperfection (solvent flux), corrected by measuring pure solvent flux; (2) hydrostatic head difference; (3) concentration polarization at the membrane surface, minimized by stirring. Static light scattering (SLS) measures the Rayleigh ratio Rθ = (Iθr2)/(I0V) at scattering angle θ, where Iθ is scattered intensity, r the sample–detector distance, I0 incident intensity, and V scattering volume. The Zimm equation Kc/Rθ = (1/Mw)P(θ)−1 + 2A2c + … with K = 4π2n02(dn/dc)2/(NAλ04) yields the weight-average molar mass Mw from the zero-angle, zero-concentration intercept. The form factor P(θ) = 1 − (16π2/3λ2)Rg2sin2(θ/2) + … gives the radius of gyration Rg from the initial slope of Kc/Rθ vs sin2(θ/2). For a random coil, Rg2 = (C∞nl2)/6, where C∞ is the characteristic ratio, n the number of bonds, l the bond length. The polydispersity index Đ = Mw/Mn characterizes molecular weight distribution: Đ = 1 for monodisperse, 1.5–2 for typical free-radical polymerization, >2 for step-growth or broad distributions. SLS requires accurate dn/dc (refractive index increment), measured by differential refractometry; typical values are 0.05–0.20 mL·g−1. Dust filtering (0.2 μm PTFE) and solvent clarification are critical because dust scatters intensely at low angles. For branched polymers, the branching factor g = Rg,branched2/Rg,linear2 (same Mw) is obtained from SLS combined with viscometry (intrinsic viscosity [η]). The Mark–Houwink–Sakurada equation [η] = KMwa relates [η] to Mw; the exponent a ranges from 0.5 (theta solvent) to 0.8 (good solvent) for linear chains, and is lower for branched structures. Combined SLS–viscometry–refractometry detectors in size-exclusion chromatography (SEC-MALS) provide complete molar mass distributions Mw(Ve), Rg(Ve), and [η](Ve) in a single run.

Ion Association in Electrolyte Solutions: Bjerrum Theory, Ion Pairs, and Triple Ions

In electrolyte solutions, the Debye–Hückel limiting law assumes complete dissociation, but at finite concentrations ions associate into pairs, triplets, and larger clusters, reducing the effective concentration of free ions. The Bjerrum theory defines an ion pair as two oppositely charged ions whose center-to-center distance r is less than a critical distance q = z+z−e2/(8πε0εrkBT), where the Coulombic attraction exceeds the thermal energy by a factor of 2. For 1:1 electrolytes in water at 298 K (εr = 78.5), q ≈ 0.36 nm; in methanol (εr = 32.6), q ≈ 0.86 nm; in low-dielectric solvents (εr < 10), q exceeds typical ion diameters and association is extensive. The association constant KA for the equilibrium Mz+ + Xz− ⇌ MX(z+−z−) is given by the Fuoss equation: KA = (4πNA/3000)∫aq r2exp(z+z−e2/4πε0εrkBTr) dr, where a is the distance of closest approach. For contact ion pairs (CIP), the ions are in direct contact (r = a); for solvent-separated ion pairs (SSIP), one or more solvent molecules intervene (r > a). The Bjerrum–Fuoss theory predicts KA ∝ εr−3/2exp(const/εr), showing the dramatic increase in association as εr decreases. Triple ions (M2X+ or MX2−) form via M+ + MX ⇌ M2X+ with equilibrium constant KT. In concentrated solutions, the mass-balance equations are: c = [M+] + [MX] + 2[M2X+] = [X−] + [MX] + 2[MX2−]. The conductivity Λ = F∑ziui[i] depends on the mobilities of free ions, ion pairs (often assumed immobile), and triple ions. The Fuoss–Kraus equation Λ = Λ0 − (A + BΛ0)√c + KAΛ0c + … separates the Debye–Hückel–Onsager relaxation/electrophoretic term from the association term. For 2:2 electrolytes (e.g., MgSO4), ion pairing is strong even in water (KA ≈ 200 M−1 at 298 K), and the limiting law fails above 10−3 M. The mean spherical approximation (MSA) and the Pitzer model incorporate association implicitly through the β(0), β(1), Cφ parameters. In mixed solvents, preferential solvation alters the local dielectric constant around ions, shifting KA. For example, in water–dioxane mixtures, MgSO4 association increases as dioxane fraction rises because water is preferentially bound to Mg2+, lowering the bulk εr. Ion association affects activity coefficients, solubility products (Ksp = aMaX = γMγX[M+][X−]), and electrode potentials. In geochemistry, ion pairing of Ca2+ with SO42− and CO32− controls calcite and gypsum saturation in seawater. In battery electrolytes, Li+–anion association reduces transference number and conductivity; designing anions with low association tendency (e.g., TFSI−, FSI−) is a key strategy.

Lower and Upper Critical Solution Temperatures in Polymer Solutions: LCST and UCST Phenomena

Binary polymer–solvent systems exhibit two distinct types of phase separation boundaries: upper critical solution temperature (UCST) and lower critical solution temperature (LCST). Below the UCST, the mixture splits into two phases; above it, the system is miscible in all proportions. Conversely, below the LCST the mixture is miscible, while above it phase separation occurs. Some systems (e.g., poly(N-isopropylacrylamide) (PNIPAM) in water) show both UCST and LCST, forming a closed-loop miscibility gap. The Flory–Huggins free energy of mixing per lattice site is ΔGmix/kBT = (φ/N)ln φ + (1−φ)ln(1−φ) + χφ(1−φ), where φ is the polymer volume fraction, N the degree of polymerization, and χ the interaction parameter. The spinodal curve is given by ∂2(ΔGmix/kBT)/∂φ2 = 0, yielding χsp = (1/2)(1/√N + 1)2 ≈ 1/2 + 1/√N for large N. The binodal (coexistence) curve is obtained from equality of chemical potentials: ln[φ/(1−φ)] + (1−1/N)φ + χ(1−2φ) = 0. The critical point occurs at φc = 1/(1+√N) and χc = (1/2)(1+1/√N)2. The temperature dependence of χ determines UCST vs LCST behavior. For UCST systems, χ = A + B/T with B > 0 (enthalpic dominance); χ decreases with T, so mixing is favored at high T. For LCST systems, χ = A + B/T + C ln T or χ = A + B/T + CT with C > 0 (entropic dominance); χ increases with T due to unfavorable conformational or hydrogen-bonding entropy changes. In PNIPAM/water, the LCST at 32°C arises from the temperature-dependent hydration of the isopropyl groups: at low T, water forms ordered clathrate-like structures around hydrophobic moieties (favorable enthalpy, unfavorable entropy); as T rises, this structured water is released, increasing entropy but exposing hydrophobic groups to each other, driving phase separation. The critical exponent β for the order parameter (φ1 − φ2) ∝ |T − Tc|β is β ≈ 0.325 (Ising universality class) for both UCST and LCST, but mean-field Flory–Huggins predicts β = 0.5. Near the critical point, concentration fluctuations cause critical opalescence (diverging correlation length ξ ∝ |T − Tc|−ν, ν ≈ 0.63). The two-phase region is characterized by tie-lines connecting coexisting compositions φ1 and φ2 at a given T; the lever rule gives phase volumes. For polydisperse polymers, fractionation occurs: the polymer-rich phase is enriched in high-M chains, the solvent-rich phase in low-M chains. The cloud-point curve (binodal) is measured by turbidimetry; the phase diagram is often plotted as T vs φ or T vs concentration c. In ternary polymer–solvent–nonsolvent systems (e.g., membrane casting), the binodal surface separates one-phase from two-phase regions; the casting solution trajectory crosses the binodal, inducing phase inversion. LCST behavior is exploited in smart hydrogels (PNIPAM) for drug delivery: below LCST the gel is swollen (hydrophilic), above LCST it collapses (hydrophobic), releasing payload. UCST behavior is seen in polystyrene/cyclohexane (UCST ≈ 35°C for high M) and in polyelectrolyte complexes where added salt screens charges and induces UCST-type phase separation.

  • Confusing the sign of ΔH in calorimetry: temperature rise of the calorimeter means the reaction is exothermic (ΔH < 0), not endothermic.
  • Using the integral enthalpy of solution at finite concentration as if it were the standard (infinite-dilution) value; the two differ significantly for electrolytes above 0.01 mol·kg−1.
  • Assuming membrane osmometry gives Mw; it yields Mn. Light scattering gives Mw. The polydispersity index Đ = Mw/Mn requires both measurements.
  • Neglecting the dn/dc correction in static light scattering; a 5% error in dn/dc propagates as a 10% error in Mw because K ∝ (dn/dc)2.
  • Applying the Debye–Hückel limiting law to 2:2 or 3:3 electrolytes at concentrations where ion pairing dominates (typically > 10−3 M for MgSO4).
  • Treating the Bjerrum critical distance q as a fixed ion-pair diameter; q depends on εr and T, and the actual association constant requires integration over the distribution of ion separations.
  • Assuming all polymer solutions show UCST; many water-soluble polymers (PNIPAM, PEO at high T, methylcellulose) exhibit LCST or closed-loop miscibility gaps due to hydrophobic hydration effects.

Thermodynamic Modeling of Multicomponent Electrolyte Solutions: The Pitzer–Simonson–Clegg Approach

The Pitzer–Simonson–Clegg (PSC) model extends the original Pitzer ion‑interaction framework to mixed‑solvent electrolyte systems by introducing solvent‑specific binary and ternary interaction parameters. In a system containing water, a co‑solvent (e.g., methanol), and an electrolyte MX, the excess Gibbs energy is expressed as a sum of long‑range Debye–Hückel terms, short‑range binary ion–ion parameters (β⁽⁰⁾, β⁽¹⁾, Cφ), and solvent‑dependent mixing parameters (θ, ψ) that capture ion–solvent and solvent–solvent interactions. The model requires experimental data such as osmotic coefficients, activity coefficients, and solubilities at several temperatures to regress the temperature‑dependent parameters. A key advantage of PSC over the simpler Bromley or SIT models is its ability to predict phase equilibria (solid–liquid, liquid–liquid, vapor–liquid) in ternary and quaternary mixtures without invoking arbitrary mixing rules. The temperature dependence of each parameter is usually represented by a polynomial in 1/T, allowing extrapolation to conditions relevant for industrial processes such as lithium‑ion battery electrolyte design or nuclear waste vitrification. Implementation in commercial simulators (e.g., OLI Systems, Aspen Plus) follows the standard Pitzer formalism but adds a solvent‑composition loop that updates the dielectric constant and the Debye–Hückel limiting slope at each iteration. Validation against the extensive UNIFAC‑Dortmund database shows average absolute deviations in water activity below 0.01 for most common salt–alcohol–water systems up to 6 mol kg⁻¹ ionic strength.

Phase Equilibria in Reactive Distillation: Reactive Azeotropes and the Concept of Reactive Residue Curve Maps

Reactive distillation combines chemical reaction and separation in a single column, and its feasibility hinges on the topology of reactive residue curve maps (RRCMs). For a reversible reaction A + B ⇌ C + D occurring in the liquid phase, the reactive azeotrope condition is defined by the simultaneous satisfaction of phase equilibrium (yᵢ = Kᵢxᵢ) and reaction equilibrium (K_eq = ∏aᵢ^νᵢ). When the reaction equilibrium constant is large, the reactive azeotrope may coincide with a conventional azeotrope of the product mixture, creating a reactive azeotropic node that can act as a distillation boundary. The RRCM is constructed by integrating the differential equations dξ/dt = r(ξ) and dxᵢ/dξ = (yᵢ − xᵢ)/∑(yⱼ − xⱼ) where ξ is the reaction extent. Singular points (nodes, saddles) on the map correspond to reactive azeotropes or pure‑component boiling points. The presence of a reactive saddle point indicates that a column can be designed to cross the boundary by adjusting the feed stage or reflux ratio. Practical design uses the concept of reactive distillation lines: a line connecting the feed composition to a stable node represents a feasible product composition. For the esterification of acetic acid with ethanol (A + B ⇌ C + D, water as by‑product), the RRCM shows a reactive azeotrope near the water–ethanol binary azeotrope; removal of water shifts the equilibrium and enables high‑purity ethyl acetate production. The method extends to multiple reactions by augmenting the stoichiometric matrix and solving the coupled equilibrium constraints at each integration step.

Worked Example: Identifying a Reactive Azeotrope
Consider the liquid‑phase reaction A + B ⇌ C + D at 1 atm with K_eq = 4.0 at the column temperature. The binary azeotrope between C and D occurs at x_C = 0.6, y_C = 0.6. The pure‑component vapor pressures are P_A^sat = 0.8 atm, P_B^sat = 0.5 atm, P_C^sat = 1.2 atm, P_D^sat = 0.9 atm. Assuming ideal vapor phase and Raoult’s law for the liquid phase, determine whether a reactive azeotrope exists at the binary azeotropic composition.
At the binary azeotrope, y_C = x_C = 0.6 and y_D = x_D = 0.4. For components A and B, assume they are absent (x_A = x_B = 0). The reaction quotient Q = (a_C a_D)/(a_A a_B). With ideal liquid, a_i = x_i. Since x_A = x_B = 0, Q → ∞, which cannot equal K_eq = 4. Therefore the binary azeotrope cannot satisfy reaction equilibrium. To find a reactive azeotrope, solve simultaneously: y_i = x_i P_i^sat/P_total and K_eq = (x_C x_D)/(x_A x_B). With four unknowns (x_A, x_B, x_C, x_D) and three equations (sum x_i = 1, two independent equilibrium relations), a one‑parameter family of solutions exists. Numerical solution yields a reactive azeotrope at approximately x_A = 0.15, x_B = 0.10, x_C = 0.45, x_D = 0.30, confirming that a reactive azeotrope distinct from the binary azeotrope is present.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the existence of a reactive azeotrope, first write the reaction equilibrium expression in terms of liquid‑phase mole fractions. Then impose the azeotropic condition y_i = x_i. If the resulting algebraic system has a physically admissible solution (all x_i > 0, Σx_i = 1), a reactive azeotrope exists. Sketching a simple RRCM with nodes and saddles earns partial credit even if the full integration is not required.

Solubility of Solids in Supercritical CO₂ with Cosolvents: The Méndez‑Santiago–Teja Correlation and Density‑Based Extensions

The Méndez‑Santiago–Teja (MST) correlation provides a semi‑empirical relationship between the solubility of a solid solute in a supercritical fluid and the fluid density: ln x₂ = a + b ln ρ + c/T, where x₂ is the mole‑fraction solubility, ρ the supercritical fluid density, T the absolute temperature, and a, b, c are solute‑specific constants. For pure CO₂, the correlation reproduces experimental data for a wide range of pharmaceuticals and polymers with average absolute relative deviations below 5 %. When a cosolvent (entrainer) such as ethanol is added, the density of the mixture changes and specific solute–cosolvent interactions (hydrogen bonding, dipole–dipole) become significant. The extended MST model introduces a cosolvent composition term: ln x₂ = a + b ln ρ + c/T + d y_cosolvent + e y_cosolvent², where y_cosolvent is the mole fraction of cosolvent in the supercritical phase. The parameters d and e capture the enhancement (or inhibition) due to cosolvent clustering around the solute. An alternative density‑based approach uses the Chrastil equation, ln x₂ = k ln ρ + m/T + n, but the MST form is preferred when high‑pressure PVT data for the mixture are available because it directly incorporates the measured mixture density. For process design, the solubility isotherms are combined with the Peng–Robinson equation of state (with Wong–Sandler mixing rules) to predict the phase envelope and to size the extractor. A practical limitation is that the correlation constants must be regressed from experimental data at each temperature–pressure–cosolvent condition; extrapolation beyond the regression range can lead to errors exceeding 30 %.

Thermodynamics of Mixed Micelle Formation: Regular Solution Theory and the Interaction Parameter β

In mixed surfactant systems, the standard free energy of micellization for a binary mixture of surfactants 1 and 2 is described by the regular solution theory (RST) adapted for micelles. The micellar mole fractions X₁ and X₂ (X₁ + X₂ = 1) relate to the bulk mole fractions α₁ and α₂ through the Clint equation for ideal mixing: 1/CMC_mix = α₁/CMC₁ + α₂/CMC₂. Non‑ideality is introduced via the interaction parameter β, which modifies the chemical potential of each surfactant in the micelle: ln(CMC_mix X₁/α₁ CMC₁) = β X₂² and ln(CMC_mix X₂/α₂ CMC₂) = β X₁². A negative β indicates synergistic mixing (lower CMC than ideal), while a positive β signals antagonism. The parameter β can be obtained experimentally by measuring CMC_mix at several α₁ values and solving the coupled equations iteratively. For ionic/non‑ionic mixtures, the electrostatic contribution to β is often separated using the Rubingh model, which adds a term accounting for counterion binding. The theory predicts the composition of the mixed micelle (X₁) and the activity coefficients of the surfactants in the micellar pseudophase (f₁ = exp(β X₂²), f₂ = exp(β X₁²)). These activity coefficients are essential for calculating solubilization capacities and partition coefficients of drugs in mixed micellar formulations. Limitations of RST include the assumption of a single, composition‑independent β and the neglect of micellar size and shape changes; more advanced models (e.g., the Motomura approach) treat β as a function of aggregation number.

Phase Equilibria in Ternary Systems Exhibiting Liquid–Liquid–Vapor Equilibrium: Three‑Phase Flash Calculations and the Gibbs Phase Rule

When a ternary mixture splits into two liquid phases (L₁, L₂) and a vapor phase (V) at a given temperature and pressure, the Gibbs phase rule gives F = C − P + 2 = 3 − 3 + 2 = 2 degrees of freedom. Fixing T and P therefore determines the compositions of all three phases uniquely. The three‑phase flash calculation solves the material balances zᵢ = β_V yᵢ + β_L₁ xᵢ¹ + β_L₂ xᵢ² (with β_V + β_L₁ + β_L₂ = 1) together with the equilibrium relations yᵢ = Kᵢ¹ xᵢ¹ = Kᵢ² xᵢ², where Kᵢ¹ and Kᵢ² are the vapor–liquid equilibrium ratios for each liquid phase. The K‑values are obtained from an activity‑coefficient model (e.g., NRTL or UNIQUAC) for the liquid phases and an equation of state for the vapor. A robust algorithm uses a nested loop: an outer loop adjusts the phase fractions β to satisfy the material balances, while an inner loop solves the LLE equilibrium (equality of component fugacities in L₁ and L₂) for a given set of β. The LLE sub‑problem is itself a two‑phase flash with the constraint that the vapor phase is absent. Convergence is accelerated by using the tangent‑plane distance criterion to initialize the liquid‑phase compositions. In practice, the three‑phase region appears as a three‑phase triangle on the ternary diagram at constant T and P; the vertices of the triangle are the equilibrium compositions of V, L₁, and L₂. The tie‑lines connecting L₁ and L₂ lie inside the binodal curve, and the vapor composition is the same for both liquid phases. This framework is essential for designing decanters in heterogeneous azeotropic distillation (e.g., the water–ethanol–benzene system) where the decanter operates at the three‑phase equilibrium to separate the aqueous and organic layers while recovering the entrainer in the vapor.

  • Assuming the Pitzer–Simonson–Clegg model reduces to the simple Pitzer model when the co‑solvent mole fraction is zero; the solvent‑dependent parameters do not vanish automatically and must be regressed separately.
  • Confusing a reactive azeotrope with a conventional azeotrope; the former satisfies both phase and reaction equilibrium simultaneously, which shifts its composition with temperature and pressure.
  • Using the Méndez‑Santiago–Teja correlation outside the density range of the regression data; the linear ln ρ term can produce unphysical negative solubilities at low densities.
  • Treating the mixed‑micelle interaction parameter β as constant for all surfactant ratios; β often varies with composition, especially near the critical micelle concentration.
  • Neglecting the equality of vapor composition across both liquid phases in a three‑phase flash; this condition is the defining feature of LLVE and must be enforced in the equilibrium relations.
  • Applying the Gibbs phase rule without accounting for the reaction constraint in reactive distillation; each independent reaction reduces the degrees of freedom by one.

Thermodynamics of Ionic Liquid–Water Mixtures: Activity Coefficients and Phase Behavior

Ionic liquids (ILs) are salts that remain liquid below 100 °C and exhibit negligible vapor pressure. Their miscibility with water spans the full composition range for many hydrophilic ILs (e.g., 1‑ethyl‑3‑methylimidazolium acetate) while hydrophobic ILs (e.g., 1‑butyl‑3‑methylimidazolium hexafluorophosphate) show limited solubility and often form two liquid phases. The thermodynamic description of IL–water mixtures requires activity‑coefficient models that capture strong Coulombic interactions, hydrogen‑bonding networks, and the large size asymmetry between ions and water molecules.

Activity‑Coefficient Models

The electrolyte NRTL (eNRTL) model extends the classical NRTL framework by adding a long‑range Debye–Hückel term for ionic interactions and a short‑range binary interaction parameter for each ion–water pair. The UNIQUAC model, when combined with a Born‑type electrostatic term, provides a lattice‑based description that accounts for the different molecular volumes of cations, anions, and water. COSMO‑RS (Conductor‑like Screening Model for Real Solvents) predicts activity coefficients from quantum‑chemical σ‑profiles of the isolated ions and water, offering a fully predictive route without fitted binary parameters. In practice, eNRTL and UNIQUAC are preferred for process simulation because they integrate readily with existing VLE/LLE flash algorithms, while COSMO‑RS is valuable for screening new ILs.

Phase‑Behavior Features

Many IL–water systems display a lower critical solution temperature (LCST) or an upper critical solution temperature (UCST). For imidazolium‑based ILs with short alkyl chains, the LCST appears near 300 K; increasing the alkyl chain length shifts the LCST to higher temperatures and can introduce an UCST at elevated temperatures. The critical composition is typically water‑rich (xwater > 0.8) because the ionic network is disrupted by excess water. The binodal curve is asymmetric, and the tie‑line slope in the two‑phase region provides direct access to the activity‑coefficient ratio γIL/γwater via the equality of component fugacities.

Thermodynamic Consistency and Parameter Estimation

Experimental LLE data (cloud‑point measurements, mutual solubility) are regressed to obtain binary interaction parameters. The Gibbs–Duhem equation in its integral form serves as a consistency check: ∫(x1 dln γ1 + x2 dln γ2) = 0 over the two‑phase region. A common pitfall is to fit only the water‑rich branch, which yields parameters that mispredict the IL‑rich branch. Simultaneous regression of both branches, together with VLE data for the water‑rich side (where the IL vapor pressure is negligible), ensures thermodynamically consistent parameters.

Applications

Accurate activity coefficients enable the design of IL‑based extraction processes (e.g., aromatic/aliphatic separation), the prediction of water activity in IL electrolytes for batteries, and the modeling of IL‑water‑gas systems for CO₂ capture. In each case, the model must reproduce the strong non‑ideality at low water content where ion pairing and clustering dominate.

Pressure Effect on Azeotropic Composition in Binary and Ternary Systems

An azeotrope occurs when the liquid and vapor phases have identical composition at a given temperature and pressure. The position of the azeotropic point shifts with pressure because the equality of component fugacities, yi P φiV = xi γi Pisat φisat exp[∫viL dP/RT], depends on pressure through the vapor‑phase fugacity coefficients φiV, the Poynting correction, and the pressure dependence of the saturation pressures. For minimum‑boiling azeotropes (e.g., ethanol–water), increasing pressure generally moves the azeotropic composition toward the more volatile component (ethanol) and raises the azeotropic temperature. For maximum‑boiling azeotropes (e.g., acetone–chloroform), the shift is toward the less volatile component.

Clapeyron‑Type Approximation

A convenient estimate of the composition shift uses the Clapeyron equation applied to the azeotropic line in the P–T plane: dP/dT = ΔHaz/(T ΔVaz), where ΔHaz and ΔVaz are the enthalpy and volume changes on vaporization at the azeotropic composition. Combining this with the Gibbs–Duhem relation for the liquid phase yields an expression for dxaz/dP that involves the difference in partial molar volumes of the components in the liquid. In practice, the shift is often correlated empirically: ln(xaz/xaz,ref) = k (P − Pref), where k is a system‑specific constant obtained from experimental data at two pressures.

Ternary Azeotropes and Pressure‑Swing Distillation

In ternary systems (e.g., water–ethanol–benzene), a heterogeneous azeotrope splits into two liquid phases of different compositions that share a common vapor composition. The three‑phase equilibrium (LLVE) is highly pressure sensitive; a modest pressure change can alter the relative amounts of the two liquid phases and the vapor composition, thereby shifting the overall distillation boundary. Pressure‑swing distillation exploits this by operating two columns at different pressures so that the azeotropic composition in one column lies on the opposite side of the feed composition relative to the other column, enabling complete separation without an entrainer.

Modeling Requirements

Accurate prediction demands an equation of state (e.g., Peng–Robinson with Wong–Sandler mixing rules) or a γ‑φ approach with a reliable activity‑coefficient model (NRTL, UNIQUAC) for the liquid phase and a cubic EOS for the vapor phase. The model must reproduce the experimental azeotropic composition at a reference pressure; otherwise, the predicted pressure derivative will be unreliable. Sensitivity analysis shows that the binary interaction parameters for the most non‑ideal pair dominate the pressure‑shift prediction.

Worked Example: Pressure Shift of the Ethanol–Water Azeotrope
At 1 atm the ethanol–water system forms a minimum‑boiling azeotrope at 89.4 mol % ethanol and 78.2 °C. Experimental data at 0.5 atm give an azeotropic composition of 92.1 mol % ethanol. Estimate the constant k in the empirical correlation ln(xaz/xaz,ref) = k (P − Pref) and predict the azeotropic composition at 2 atm.
Take the reference state at 1 atm: xaz,ref = 0.894. At 0.5 atm, xaz = 0.921. Compute k = ln(0.921/0.894) / (0.5 − 1) = ln(1.0302) / (−0.5) = 0.0298 / (−0.5) = −0.0596 atm⁻¹. For P = 2 atm, ln(xaz/0.894) = −0.0596 × (2 − 1) = −0.0596 → xaz = 0.894 × exp(−0.0596) = 0.894 × 0.942 = 0.842. The predicted azeotropic composition at 2 atm is 84.2 mol % ethanol.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the effect of pressure on an azeotrope, first identify whether it is minimum‑ or maximum‑boiling. Remember that for minimum‑boiling azeotropes the azeotropic composition moves toward the more volatile component as pressure increases; for maximum‑boiling it moves toward the less volatile component. If numerical data at two pressures are given, use the empirical ln‑ratio correlation to find k and then extrapolate. Always check that the predicted composition stays between 0 and 1.

Classical and Non‑Classical Nucleation Theory Applied to Supersaturated Solutions

Nucleation is the stochastic formation of a stable crystalline nucleus from a supersaturated solution.
