Classical nucleation theory (CNT) treats the nucleus as a spherical cluster of the bulk solid phase with a sharp interface, characterized by a surface tension γ that is assumed independent of size. The Gibbs free energy to form a cluster of radius r is ΔG(r) = −(4/3)πr³Δμ/vm + 4πr²γ, where Δμ = kT ln S is the chemical‑potential difference (S = supersaturation ratio) and vm is the molecular volume in the solid. The critical radius r* = 2γvm/Δμ corresponds to the maximum of ΔG, and the nucleation rate J = A exp(−ΔG*/kT) with ΔG* = (16πγ³vm²)/(3Δμ²). The pre‑exponential factor A contains the Zeldovich factor, the attachment frequency, and the number density of nucleation sites.

Limitations of CNT

CNT often overestimates the nucleation barrier because it neglects (i) the curvature dependence of γ (Tolman length), (ii) the non‑spherical shape of sub‑critical clusters, (iii) the presence of pre‑nucleation clusters (PNCs) that are stable, dense liquid‑like precursors, and (iv) the role of impurities or heterogeneous surfaces that lower the effective interfacial energy. Experimental nucleation rates for many salts (e.g., NaCl, KCl) exceed CNT predictions by several orders of magnitude at moderate supersaturations.

Two‑Step Nucleation and Non‑Classical Pathways

In the two‑step mechanism, a metastable dense liquid phase (or amorphous precursor) forms first via a low‑barrier spinodal‑like decomposition, followed by ordering into the crystal within the dense droplets. This pathway is supported by in‑situ SAXS, cryo‑TEM, and molecular dynamics simulations for systems such as calcium carbonate, glycine, and proteins. The free‑energy landscape then features two barriers: one for the formation of the dense precursor and a second for the solid‑state ordering inside it. The overall rate is governed by the higher of the two barriers.

Quantitative Modeling

Modern density‑functional theory (DFT) of nucleation replaces the sharp‑interface assumption with a continuous density profile ρ(r) and computes the grand potential Ω[ρ] = F[ρ] − μ∫ρ(r)dr. The equilibrium profile minimizes Ω, yielding a size‑dependent surface tension and a more accurate ΔG*. For electrolyte solutions, the electrostatic contribution to the interfacial free energy is incorporated via a Poisson–Boltzmann term. These approaches bridge CNT and non‑classical pictures and can be parameterized from molecular simulations.

Practical Implications

In industrial crystallization, controlling the nucleation pathway determines crystal size distribution, polymorphism, and purity. Additives that stabilize PNCs (e.g., polymers, specific ions) can promote the two‑step route, leading to smaller, more uniform crystals. Conversely, seeding bypasses primary nucleation altogether, making the process insensitive to the exact nucleation mechanism.

Thermodynamic Modeling of Electrolyte Solutions with the ePC‑SAFT Equation of State

The electrolyte Perturbed‑Chain Statistical Associating Fluid Theory (ePC‑SAFT) extends the molecular‑based PC‑SAFT EOS by adding a Debye–Hückel term for long‑range ionic interactions and an association scheme for ion pairing and solvation. Each ion is represented as a chain of spherical segments characterized by segment number m, segment diameter σ, and dispersion energy ε/k. The association sites on cations and anions capture specific short‑range forces such as hydrogen bonding between water and ions, and the formation of contact ion pairs (e.g., Na⁺–Cl⁻). The Helmholtz free energy is written as A = Aideal + Ahc + Adisp + Aassoc + ADH, where the Debye–Hückel contribution ADH depends on the ionic strength and the dielectric constant of the medium, which itself is computed from the PC‑SAFT density and composition.

Parameter Estimation

Pure‑component parameters for ions are obtained by regressing experimental density, vapor pressure (for volatile salts), and mean ionic activity coefficient data for a reference electrolyte (usually NaCl) at infinite dilution. The association energy and volume for ion–water and ion–ion pairs are fitted to osmotic coefficient and activity coefficient data over a range of concentrations (0.1–6 mol kg⁻¹). Cross‑association between different ions in mixed‑electrolyte solutions is usually assumed to follow combining rules, but binary interaction parameters kij in the dispersion term may be introduced for highly asymmetric mixtures (e.g., Mg²⁺/Na⁺).

Applications to Phase Equilibria

ePC‑SAFT predicts vapor–liquid equilibria (VLE) for electrolyte–solvent systems (e.g., CO₂–NaCl–H₂O) without the need for separate Henry’s law constants, because the gas solubility emerges from the equality of fugacities. It also handles liquid–liquid equilibria (LLE) in aqueous two‑phase systems (e.g., PEG–salt) and solid–liquid equilibria (SLE) for salt solubility, including the effect of temperature and pressure on the solubility product. The model reproduces the salting‑out of gases (Setchenov behavior) and the salting‑in of hydrophobic organics at high salt concentrations.

Comparison with Pitzer and eNRTL

Unlike the Pitzer model, which is limited to moderate ionic strengths and requires virial coefficients for each ion pair, ePC‑SAFT remains accurate up to saturation because the hard‑chain reference captures excluded‑volume effects. Compared with eNRTL, ePC‑SAFT offers a unified framework for VLE, LLE, and SLE, and its parameters have a clearer molecular interpretation. However, ePC‑SAFT demands more computational effort and a larger parameter set, which can be a drawback for rapid process‑design calculations.

  • Assuming the Debye–Hückel term in ePC‑SAFT is valid at all concentrations; it breaks down near saturation where ion‑pair association dominates.
  • Neglecting the composition dependence of the dielectric constant when using a constant‑ε Debye–Hückel correction.
  • Treating the azeotropic pressure shift as linear over wide pressure ranges; the Clapeyron slope itself varies with composition.
  • Applying classical nucleation theory to systems known to exhibit dense liquid precursors without checking for two‑step signatures.
  • Using a single interfacial tension value for all nucleus sizes in CNT; the Tolman correction can change the barrier by 20–30 % for nanometric nuclei.
  • Fitting IL–water binary parameters only to the water‑rich branch of the binodal curve, leading to large errors in the IL‑rich phase.
  • Ignoring the equality of vapor composition across both liquid phases in a three‑phase flash; this condition is the defining feature of LLVE and must be enforced.

Thermodynamic Modeling of Salt Precipitation in Evaporative Crystallization: The Mixed‑Solvent Electrolyte (MSE) Model

The Mixed‑Solvent Electrolyte (MSE) model extends the classical Pitzer framework to multicomponent, mixed‑solvent systems by coupling a long‑range Debye–Hückel term with a short‑range virial expansion that explicitly accounts for solvent–solvent, solvent–ion, and ion–ion interactions. The Helmholtz free energy is partitioned as A = Aideal + ADH + Avirial + Aassoc, where the association term captures contact ion‑pair formation and solvent‑mediated complexes such as MgSO₄·H₂O. Binary interaction parameters β⁽⁰⁾, β⁽¹⁾, and Cφ are regressed against osmotic coefficient, mean activity coefficient, and solid‑liquid equilibrium data for each electrolyte in each solvent mixture. Because the model treats the dielectric constant of the mixed solvent as a composition‑dependent property (often via the Onsager or Kirkwood–Fröhlich equation), it predicts the shift in solubility product Ksp with solvent composition without empirical Setchenov constants.

In evaporative crystallization the MSE model is coupled to a mass‑balance solver that tracks water removal, temperature change, and the evolving liquid composition. The precipitation condition for a salt Mν+Xν‑ is μM + ν+ μX = μsolid, where the chemical potentials are obtained from the MSE activity coefficients. The model correctly reproduces the “salting‑out” of NaCl in ethanol‑water mixtures and the “salting‑in” of KNO₃ in acetone‑water at high organic fractions, phenomena that single‑solvent Pitzer cannot capture. A key practical output is the metastable zone width, derived from the supersaturation ratio S = aM aX / Ksp at the onset of nucleation; MSE‑based predictions agree with experimental induction times within ±15 % for systems such as Na₂SO₄–H₂O–MeOH.

Examiners often test the distinction between the MSE virial coefficients and the Pitzer β parameters. Remember that MSE β⁽⁰⁾ and β⁽¹⁾ are defined on a molality basis for each solvent component, whereas Pitzer β’s are defined for a single solvent. Also, the association term in MSE introduces an equilibrium constant Kassoc for ion‑pair formation; neglecting it leads to systematic under‑prediction of solubility for divalent salts (e.g., CaSO₄) in low‑dielectric media. When a question asks for the effect of adding a co‑solvent on the solubility of a sparingly soluble salt, the correct answer must reference the composition‑dependent dielectric constant and the resulting change in the Debye–Hückel term, not merely a qualitative “solubility decreases”.

Phase Equilibria in Water‑Salt‑Organic Ternary Systems: The UNIQUAC‑Dortmund Extension

The UNIQUAC‑Dortmund model augments the original UNIQUAC combinatorial and residual terms with a temperature‑dependent binary interaction parameter τij = aij + bij/T + cij ln T, allowing accurate representation of liquid‑liquid equilibrium (LLE) in ternary water‑salt‑organic systems such as H₂O–NaCl–ethanol. The combinatorial part uses volume (r) and surface area (q) parameters for each component, including the electrolyte treated as a pseudo‑component with effective r and q derived from ionic radii and hydration numbers. The residual part incorporates an electrostatic contribution via the Debye–Hückel limiting law, ensuring that the activity coefficient of the salt approaches the Pitzer limit at infinite dilution.

Parameter estimation proceeds by simultaneous regression of LLE tie‑line data (compositions of the aqueous and organic phases) and vapor‑liquid equilibrium (VLE) data for the binary subsystems. The Dortmund group provides a public database of τij for over 200 ion–organic pairs, enabling predictive calculations for systems lacking experimental tie‑lines. A typical application is the design of extractive crystallization, where an organic antisolvent (e.g., isopropanol) is added to a saturated brine to precipitate NaCl while minimizing co‑precipitation of KCl. The model predicts the binodal curve, the plait point, and the distribution coefficient D = xNaClorg / xNaClaq as functions of temperature and overall composition.

Common examination pitfalls include confusing the UNIQUAC‑Dortmund τ parameters with the original UNIQUAC uij – uji differences, and forgetting that the combinatorial term requires the electrolyte’s effective r and q to be calculated from the ionic volumes (e.g., rNa⁺ = 0.92, qNa⁺ = 1.10) plus a hydration correction (typically 4–6 water molecules per ion). Another trap is assuming the model can be applied to solid‑liquid equilibrium without adding a separate SLE module; UNIQUAC‑Dortmund only yields liquid‑phase activities, so the solid‑phase fugacity must be supplied from an independent thermodynamic database (e.g., the NIST Chemistry WebBook).

Examiner's trap
A ternary mixture of water (1), NaCl (2), and ethanol (3) at 298 K forms two liquid phases. The overall composition is z₁ = 0.70, z₂ = 0.10, z₃ = 0.20 (mole fractions). Using UNIQUAC‑Dortmund, the calculated tie‑line endpoints are (x₁ᵃ, x₂ᵃ, x₃ᵃ) = (0.85, 0.05, 0.10) for the aqueous phase and (x₁ᵇ, x₂ᵇ, x₃ᵇ) = (0.30, 0.01, 0.69) for the organic phase. Determine the phase split (fraction of total moles in the aqueous phase) and verify the lever rule.
Let β be the mole fraction of the overall mixture that resides in the aqueous phase. Mass balance for component 1: z₁ = β x₁ᵃ + (1‑β) x₁ᵇ → 0.70 = β·0.85 + (1‑β)·0.30. Solving gives β = (0.70‑0.30)/(0.85‑0.30) = 0.40/0.55 ≈ 0.727. Check component 2: β·0.05 + (1‑β)·0.01 = 0.727·0.05 + 0.273·0.01 = 0.0364 + 0.0027 = 0.0391 ≈ 0.10? The discrepancy indicates the given tie‑line is inconsistent with the overall composition; a correct tie‑line must satisfy all three component balances simultaneously. In an exam, you would flag the inconsistency and state that the provided tie‑line cannot correspond to the stated overall composition.
Exam‑day strategy
When a ternary LLE problem appears, first write the three component mass‑balance equations and the lever‑rule expression β = (zᵢ‑xᵢᵇ)/(xᵢᵃ‑xᵢᵇ) for any component i. Use the component with the largest composition difference between phases to minimise rounding error. If the calculated β differs for the three components, the data set is inconsistent – state this clearly; examiners award marks for recognising thermodynamic inconsistency.

Solubility of Gases in Ionic Liquids at High Pressure: The Peng‑Robinson EOS with Wong‑Sandler Mixing Rules

The Peng‑Robinson equation of state (PR‑EOS) combined with Wong‑Sandler (WS) mixing rules provides a unified framework for vapor‑liquid equilibrium of gas‑ionic liquid (IL) systems up to 100 MPa. The WS rules enforce the exact low‑pressure limit of the excess Gibbs energy by linking the EOS mixing parameters to an activity‑coefficient model (commonly NRTL or UNIQUAC) through the relation: amix = Σᵢ Σⱼ xᵢ xⱼ aij (1‑kij) and bmix = Σᵢ xᵢ bᵢ, where the binary interaction parameter kij is temperature‑dependent: kij = k₀ + k₁/T. The IL is treated as a single pseudo‑component with critical properties estimated by the Valderrama group‑contribution method; the gas (e.g., CO₂, CH₄, H₂S) uses literature critical constants.

At high pressure the solubility is governed by the equality of fugacities: φgas⁽ᵛ⁾ ygas P = φgas⁽ˡ⁾ xgas P. The liquid‑phase fugacity coefficient φgas⁽ˡ⁾ is obtained from the PR‑EOS with the WS‑derived amix and bmix. Because ILs have negligible vapor pressure, the vapor phase is essentially pure gas, simplifying the calculation. The model reproduces the characteristic “solubility maximum” of CO₂ in [bmim][PF₆] near 12 MPa at 313 K, arising from the competition between gas‑IL attractive forces (captured by aij) and free‑volume reduction (captured by bmix).

Key examiner traps: (1) Assuming the PR‑EOS alone (with classical van der Waals mixing rules) is sufficient; it fails to predict the correct Henry’s constant at infinite dilution because it does not enforce the Gibbs‑Duhem consistency of the activity‑coefficient model. (2) Ignoring the temperature dependence of kij; a constant kij yields errors >30 % in solubility at 350 K. (3) Treating the IL as a volatile component with a non‑zero vapor pressure; this introduces a fictitious second vapor phase and violates the assumption of a single‑component vapor. When asked to estimate the Henry’s constant from the EOS, use the infinite‑dilution limit: H = limx→0 (φgas⁽ˡ⁾ P / xgas) = φgas⁽ˡ,∞⁾ P, where φgas⁽ˡ,∞⁾ is evaluated with the WS mixing rules at xgas → 0.

Thermodynamics of Polymer‑Salt Complexation and Its Impact on Solution Viscosity

Polymer‑salt complexation arises when multivalent cations (e.g., Ca²⁺, Al³⁺) bind to anionic repeat units of polyelectrolytes such as poly(acrylic acid) or to neutral polymers bearing donor groups (e.g., poly(ethylene oxide) with Li⁺). The binding equilibrium is described by a stepwise formation constant Kn for the n‑th ion‑polymer association: P + n Mz+ ⇌ P·Mn. The overall degree of complexation θ = Σ n·[P·Mn] / CP (CP = total polymer concentration) modifies both the effective charge density and the hydrodynamic volume of the polymer coil. In the semi‑dilute regime, the specific viscosity ηsp follows the scaling law ηsp ∝ (CP·(1+αθ))ν, where α accounts for the increase in chain stiffness per bound ion and ν ≈ 0.7–0.8 for good solvents.

The thermodynamic model couples the binding isotherm (often a Langmuir‑type or cooperative Hill equation) with the Flory‑Huggins free energy of mixing, adding an electrostatic contribution from the bound ions treated as a Donnan phase. The chemical potential of the free salt μsalt = μ⁰ + RT ln asalt + zFψD includes the Donnan potential ψD, which itself depends on θ. This feedback loop can produce a sharp viscosity increase (the “gel point”) at a critical salt concentration, observed experimentally for PAA‑Ca²⁺ systems at Ca²⁺/COOH ≈ 0.3. The model predicts that adding a monovalent salt (NaCl) screens the electrostatic attraction, shifting the gel point to higher divalent‑cation concentrations – a classic exam scenario.

Typical pitfalls: (i) Using the simple Debye‑Hückel limiting law for the activity coefficient of the free salt inside the polymer‑rich phase; the high ionic strength and specific binding require the Pitzer or MSE model. (ii) Assuming the polymer’s intrinsic viscosity [η] is constant; complexation changes the Mark‑Houwink parameters K and a, so [η] must be expressed as a function of θ. (iii) Neglecting the volume change upon binding; the partial molar volume of the bound ion differs from that of the free ion, affecting the solution density and thus the conversion between molarity and molality in viscosity correlations.

Non‑Equilibrium Thermodynamics of Membrane Transport: The Solution‑Diffusion Model with Concentration‑Dependent Diffusivity

The solution‑diffusion model describes permeation through a dense polymer membrane as three steps: sorption at the upstream interface, diffusion across the membrane, and desorption at the downstream interface. For a binary mixture of solvent (1) and solute (2), the steady‑state flux Ji = –Di(c) (dci/dx) where the diffusivity Di depends on the local concentration via the free‑volume theory: Di = Di,0 exp[–γ V*i / Vf(c)], with Vf the specific free volume of the swollen polymer. The concentration dependence of Vf is obtained from the dual‑mode sorption isotherm: c = kD p + (C′H b p)/(1 + b p), where kD is the Henry’s law constant, C′H the Langmuir capacity, and b the affinity parameter.

Coupling the sorption isotherm with the concentration‑dependent diffusivity yields a nonlinear diffusion equation that must be solved numerically (e.g., finite‑difference or orthogonal collocation). The model predicts the “plasticization” phenomenon: at high upstream CO₂ pressure, the sorbed CO₂ swells the polymer, increasing Vf and thus DCO₂, which leads to a super‑linear increase in CO₂ flux and a concomitant loss of selectivity for CO₂/CH₄ separation. The critical plasticization pressure pplast can be estimated from the inflection point of the permeability‑versus‑pressure curve, where d²P/dp² = 0.

Examination focus areas: (1) Deriving the expression for the overall permeability Pi = (1/L) ∫₀ᴸ Di(c) (∂c/∂p) dx and showing that for constant D it reduces to the classic solution‑diffusion permeability P = D·S. (2) Explaining why the Maxwell‑Stefan formulation is required for multicomponent mixtures with strong coupling (e.g., water‑ethanol‑salt) – the solution‑diffusion model assumes independent diffusion, which violates the Onsager reciprocal relations when cross‑coefficients are significant. (3) Recognising that the dual‑mode parameters kD, C′H, b are temperature dependent; a common trap is to treat them as constants when extrapolating permeability data from 298 K to 350 K.

  • Confusing the MSE association constant with the Pitzer ion‑pair formation constant; they have different standard states.
  • Applying UNIQUAC‑Dortmund τ parameters to solid‑liquid equilibrium without a separate SLE module.
  • Using classical van der Waals mixing rules in PR‑EOS for gas‑IL systems instead of Wong‑Sandler rules.
  • Assuming polymer‑salt binding does not alter the Mark‑Houwink parameters for intrinsic viscosity.
  • Treating diffusivity as constant in the solution‑diffusion model when the sorption isotherm shows strong Langmuir‑type curvature.
  • Neglecting the temperature dependence of dual‑mode sorption parameters when predicting high‑temperature membrane performance.
  • Forgetting that the Donnan potential in polymer‑salt complexation must be solved self‑consistently with the binding isotherm.

These advanced topics illustrate how modern thermodynamic models integrate molecular‑level interactions with macroscopic phase‑equilibrium and transport predictions, a perspective that examiners increasingly reward.

Thermodynamics of Mixed‑Solvent Electrolyte Solutions: The e‑NRTL Model

The electrolyte Non‑Random Two‑Liquid (e‑NRTL) model extends the classical NRTL framework to multicomponent electrolyte solutions by separating the excess Gibbs energy into a long‑range electrostatic term (based on the Pitzer‑Debye‑Hückel formulation) and a short‑range local‑composition term that accounts for specific ion‑solvent and solvent‑solvent interactions. The total excess molar Gibbs energy is written as GE = GE,LR + GE,SR. The long‑range part uses the ionic strength I = ½ Σ mizi2 and the Debye‑Hückel parameter Aφ to give GE,LR = –RT (2AφI3/2)/(1 + bI1/2) where b is a temperature‑dependent constant. The short‑range part follows the NRTL expression GE,SR = RT Σ xi Σ τjiGjixj / Σ Gkixk with binary interaction parameters τji = aji + bji/T + cjiln T and non‑randomness factors αji (typically 0.2–0.3 for ion‑solvent pairs). The model requires a consistent standard state: the symmetric convention for solvents (pure liquid at system T, P) and the infinite‑dilution convention for ions (hypothetical 1 mol kg−1 solution). Parameter estimation proceeds by simultaneous regression of vapor‑liquid equilibrium (VLE), liquid‑liquid equilibrium (LLE), and osmotic coefficient data. A key advantage over the Pitzer model is the ability to handle mixed‑solvent systems (e.g., water‑ethanol‑NaCl) without introducing ternary ion‑interaction parameters; the NRTL local‑composition term naturally captures solvent‑solvent non‑ideality. Typical exam questions ask for the derivation of the activity coefficient of water in a water‑methanol‑LiBr mixture, showing how the electrostatic term contributes a negative deviation at low ionic strength while the short‑range term produces a positive deviation at high salt concentrations. Students must remember that the e‑NRTL parameters are temperature dependent and that the model reduces to the classical NRTL when all ionic concentrations are set to zero.

Phase Equilibria in Ternary Systems Containing a Supercritical Component

When a supercritical fluid (SCF) such as CO₂ forms a ternary mixture with a polar solvent (e.g., water) and a solute (e.g., a pharmaceutical), the phase diagram exhibits a three‑phase region (vapor‑liquid‑liquid) bounded by a critical curve that terminates at a plait point. The thermodynamic condition for equilibrium is equality of component fugacities in all coexisting phases: fiV = fiL1 = fiL2. Fugacities are computed from an equation of state (EOS) capable of describing both the SCF and the polar liquid; the Peng‑Robinson EOS with the Wong‑Sandler mixing rule combined with an activity‑coefficient model (e.g., NRTL) for the liquid phase is a standard choice. The mixing rule enforces the low‑pressure limit GE = GE,model and provides a consistent route to calculate the binary interaction parameters kij(T) from VLE data. At temperatures above the critical temperature of the SCF, the vapor phase becomes a supercritical phase and the traditional bubble‑point calculation is replaced by a stability test based on the tangent plane distance (TPD) criterion: TPD(y) = Σ yi[ln φi(y) – ln φi(z)] ≥ 0 for all trial compositions y. The critical curve is traced by solving simultaneously the equilibrium equations and the determinant of the Hessian matrix of the Gibbs energy equal to zero. A common examination scenario provides binary VLE data for CO₂‑water and CO₂‑solute at 313 K and asks to predict the ternary LLE envelope at 150 bar. The solution requires (i) regressing kij for each binary, (ii) performing a flash calculation at the specified pressure and temperature, and (iii) checking for the appearance of a second liquid phase by evaluating the TPD for a trial composition rich in the polar solvent. Pitfalls include using the classical van der Waals mixing rule (which fails for highly asymmetric systems) and neglecting the pressure dependence of the NRTL parameters when the pressure exceeds 100 bar.

Worked Example: Ternary Flash with Supercritical CO₂
A ternary mixture of CO₂ (1), water (2), and ibuprofen (3) at 313 K and 150 bar has overall composition z = (0.70, 0.25, 0.05). Binary interaction parameters for the Peng‑Robinson‑Wong‑Sandler EOS are k₁₂ = 0.12, k₁₃ = 0.08, k₂₃ = 0.02. NRTL parameters for the liquid phase (water‑ibuprofen) are τ₂₃ = 1.2, τ₃₂ = 0.8, α₂₃ = 0.3. Determine whether a single liquid phase or two liquid phases exist at equilibrium.
1. Compute pure‑component PR parameters ai, bi at 313 K. 2. Apply Wong‑Sandler mixing rule to obtain mixture amix, bmix for a trial liquid composition x = (0.02, 0.70, 0.28) (CO₂‑poor, water‑rich). 3. Solve PR EOS for compressibility factor Z and fugacity coefficients φiL. 4. Calculate liquid‑phase fugacities fiL = xiφiLP. 5. Perform a stability test: evaluate TPD for a second trial composition y = (0.01, 0.30, 0.69) (ibuprofen‑rich). If TPD(y) < 0, the single‑phase assumption is unstable and a second liquid phase will form. 6. Using the given parameters, TPD(y) evaluates to –0.04, indicating instability. Therefore the system splits into two liquid phases at the specified conditions. The equilibrium compositions are obtained by solving the full three‑phase flash (V‑L1‑L2) with equality of fugacities for each component.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a ternary flash problem involves a supercritical component, first identify the appropriate EOS‑mixing‑rule combination (PR‑WS + NRTL is the textbook standard). Write down the stability criterion (TPD) before attempting the full flash; a negative TPD for any trial composition immediately proves phase splitting and earns partial credit. Memorise the Wong‑Sandler constraint GE = GE,model because examiners often ask to derive the expression for the mixture a parameter. Keep a table of typical kij ranges (0.05–0.15 for CO₂‑polar solvent) to sanity‑check regressed values.

Composition‑Dependent Interaction Parameters in Polymer‑Mixed‑Solvent Systems

In polymer solutions where the solvent is a binary mixture (e.g., water‑ethanol), the Flory‑Huggins interaction parameter χ is not a constant but varies with solvent composition φs1, φs2 and polymer volume fraction φp. The Gibbs free energy of mixing per lattice site is ΔGmix/RT = φpln φp/N + φs1ln φs1 + φs2ln φs2 + χps1φpφs1 + χps2φpφs2 + χs1s2φs1φs2. The binary χ parameters are often expressed as χij = Aij + Bij/T + Cijφk where φk is the volume fraction of the third component. This composition dependence captures the preferential solvation of the polymer by one solvent component, a phenomenon observed for poly(vinylpyrrolidone) in water‑ethanol where χp‑water decreases sharply as ethanol content rises. The spinodal condition for phase separation is obtained from the determinant of the second‑derivative matrix of ΔGmix with respect to the independent volume fractions: |∂²ΔG/∂φi∂φj| = 0. For a ternary system this yields a quadratic equation in φp whose coefficients contain the composition‑dependent χ values. The binodal curve is found by solving the equality of chemical potentials μi = ∂ΔG/∂φi in the two coexisting phases. A typical exam problem supplies χps1(φs2) = 0.45 – 0.30φs2, χps2(φs1) = 0.60 + 0.20φs1, χs1s2 = 0.10, N = 500, and asks to compute the critical polymer concentration at 298 K for a solvent mixture containing 30 % ethanol by volume. The solution proceeds by (i) evaluating χ values at the given solvent composition, (ii) constructing the spinodal determinant, (iii) setting φp = φp,crit and solving for the critical point where the spinodal and binodal meet (∂³ΔG/∂φp³ = 0). The result shows that the critical polymer volume fraction drops from 0.025 in pure water to 0.012 in 30 % ethanol, illustrating the cosolvency effect. Students must be careful to use volume fractions, not mole fractions, and to include the combinatorial entropy of the solvent mixture in the chemical‑potential expressions.

Protein Solubility in Crowded Aqueous Media: Excluded Volume and Preferential Interaction

Protein solubility in solutions containing high concentrations of cosolutes (e.g., polyethylene glycol, salts, or other proteins) is governed by a balance between excluded‑volume (steric) stabilization and preferential interaction (binding or depletion) effects. The chemical potential of the protein μp = μp⁰ + RT ln ap can be expressed through the osmotic virial expansion of the solution osmotic pressure Π = RT (cp/Mp + B22cp² + …) where the second virial coefficient B22 = B22⁰ + ΔB22(crowder). The excluded‑volume contribution to ΔB22 is positive and scales with the crowder radius of gyration Rg and concentration cc as ΔB22,EV ≈ (2π/3) NA (Rp + Rg)³ cc/Mc. Preferential interaction is quantified by the preferential interaction coefficient Γpc = (∂μp/∂μc)T,P, which can be measured by dialysis or density‑contrast methods. A negative Γpc indicates preferential exclusion (stabilizing), while a positive value indicates preferential binding (destabilizing). The solubility S (mol L−1) at a given crowder concentration follows ln(S/S₀) = – (Δμp/RT) where Δμp = ∫₀c_c Γpc dμc. For PEG‑8000 at 200 g L−1, typical Γpc for lysozyme is –0.15 mol kg−1, leading to a 3‑fold increase in solubility. In contrast, ammonium sulfate at 1 M gives Γpc ≈ +0.05 mol kg−1 (salting‑in at low concentration) but becomes negative at higher ionic strength due to the Hofmeister series. Examination questions often present a table of Γpc versus crowder concentration and ask to predict the solubility trend or to calculate the change in the protein chemical potential using numerical integration (trapezoidal rule). A common trap is to confuse the preferential interaction coefficient with the preferential hydration parameter Γph = Γpc – (νc/νw)Γpw, where ν are partial molar volumes; the two are related but not interchangeable.

  • Assuming the e‑NRTL model reduces to the Pitzer model at high ionic strength; the two have different standard‑state conventions and cannot be interchanged without re‑parameterisation.
  • Using the classical van der Waals mixing rule in a PR‑EOS for a ternary system containing a supercritical component; the Wong‑Sandler rule is required to enforce the correct low‑pressure limit.
  • Treating the Flory‑Huggins χ parameter as constant in a polymer‑mixed‑solvent system; composition dependence is essential for predicting cosolvency and the shift of the critical point.
  • Confusing the preferential interaction coefficient Γpc with the preferential hydration parameter Γph; they differ by a factor involving partial molar volumes of crowder and water.
  • Neglecting the pressure dependence of NRTL parameters when performing high‑pressure flash calculations above 100 bar; parameters must be regressed at each pressure or a pressure‑correction term must be included.
  • Applying the simple Debye‑Hückel limiting law for activity coefficients in the e‑NRTL long‑range term at ionic strengths >0.1 mol kg−1; the extended Pitzer‑Debye‑Hückel form with the b parameter is required.
  • Assuming the spinodal condition alone defines the binodal in a ternary polymer‑solvent system; the binodal requires equality of chemical potentials, not just the determinant of the Hessian.

These advanced topics illustrate how modern thermodynamic models integrate molecular‑level interactions with macroscopic phase‑equilibrium and solubility predictions, a perspective that examiners increasingly reward.

Thermodynamics of Ionic Liquid–Water Mixtures: Phase Behavior and Solubility

Ionic liquids (ILs) combined with water form a class of solvents whose phase behaviour ranges from complete miscibility to liquid–liquid equilibrium (LLE) with a critical solution temperature. The driving forces are a balance between strong Coulombic interactions of the ions and hydrogen‑bonding networks of water. For imidazolium‑based ILs with short alkyl chains (e.g., [C2mim][BF4]), the mixture is miscible at all compositions, whereas longer alkyl chains (e.g., [C8mim][PF6]) exhibit an upper critical solution temperature (UCST) near 320 K. The Gibbs free energy of mixing ΔGmix = ΔHmix – TΔSmix is dominated by the enthalpic term because the entropy of mixing is close to the ideal value for the small water molecule. Experimental LLE data are correlated with the non‑random two‑liquid (NRTL) model using temperature‑dependent binary interaction parameters τ12 = a + b/T and τ21 = c + d/T. The critical point satisfies the spinodal condition ∂²ΔGmix/∂x₁² = 0 together with the equality of chemical potentials μ₁α = μ₁β and μ₂α = μ₂β. In practice, the critical composition xc shifts toward the IL‑rich side as the alkyl chain length increases, reflecting the growing hydrophobic effect. Solubility of gases (CO₂, CH₄) in IL–water mixtures follows Henry’s law at low pressures, but the Henry constant Hi varies non‑monotonically with water mole fraction because water disrupts the IL’s nanostructure, creating transient cavities that enhance gas accommodation. A widely used correlation is the Krichevsky–Kasarnovsky equation extended for mixed solvents: ln Hi = ln Hi⁰ + (V̅i⁰/RT)(P – P⁰) + Σ kijxj, where kij are binary interaction coefficients obtained from VLE measurements. For process design (e.g., CO₂ capture), the water content is often optimised around 0.2–0.3 mole fraction to maximise CO₂ solubility while keeping viscosity manageable.

Solubility of Gases in Deep Eutectic Solvents: Molecular Interactions and Correlation Methods

Deep eutectic solvents (DESs) are formed by mixing a hydrogen‑bond acceptor (typically a quaternary ammonium salt such as choline chloride) with a hydrogen‑bond donor (e.g., urea, glycerol, ethylene glycol) at a molar ratio that yields a eutectic point far below the melting points of the pure components. The resulting liquid exhibits a highly structured hydrogen‑bond network that governs gas solubility. Experimental data for CO₂, N₂, and CH₄ in choline‑chloride‑based DESs show that CO₂ solubility is an order of magnitude higher than in conventional molecular solvents at the same temperature and pressure, owing to specific Lewis acid–base interactions between the carbonyl oxygen of urea (or the hydroxyl groups of glycerol) and the quadrupolar CO₂ molecule. The temperature dependence of Henry’s constant in DESs is commonly described by the van’t Hoff relation ln H = A + B/T, where the enthalpy of absorption ΔHabs = –RB is typically –15 to –25 kJ mol⁻¹ for CO₂, indicating exothermic physical absorption. For predictive purposes, the COSMO‑RS (Conductor‑like Screening Model for Real Solvents) approach has been adapted to DESs by treating the eutectic mixture as a pseudo‑component with a σ‑profile generated from the weighted average of the constituent σ‑profiles. The predicted Henry constants agree with experiment within 15 % for a wide range of DES compositions. An alternative empirical correlation is the modified Apelblat equation: ln x = A + B/T + C ln T, where x is the gas mole fraction in the liquid phase. The parameters A, B, C are regressed from experimental solubility data at several temperatures. When DESs are used as absorbents in gas‑separation processes, the viscosity increase upon gas loading must be accounted for; the Einstein relation η = η₀(1 + 2.5φ) with φ the volume fraction of dissolved gas provides a first‑order estimate, but the strong hydrogen‑bond restructuring often leads to a non‑linear viscosity rise that is better captured by the free‑volume model η = η₀ exp(γV*/Vf).

Worked Example: Henry Constant from Apelblat Parameters
For CO₂ in a choline‑chloride‑urea (1:2) DES, the Apelblat parameters are A = –12.5, B = 1800 K, C = 2.3. Calculate the Henry constant H (in bar) at 298 K assuming the gas phase behaves ideally and the liquid‑phase mole fraction x = P/H.
First compute ln x at 298 K: ln x = –12.5 + 1800/298 + 2.3 ln 298 = –12.5 + 6.040 + 2.3 × 5.697 = –12.5 + 6.040 + 13.103 = 6.643. Thus x = e6.643 ≈ 770. This value is the dimensionless mole fraction at 1 bar (since the Apelblat form is usually fitted to x at P = 1 bar). Therefore H = P/x = 1 bar / 770 ≈ 1.30 × 10⁻³ bar. In practice the Apelblat equation is fitted to x at various pressures; the above illustrates the algebraic steps. If the regression was performed with x at 10 bar, the Henry constant would be H = 10 bar / 770 ≈ 0.013 bar. Always verify the pressure basis of the reported parameters.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question supplies Apelblat or van’t Hoff parameters, first identify the reference pressure used in the correlation. Write the explicit expression for ln x, substitute the temperature, compute x, then obtain H = Pref/x. Keep units consistent (bar, Pa, atm) and note that Henry constants are often reported as kH = P/x (pressure‑over‑mole‑fraction) or as Hcp = x/P (mole‑fraction‑over‑pressure). The inverse relationship is a frequent trap.

Kinetic Aspects of Nucleation and Crystal Growth in Supersaturated Solutions

Supersaturation is the thermodynamic driving force for crystallisation, but the observable rate is controlled by nucleation and crystal‑growth kinetics. Classical nucleation theory (CNT) expresses the steady‑state homogeneous nucleation rate J = A exp(–ΔG*/kBT), where the critical free‑energy barrier ΔG* = (16πγ³vm²)/(3(kBT ln S)²). Here γ is the solid–liquid interfacial tension, vm the molecular volume in the crystal, and S = c/csat the supersaturation ratio. The pre‑exponential factor A ≈ ρl Z f* combines the number density of nucleation sites ρl, the Zeldovich factor Z ≈ (ΔG*/3πkBT)1/2, and the molecular attachment frequency f* ≈ (kBT/h) exp(–ΔGa/kBT). In practice, heterogeneous nucleation on vessel walls or impurities reduces the effective interfacial tension by a factor f(θ) = (2 + cos θ)(1 – cos θ)²/4, where θ is the contact angle. Crystal growth is often described by the Burton‑Cabrera‑Frank (BCF) model for spiral growth or by the two‑dimensional nucleation model for layer‑by‑layer growth. The linear growth rate G = kg σg, where σ = (c – csat)/csat is the relative supersaturation, kg a temperature‑dependent rate constant, and g the growth order (typically 1–2). For many inorganic salts (e.g., KCl, (NH₄)₂SO₄) g ≈ 1, indicating surface‑integration control; for organic molecules with strong hydrogen‑bonding, g can be 2–3, signalling two‑dimensional nucleation control. The overall crystallisation kinetics in a batch crystalliser are captured by the population balance equation ∂n/∂t + ∂(Gn)/∂L = 0 with nucleation boundary condition n(0,t) = J/G. Numerical solution (method of moments or discretised size classes) yields the crystal size distribution (CSD) needed for product specification. A common industrial strategy is to operate in the metastable zone where J is negligible but G is sufficient, achieved by controlled cooling or antisolvent addition profiles that keep σ within a narrow window.

Thermodynamic Modeling of Electrolyte Solutions Using the ePC‑SAFT Equation of State

The electrolyte Perturbed‑Chain Statistical Associating Fluid Theory (ePC‑SAFT) extends the molecular‑based PC‑SAFT equation of state to ionic species by adding a Debye‑Hückel‑type electrostatic term and a Born solvation term. Each ion is characterised by a segment number m, segment diameter σ, dispersion energy ε/kB, and charge z. The residual Helmholtz energy is a sum of hard‑chain, dispersion, association (for hydrogen‑bonding ions), and electrostatic contributions: ares = ahc + adisp + aassoc + aelec. The electrostatic term uses the Mean Spherical Approximation (MSA) for restricted primitive models: aelec/RT = –(κ³/12πρ) [1 + (κσ/2)]⁻¹, where κ² = (4πe²/εrkBT) Σ ρizi². The Born term accounts for the free energy of transferring an ion from vacuum into the dielectric medium: aBorn/RT = –(e²/2εrkBT) Σ ρizi²/σi. Parameter estimation proceeds by fitting pure‑component density and vapour‑pressure data for the neutral solvent, then regressing ion parameters against mean ionic activity coefficients and osmotic coefficients of binary salt solutions. Once parameters are available, ePC‑SAFT predicts VLE, LLE, and solid–liquid equilibria for multicomponent electrolyte mixtures without additional binary interaction parameters. For example, the solubility of NaCl in water–ethanol mixtures at 298 K is reproduced within 3 % using only the pure‑component and ion parameters. The model also captures the salting‑out effect on gas solubility: the Henry constant of CO₂ in aqueous NaCl solutions increases exponentially with salt molality, a trend that ePC‑SAFT reproduces through the electrostatic contribution to the chemical potential of CO₂. A practical limitation is the need for accurate dielectric constant εr of the mixture as a function of composition and temperature; this is usually supplied by a separate correlation (e.g., the Kirkwood‑Fröhlich equation) and fed into the ePC‑SAFT calculation.

  • Assuming the critical solution temperature of an ionic‑liquid–water system is always an UCST; some ILs exhibit a lower critical solution temperature (LCST) or a closed‑loop miscibility gap.
  • Using the simple van’t Hoff plot for gas solubility in DESs without checking linearity; curvature often signals a change in absorption mechanism (physical vs. chemical).
  • Neglecting the contact‑angle correction in heterogeneous nucleation calculations; even a modest θ = 30° reduces the barrier by ~30 %.
  • Treating the ePC‑SAFT electrostatic term as a simple Debye‑Hückel limiting law; the MSA expression includes the ionic diameter and is essential at molalities >0.5 mol kg⁻¹.
  • Confusing the growth order g in the BCF model with the nucleation order; they are independent kinetic parameters.
  • Applying the Apelblat correlation outside the temperature range of the fitted data; extrapolation can give negative Henry constants.
  • Overlooking the viscosity‑induced mass‑transfer limitation when designing a DES‑based gas absorber; the overall rate may be controlled by liquid‑film diffusion rather than equilibrium solubility.

These advanced topics illustrate how modern thermodynamic and kinetic frameworks integrate molecular‑level interactions with macroscopic phase‑equilibrium and crystallisation predictions, a perspective that examiners increasingly reward.

Thermodynamics of Electrolyte Solutions at High Temperatures and Pressures: The Helgeson–Kirkham–Flowers Model

The Helgeson–Kirkham–Flowers (HKF) model provides a rigorous framework for calculating standard-state thermodynamic properties of aqueous ions and neutral species up to 1000 °C and 5 kbar. It extends the Born equation by incorporating a temperature‑ and pressure‑dependent dielectric constant of water, ε(T,P), and a non‑electrostatic “intrinsic” term that accounts for short‑range ion–solvent interactions. The standard partial molal Gibbs energy of an ion i is expressed as

G°ᵢ = G°ᵢ,ref + ωᵢ (1/ε – 1/ε_ref) + Σ aⱼ (T – T_ref)ʲ + Σ bⱼ (P – P_ref)ʲ

where ωᵢ is the Born coefficient (proportional to zᵢ²/rᵢ), ε_ref is the dielectric constant at the reference state (usually 25 °C, 1 bar), and the aⱼ, bⱼ coefficients are fitted to experimental heat‑capacity, volume, and compressibility data. The model uses the revised HKF equations of state for water (e.g., the IAPWS‑95 formulation) to evaluate ε(T,P) and its derivatives, ensuring internal consistency between volumetric and dielectric properties.

Key Parameters and Their Physical Meaning

  • Born coefficient ωᵢ: ωᵢ = (N_A e² zᵢ²)/(8π ε₀ rᵢ) where rᵢ is the effective electrostatic radius. For common ions, ω ranges from 0.5 × 10⁵ J mol⁻¹ (large monovalent ions) to 5 × 10⁵ J mol⁻¹ (small multivalent ions).
  • Intrinsic heat‑capacity coefficients a₁, a₂, a₃, a₄: capture the non‑electrostatic contribution to C°ₚ,ᵢ. They are typically determined from high‑temperature calorimetry or from solubility measurements of sparingly soluble salts.
  • Intrinsic volume coefficients b₁, b₂, b₃: describe the pressure dependence of the standard partial molal volume V°ᵢ. The first coefficient b₁ is often correlated with the ion’s crystallographic radius.

The HKF model has been successfully applied to predict the solubility of minerals such as quartz, calcite, and albite in hydrothermal systems, the speciation of metal‑bearing fluids in ore‑forming environments, and the equilibrium constants for reactions like NaCl(aq) ⇌ Na⁺ + Cl⁻ at 600 °C, 2 kbar. In examination contexts, the model is frequently tested through the calculation of ΔG° for a reaction at elevated T‑P using tabulated HKF parameters (e.g., from the SUPCRT92 database) and the relation ΔG° = –RT ln K.

Common Examination Twists

  • Providing only the Born coefficient and asking for the electrostatic contribution to ΔG° at a given T‑P, requiring the student to compute ε(T,P) from the IAPWS‑95 correlation.
  • Mixing HKF parameters for ions from different sources (e.g., Shock vs. Sverjensky) and asking to identify the inconsistency.
  • Requesting the conversion of log K at 25 °C to log K at 400 °C, 1 kbar using the integrated form of the van’t Hoff equation with temperature‑dependent ΔH° derived from HKF heat‑capacity polynomials.

Thermodynamics of Polymer Solutions in Mixed Solvents: Composition‑Dependent Interaction Parameters

In mixed‑solvent systems the Flory–Huggins interaction parameter χ is no longer a constant but a function of solvent composition φ₁ (volume fraction of solvent 1). The most widely used representation is the linear mixing rule χ(φ₁) = φ₁ χ₁₂ + (1–φ₁) χ₁₃ + φ₁(1–φ₁) χ₂₃, where χ₁₂ and χ₁₃ are the binary polymer–solvent interaction parameters and χ₂₃ is the solvent–solvent interaction parameter. This expression captures the cosolvency effect: a polymer that is insoluble in either pure solvent may dissolve in a specific mixture because the effective χ drops below the critical value χ_c = 0.5 (for high molecular weight).

Determination of χ(φ₁) from Cloud‑Point Measurements

Cloud‑point curves (temperature vs. polymer concentration) are measured at several fixed solvent compositions. For each composition the critical point (φ_c, T_c) is located, and χ_c is obtained from the Flory–Huggins spinodal condition ∂²ΔG_mix/∂φ² = 0, yielding χ_c = (1/2)(1/√N + 1)² ≈ 0.5 for N ≫ 1. Plotting χ_c versus φ₁ gives the experimental χ(φ₁) which can be fitted to the mixing rule to extract χ₂₃. A negative χ₂₃ indicates favorable solvent–solvent interactions that enhance polymer solubility (cosolvency), while a positive χ₂₃ signals anti‑cosolvency.

Effect on Phase Diagrams

The composition dependence of χ transforms the binary polymer–solvent phase diagram into a ternary diagram with a two‑phase region that can close (re‑entrant miscibility) or split into two distinct miscibility gaps. For example, poly(N‑isopropylacrylamide) in water–methanol exhibits a closed‑loop miscibility gap: at low methanol the polymer is soluble, at intermediate methanol it phase‑separates, and at high methanol it dissolves again. The loop arises because χ(φ₁) passes through a maximum near φ₁ ≈ 0.3, making χ > χ_c only in that window.

Advanced Modeling: The Koningsveld–Kleintjens Formalism

For quantitative prediction, the Koningsveld–Kleintjens (KK) approach expresses the chemical potential of the polymer as μ_p = μ_p° + RT[ln φ_p + (1–1/N)φ_s + χ_eff φ_s²], where φ_s = φ₁ + φ₂ is the total solvent volume fraction and χ_eff = χ(φ₁) – (φ₁φ₂/φ_s²) χ₂₃. This formulation automatically satisfies the Gibbs–Duhem relation for the ternary mixture and is the basis for calculating tie‑lines in the two‑phase region. Examination problems often provide cloud‑point data at three compositions and ask the student to (i) compute χ_c at each composition, (ii) fit the mixing rule, and (iii) predict the binodal curve at a fourth composition.

Worked Example: Cosolvency in a Ternary Polymer‑Solvent System
A polymer (N = 10⁴) shows cloud‑point temperatures of 45 °C in pure water (φ₁ = 1), 30 °C in pure ethanol (φ₁ = 0), and 20 °C at φ₁ = 0.4 (water volume fraction). Assuming the critical polymer volume fraction φ_c ≈ N⁻¹ᐟ² = 0.01 at all compositions, calculate χ_c at each composition, fit the linear mixing rule χ(φ₁) = φ₁ χ₁₂ + (1–φ₁) χ₁₃ + φ₁(1–φ₁) χ₂₃, and predict the cloud‑point temperature at φ₁ = 0.7 if the temperature dependence of χ follows χ = A + B/T with A = 0.2, B = 150 K.
  1. At the critical point χ_c = 0.5 (since N ≫ 1). Thus χ_c = 0.5 for all three compositions.
  2. Set up equations: φ₁=1 → χ₁₂ = 0.5 φ₁=0 → χ₁₃ = 0.5 φ₁=0.4 → 0.4·0.5 + 0.6·0.5 + 0.4·0.6·χ₂₃ = 0.5 → 0.5 + 0.24 χ₂₃ = 0.5 → χ₂₃ = 0.
  3. With χ₂₃ = 0, χ(φ₁) = 0.5 for all φ₁. The temperature dependence χ = 0.2 + 150/T gives T = 150/(χ – 0.2). For χ = 0.5, T = 150/0.3 = 500 K = 227 °C. This contradicts the experimental cloud points, indicating that the assumption χ_c = 0.5 is too crude; the finite‑N correction χ_c = 0.5(1+1/√N)² ≈ 0.51 must be used. Re‑calculating with χ_c = 0.51 yields χ₂₃ ≈ –0.04, a weak cosolvency. The predicted cloud point at φ₁ = 0.7 then becomes T ≈ 150/(0.51 – 0.2) ≈ 484 K (211 °C), still far from the measured range, showing that the simple linear mixing rule is insufficient for this system and higher‑order composition terms are needed.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a problem gives cloud‑point temperatures at several solvent compositions, first convert each temperature to χ using the appropriate χ(T) correlation (often χ = A + B/T). Then fit the composition dependence to the mixing rule. Remember that χ_c is not exactly 0.5 for finite N; use χ_c = 0.5(1 + N⁻¹ᐟ²)². If the fitted χ₂₃ is negative, the system exhibits cosolvency; if positive, anti‑cosolvency. Always check whether the predicted χ at the target composition yields a physically realistic temperature (positive, within the experimental range).

Thermodynamics of Liquid–Liquid Equilibrium in Polymer–Solvent Systems: The Sanchez–Lacombe Equation of State

The Sanchez–Lacombe (SL) equation of state is a lattice‑fluid model that treats both polymer and solvent as compressible fluids on a lattice. It expresses the reduced pressure P̃ = P/P*, reduced temperature T̃ = T/T*, and reduced density ρ̃ = ρ/ρ* in terms of the dimensionless parameters r (chain length ratio), ε* (characteristic energy), and v* (characteristic volume). The Helmholtz free energy per lattice site is

Ã = ρ̃ [ln(ρ̃) + (1 – 1/r) ln(1 – ρ̃) – (1 – 1/r) ρ̃ – (ε*/RT) ρ̃²]

From this, the chemical potentials of solvent (μ₁) and polymer (μ₂) are derived, enabling calculation of binodal and spinodal curves. The SL EOS reduces to the Flory–Huggins model in the incompressible limit (ρ̃ → 1) but captures the effect of free volume on phase separation, which is crucial for polymer solutions near the critical point.

Parameter Estimation

For a binary polymer–solvent system, three pure‑component parameters (T*, P*, ρ*) are needed for each component, plus a binary interaction parameter κ₁₂ (often denoted Δε/k_B). These are obtained by fitting pure‑component PVT data and binary cloud‑point curves. Typical values for polystyrene in cyclohexane are T*₁ = 450 K, P*₁ = 300 MPa, ρ*₁ = 0.85 g cm⁻³ for the solvent and T*₂ = 550 K, P*₂ = 350 MPa, ρ*₂ = 0.95 g cm⁻³ for the polymer, with κ₁₂ ≈ 0.02.

Prediction of Closed‑Loop Miscibility Gaps

Because the SL EOS includes compressibility, it can predict both upper critical solution temperature (UCST) and lower critical solution temperature (LCST) behavior within a single framework. The LCST arises from the negative thermal expansion of the lattice fluid at high densities, which makes the effective χ decrease with temperature. For polymer–solvent pairs with strong specific interactions (e.g., hydrogen bonding), the binary parameter κ₁₂ becomes temperature dependent, κ₁₂ = κ₀ + κ₁/T, allowing the model to reproduce re‑entrant phase diagrams observed experimentally.

Examination Relevance

Questions may provide the SL parameters for a polymer–solvent pair and ask for (i) the critical temperature and composition from the spinodal condition (∂²ΔG/∂φ² = 0), (ii) the pressure effect on the critical point (∂T_c/∂P), or (iii) a comparison of the SL prediction with the Flory–Huggins result at the same χ. The key is to remember that the SL spinodal condition involves the second derivative of the reduced Helmholtz energy with respect to density and composition, leading to a matrix determinant that must vanish.

Thermodynamics of Gas Solubility in Supercritical CO₂ with Cosolvents: Peng–Robinson EOS and Mixing Rules

Supercritical CO₂ (scCO₂) is widely used as a green solvent for extraction and reaction processes. The solubility of a solid solute in scCO₂ is often enhanced by adding a polar cosolvent (e.g., ethanol, water). The Peng–Robinson (PR) equation of state with the Wong–Sandler (WS) mixing rule provides a thermodynamically consistent framework for modeling such ternary systems. The PR EOS is

P = RT/(V – b) – aα/(V² + 2bV – b²)

where a = 0.45724 R²T_c²/P_c, b = 0.07780 RT_c/P_c, and α = [1 + κ(1 – √(T/T_c))]² with κ = 0.37464 + 1.54226ω – 0.26992ω².

Wong–Sandler Mixing Rule

The WS rule enforces the exact low‑pressure limit of the excess Gibbs energy: Gᴱ = Σ x_i x_j (a_ij – a_ii – a_jj)/b_mix. The mixture parameters are

a_mix = Σ Σ x_i x_j a_ij, b_mix = Σ x_i b_i

with a_ij = √(a_i a_j) (1 – k_ij). The binary interaction parameters k_ij are fitted to VLE or solubility data. For CO₂–ethanol, k12 ≈ 0.12; for CO₂–solute (e.g., naphthalene), k13 ≈ 0.15; for ethanol–solute, k23 ≈ 0.05.

Calculation of Solid Solubility

The equilibrium condition for a solid solute (component 3) is f₃^solid = f₃^fluid. The solid fugacity is f₃^solid = P₃^sat φ₃^sat exp[∫P₃^sat^P V₃^solid/RT dP] ≈ P₃^sat φ₃^sat exp[V₃^solid (P – P₃^sat)/RT]. The fluid‑phase fugacity is obtained from the PR EOS with WS mixing rule. The solubility y₃ is found by solving the fugacity equality at the given T, P, and cosolvent composition y₂. Typical results for naphthalene in scCO₂ at 313 K, 15 MPa show y₃ ≈ 2 × 10⁻⁴ without cosolvent, rising to 8 × 10⁻⁴ with 5 mol % ethanol.

Common Pitfalls in Numerical Solution

  • Using the classic van der Waals mixing rule (a_mix = Σ Σ x_i x_j a_ij, b_mix = Σ x_i b_i) without the WS correction leads to large errors in the critical region because the low‑pressure Gᴱ limit is violated.
  • Neglecting the Poynting correction for the solid fugacity when P ≫ P₃^sat; the exponential term can change the solubility by a factor of 2–3 at 20 MPa.
  • Assuming the binary interaction parameter k_ij is temperature independent; for CO₂–polar cosolvent systems k12 often varies linearly with 1/T.

Thermodynamics of Protein Crystallization: Phase Diagrams, Nucleation, and the Role of Precipitants

Protein crystallization is governed by the same thermodynamic principles as small‑molecule crystallization but with additional complexity due to the large size, anisotropy, and conformational flexibility of protein molecules. The phase diagram in the plane of protein concentration (c_p) versus precipitant concentration (c_prec) typically shows a solubility curve (binodal) separating the undersaturated region from the supersaturated region, a metastable zone where nucleation is slow, and a labile zone where spontaneous nucleation occurs. The solubility curve often follows the empirical relation ln c_p = A + B c_prec + C c_prec², reflecting the preferential exclusion or binding of precipitant.

Nucleation Kinetics

Classical nucleation theory gives the steady‑state nucleation rate J = J₀ exp(–ΔG*/k_B T) with ΔG* = 16πγ³ v_m² / (3 (k_B T ln S)²), where γ is the crystal–solution interfacial tension, v_m the molecular volume, and S = c_p / c_sat the supersaturation ratio. For proteins, γ is typically 0.5–2 mJ m⁻², much lower than for inorganic salts, leading to smaller critical nuclei (10–100 molecules). The pre‑exponential factor J₀ includes the attachment frequency and the Zeldovich factor; it is often treated as an adjustable parameter fitted to induction‑time measurements.

Effect of Precipitant Type

  • Salts (e.g., (NH₄)₂SO₄): Act mainly by preferential exclusion, increasing the chemical potential of the protein and reducing its solubility. The Hofmeister series ranks anions by their effectiveness: SO₄²⁻ > HPO₄²⁻ > CH₃COO⁻ > Cl⁻ > NO₃⁻ > ClO₄⁻ > SCN⁻.
  • Polymers (e.g., PEG 4000): Induce depletion attraction; the phase diagram shows a steep solubility curve and a narrow metastable zone.
  • Organic solvents (e.g., MPD, ethanol): Reduce the dielectric constant, weakening electrostatic repulsion and promoting specific protein–protein contacts.

Crystal Growth and Quality

After nucleation, crystal growth proceeds by layer‑by‑layer (2D nucleation) or spiral growth mechanisms. The growth rate G = k_g σ^g, where σ = (c_p – c_sat)/c_sat. For many proteins g ≈ 1–2, indicating surface‑integration control. Impurities (e.g., partially denatured protein) can adsorb on growth steps, reducing G and increasing mosaic spread. The final crystal quality is assessed by X‑ray diffraction resolution; a common target is ≤ 2.0 Å.

Examination Focus

Problems may ask to (i) construct a solubility curve from a set of (c_p, c_prec) data points using the quadratic log‑linear model, (ii) calculate the critical nucleus size n* = 32πγ³ v_m² / (3 (k_B T ln S)³) for given γ and S, (iii) predict how a change in pH shifts the solubility curve via the protein’s net charge (using the Tanford–Kirkwood model), or (iv) design a vapor‑diffusion experiment to traverse the metastable zone without entering the labile zone.

  • Assuming the Flory–Huggins χ parameter is constant in mixed‑solvent polymer systems; it is composition dependent and can change sign.
  • Using the simple van’t Hoff equation for gas solubility in supercritical CO₂ without accounting for the density‑dependent solvation enthalpy.
  • Neglecting the Poynting correction when calculating solid fugacity at pressures above 10 MPa.
  • Treating the Sanchez–Lacombe EOS as incompressible; the free‑volume term is essential for LCST prediction.
  • Confusing the growth order g in the Burton–Cabrera–Frank model with the nucleation order; they are independent kinetic parameters.
  • Applying the Helgeson–Kirkham–Flowers model outside its calibrated T‑P range (above 1000 °C or 5 kbar) without extrapolation warnings.
  • Overlooking the temperature dependence of binary interaction parameters in the Wong–Sandler mixing rule for CO₂–cosolvent systems.

These advanced topics illustrate how modern thermodynamic and kinetic frameworks integrate molecular‑level interactions with macroscopic phase‑equilibrium and crystallisation predictions, a perspective that examiners increasingly reward.

Thermodynamics of Ionic Liquid–Water Mixtures: Activity Coefficient Models and Phase Behavior

Ionic liquids (ILs) combined with water form highly non‑ideal binary systems that exhibit liquid–liquid equilibrium (LLE), vapor–liquid equilibrium (VLE), and sometimes upper or lower critical solution temperatures (UCST/LCST). The strong Coulombic interactions, hydrogen‑bonding networks, and large size asymmetry between the IL ions and water molecules make simple Raoult‑law or Henry‑law descriptions inadequate. Two activity‑coefficient frameworks have become standard for correlating and predicting phase equilibria in these mixtures: the electrolyte‑NRTL (e‑NRTL) model and the COSMO‑RS (Conductor‑like Screening Model for Real Solvents) approach.

The e‑NRTL model extends the classical NRTL expression by adding a long‑range Debye–Hückel term for the ionic contribution and a short‑range NRTL term for the neutral‑species interactions. For a binary IL–water system the excess Gibbs energy is written as Gᴱ = Gᴱ_DH + Gᴱ_NRTL, where Gᴱ_DH = –RT Σ_i m_i z_i² A_φ ln(1 + κ a) / (κ a) and Gᴱ_NRTL follows the usual τ_ij, α_ij parameter set. The binary interaction parameters τ_IL‑water and τ_water‑IL are temperature dependent, often expressed as τ = a + b/T + c ln T. Accurate VLE data (typically measured by static or dynamic methods up to 10 MPa) are regressed to obtain these parameters; the resulting model reproduces both the water activity (a_w) and the IL activity over the full composition range.

COSMO‑RS, by contrast, requires only the σ‑profiles of the isolated ions and water, generated from quantum‑chemical COSMO calculations (usually BP/TZVP level). The chemical potential of each species in the mixture is obtained from the statistical thermodynamics of surface segments, automatically accounting for hydrogen‑bonding, electrostatic misfit, and van der Waals interactions. Because no experimental binary parameters are needed, COSMO‑RS is valuable for screening new ILs for water‑absorption or dehydration applications. However, its predictive accuracy for LLE near the critical region can be 5–10 % in mole fraction, so experimental tie‑line data are still recommended for final process design.

Phase diagrams of IL–water systems frequently show a miscibility gap that closes at a UCST (e.g., [C₄mim][BF₄]–water) or an LCST (e.g., [C₄mim][PF₆]–water). The critical composition shifts with the alkyl chain length on the cation and the hydrophobicity of the anion. The thermodynamic condition for criticality is (∂²G/∂x²)_T,P = 0 and (∂³G/∂x³)_T,P = 0, which in the e‑NRTL framework translates into a set of nonlinear equations for the critical temperature T_c and critical mole fraction x_c. Solving these equations provides a direct test of the model’s consistency: a model that fits VLE but predicts an incorrect critical point is thermodynamically unsound.

Practical implications include the design of IL‑based extractive distillation for azeotrope breaking (e.g., ethanol–water with [C₄mim][OAc]), the sizing of water‑removal units in IL recycle loops, and the prediction of IL loss to the aqueous phase in biphasic catalysis. In all cases, the activity‑coefficient model must be validated against at least three independent data sets (VLE, LLE, and water activity) before it is used for equipment sizing.

Phase Behavior of Mixed Electrolyte Solutions: The Extended UNIQUAC Model

Mixed electrolyte solutions containing two or more salts (e.g., NaCl–KCl–H₂O or Na₂SO₄–MgCl₂–H₂O) are central to brine processing, seawater desalination, and nuclear waste treatment. The Extended UNIQUAC model, originally developed by Thomsen and Rasmussen, combines a Debye–Hückel electrostatic term with a UNIQUAC combinatorial‑residual term that treats ions as “pseudo‑components” having effective volumes (r_i) and surface areas (q_i). The excess Gibbs energy is Gᴱ = Gᴱ_DH + Gᴱ_UNIQUAC, where Gᴱ_DH uses the Pitzer‑type ionic strength dependence and Gᴱ_UNIQUAC = RT Σ_i x_i ln(φ_i/x_i) + RT Σ_i x_i q_i ln(θ_i/φ_i) – RT Σ_i x_i q_i Σ_j θ_j τ_ji / Σ_k θ_k τ_ki.

Key to the model is the definition of the ionic mole fractions x_i on a “true‑component” basis (including water) and the use of temperature‑dependent binary interaction parameters τ_ij = a_ij + b_ij/T + c_ij ln T for each ion‑pair (cation–anion, cation–cation, anion–anion) and ion–water pair. The parameter set for the Na⁺–Cl⁻–H₂O subsystem, for example, contains τ_Na⁺‑Cl⁻, τ_Na⁺‑H₂O, τ_Cl⁻‑H₂O, plus the combinatorial r and q values (r_Na⁺ = 0.92, q_Na⁺ = 1.10, r_Cl⁻ = 1.18, q_Cl⁻ = 1.20, r_H₂O = 0.92, q_H₂O = 1.40). When a second salt is added, only the new cross‑interaction parameters (e.g., τ_K⁺‑Cl⁻, τ_K⁺‑SO₄²⁻) need to be regressed; the original parameters remain unchanged, ensuring thermodynamic consistency across the whole composition space.

The model predicts water activity, osmotic coefficient, and mean ionic activity coefficients up to saturation (≈ 6 mol kg⁻¹ for NaCl). It also yields solid–liquid equilibrium (SLE) by equating the chemical potential of the solid salt (μ_i^solid = μ_i0,solid + RT ln a_i^solid) with that in solution (μ_i^sol = μ_i0,sol + RT ln a_i^sol). The solid activity a_i^solid is taken as unity for pure solids, but for solid solutions (e.g., (Na,K)Cl) a regular‑solution model for the solid phase can be coupled. This capability makes Extended UNIQUAC the workhorse for simulating evaporative crystallization sequences in multi‑salt brines.

Validation against the comprehensive “Salt‑Water” database (over 200 binary and ternary systems) shows average absolute deviations in water activity of 0.003 and in mean activity coefficients of 0.02 up to 5 mol kg⁻¹. The model’s predictive power for quaternary systems (e.g., Na⁺–K⁺–Mg²⁺–Cl⁻–SO₄²⁻–H₂O) is limited by the scarcity of cross‑interaction data; in practice, the “common‑ion” approximation (τ_Na⁺‑Cl⁻ ≈ τ_K⁺‑Cl⁻) is often employed as a first estimate, followed by targeted experiments to refine the most influential parameters.

Worked Example: Water Activity in a Ternary NaCl–KCl–Water Brine
A brine at 298 K contains 2.0 mol kg⁻¹ NaCl and 1.5 mol kg⁻¹ KCl. Using the Extended UNIQUAC parameters τ_Na⁺‑Cl⁻ = 8.5, τ_K⁺‑Cl⁻ = 7.9, τ_Na⁺‑H₂O = –4.2, τ_K⁺‑H₂O = –3.8, τ_Cl⁻‑H₂O = –5.1 (all in K), r and q values as given above, calculate the water activity a_w.
1. Convert molalities to mole fractions on a true‑component basis. Total moles of solute species = 2.0 + 1.5 + (2.0+1.5) = 7.0 mol per kg water (55.51 mol). x_H₂O = 55.51 / (55.51+7.0) = 0.888. x_Na⁺ = 2.0/62.51 = 0.0320, x_K⁺ = 1.5/62.51 = 0.0240, x_Cl⁻ = 3.5/62.51 = 0.0560. 2. Compute ionic strength I = ½ Σ m_i z_i² = ½(2.0·1² + 1.5·1² + 3.5·1²) = 3.5 mol kg⁻¹. 3. Evaluate Debye–Hückel term: ln γ_i^DH = –A_φ z_i² √I / (1 + b √I) with A_φ = 0.392 kg½ mol⁻½, b = 1.2 kg½ mol⁻½ → ln γ_i^DH ≈ –0.392·√3.5/(1+1.2·√3.5) = –0.392·1.87/(1+2.24) = –0.734/3.24 = –0.226. 4. Compute UNIQUAC combinatorial part: φ_i = x_i r_i / Σ x_k r_k, θ_i = x_i q_i / Σ x_k q_k. Σ x_k r_k = 0.888·0.92 + 0.032·0.92 + 0.024·0.92 + 0.056·1.18 = 0.817 + 0.029 + 0.022 + 0.066 = 0.934. φ_H₂O = 0.888·0.92/0.934 = 0.874. Σ x_k q_k = 0.888·1.40 + 0.032·1.10 + 0.024·1.10 + 0.056·1.20 = 1.243 + 0.035 + 0.026 + 0.067 = 1.371. θ_H₂O = 0.888·1.40/1.371 = 0.907. ln γ_H₂O^comb = ln(φ_H₂O/x_H₂O) + (z/2) q_H₂O ln(θ_H₂O/φ_H₂O) with coordination number z = 10. ln(0.874/0.888) = –0.016; (10/2)·1.40·ln(0.907/0.874) = 7·1.40·0.037 = 0.363. Sum = 0.347. 5. Residual part: τ_H₂O‑Na⁺ = –4.2/298 = –0.0141, τ_H₂O‑K⁺ = –3.8/298 = –0.0128, τ_H₂O‑Cl⁻ = –5.1/298 = –0.0171. Compute Σ θ_j τ_jH₂O etc. (omitted for brevity) → ln γ_H₂O^res ≈ –0.12. 6. Total ln γ_H₂O = ln γ_H₂O^DH + ln γ_H₂O^comb + ln γ_H₂O^res = –0.226 + 0.347 – 0.12 = 0.001. γ_H₂O ≈ 1.001. 7. Water activity a_w = x_H₂O γ_H₂O = 0.888 × 1.001 ≈ 0.889. The result matches experimental data (a_w ≈ 0.887) within 0.2 %.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for water activity or osmotic coefficient in a mixed‑salt brine, first identify whether the problem expects the Extended UNIQUAC or the Pitzer model. Extended UNIQUAC is preferred for systems up to 6 mol kg⁻¹ with available binary parameters; Pitzer is more robust above 6 mol kg⁻¹. Write down the full expression for Gᴱ, list the required τ_ij and r_i/q_i values, and show the conversion from molality to mole fraction. Examiners award partial credit for a correct setup even if the final arithmetic is truncated.

Thermodynamic Modeling of Protein–Ligand Binding in Solution: Linkage to Colligative Properties

Protein–ligand binding equilibria are traditionally described by the binding constant K_b = [PL]/([P][L]) and the associated standard Gibbs energy ΔG° = –RT ln K_b. In dilute aqueous solution the ligand activity can be approximated by its molar concentration, but at the high macromolecular concentrations used in crystallography or formulation (≥ 10 mg mL⁻¹) the solution non‑ideality becomes significant. The colligative properties of the solution—osmotic pressure Π, vapor‑pressure lowering Δp, and freezing‑point depression ΔT_f—provide a macroscopic route to the activity coefficients of both protein and ligand.

The osmotic pressure of a protein solution is given by the virial expansion Π = RT (c/M + B₂ c² + B₃ c³ + …), where c is the mass concentration, M the molar mass, and B₂ the second osmotic virial coefficient. B₂ is directly related to the protein–protein interaction potential and can be measured by static light scattering or membrane osmometry. When a ligand binds, the apparent molar mass of the complex changes, and the osmotic pressure at a fixed total protein mass concentration shifts. By measuring Π as a function of ligand concentration at constant protein mass, one can extract the binding stoichiometry n and the binding constant corrected for non‑ideality: K_b^app = K_b (γ_P γ_L / γ_PL). The activity coefficients γ_i are obtained from the osmotic virial coefficients via the McMillan–Mayer framework: ln γ_P = 2 B₂P c_P + …, ln γ_L = 2 B₂L c_L + …, ln γ_PL = 2 B₂PL c_PL + ….

Vapor‑pressure osmometry (VPO) offers an alternative: the solvent chemical potential μ₁ = μ₁° + RT ln a₁, with a₁ = p/p° = exp(–Π V̄₁/RT) where V̄₁ is the partial molar volume of water. A small change in a₁ upon ligand addition yields Δln a₁ = –ΔΠ V̄₁/RT. Because VPO detects changes in the number of solute particles, it is sensitive to the dissociation/association equilibrium of the ligand (e.g., dimerization) as well as to protein–ligand binding. The measured Δln a₁ can be fitted to a binding model that includes the ligand self‑association constant K_dimer, providing a simultaneous determination of both equilibria.

Freezing‑point depression ΔT_f = K_f m_eff, where m_eff is the effective molality of all solute species (including bound and free forms). In a system where a protein (P) binds n ligands (L) to form PL_n, the total molality of particles is m_eff = m_P + m_L + (n–1) m_PL_n. By measuring ΔT_f at several ligand‑to‑protein ratios, one can solve for n and K_b without any spectroscopic label. This colligative approach is especially valuable for ligands that lack chromophores or for membrane proteins in detergent micelles where optical methods suffer from scattering.

The thermodynamic linkage between colligative properties and binding constants is formalized by the Wyman–Tanford linkage equation: (∂ln K_b/∂ln a₁)_T,P = –Δν₁, where Δν₁ is the change in the number of bound water molecules upon complex formation. A positive Δν₁ (release of water) leads to an increase in K_b as the solvent activity decreases (e.g., by adding osmolytes such as PEG or sucrose). This principle underlies the “osmotic stress” technique used to probe hydration changes in protein–DNA and protein–ligand interactions.

Effect of Pressure on Aqueous Electrolyte Activity Coefficients: The Helgeson–Kirkham–Flowers Model

At pressures exceeding 10 MPa, the standard‑state properties of aqueous ions and the Debye–Hückel limiting slope become pressure dependent, and the simple Pitzer or e‑NRTL models calibrated at 0.1 MPa lose accuracy. The Helgeson–Kirkham–Flowers (HKF) equation of state provides a theoretically grounded framework for calculating the standard partial molal Gibbs energy, enthalpy, volume, and heat capacity of aqueous species as functions of temperature (up to 1000 °C) and pressure (up to 5 kbar). The HKF model expresses the standard‑state Gibbs energy as G°(T,P) = G°_ref + ∫_Tref^T (–S°) dT + ∫_Pref^P V° dP, where the standard‑state volume V°(T,P) is given by a Born‑type solvation term plus a non‑solvation “intrinsic” volume.

The Born equation for the solvation volume is V°_Born = –(ω/ε) (∂ε/∂P)_T, where ω = (z²e²N_A)/(8πε₀r_eff) is the Born coefficient, ε the dielectric constant of water, and r_eff the effective electrostatic radius of the ion. The dielectric constant ε(T,P) is itself modeled by the Fernández et al. (1997) formulation, which reproduces the steep increase of ε with pressure at low temperatures and its decrease at high temperatures. The non‑solvation volume is represented by a polynomial in 1/(T–Θ) and P, with Θ = 228 K. The HKF parameters for each ion (a₁, a₂, a₃, a₄, c₁, c₂, ω) are compiled in the SUPCRT92 database and have been validated against high‑P‑T solubility data for minerals such as quartz, calcite, and albite.

To obtain activity coefficients at elevated pressure, the HKF standard‑state properties are combined with a pressure‑extended Debye–Hückel term: ln γ_i = –A_γ z_i² √I / (1 + B_γ a_i √I) + Σ_j m_j (2β_ij + Z C_ij) + …, where the Debye–Hückel parameters A_γ and B_γ are functions of ε(T,P) and the water density ρ(T,P). The ion‑size parameter a_i is often taken as the “effective diameter” from the HKF intrinsic volume. The binary interaction parameters β_ij, C_ij are assumed pressure independent over moderate ranges (≤ 100 MPa), but for higher pressures a linear pressure correction β_ij(P) = β_ij⁰ + (∂β_ij/∂P) P can be introduced, with ∂β_ij/∂P obtained from high‑pressure osmotic coefficient measurements.

Application of the HKF‑Debye–Hückel framework to the NaCl–H₂O system at 500 °C and 100 MPa predicts a mean activity coefficient γ_± = 0.55, compared with the experimental value 0.53. The model also correctly captures the sign reversal of (∂ln γ_±/∂P)_T observed near the critical point of water (374 °C, 22.1 MPa), where the dielectric constant drops sharply and the Debye–Hückel term changes curvature. For geochemical modeling of hydrothermal ore formation or for designing supercritical water oxidation reactors, the HKF model remains the only internally consistent thermodynamic database that spans the required T‑P domain.

Solubility of Ionic Compounds in Mixed Solvents: The Jouyban–Acree Model Extended to Temperature Dependence

The Jouyban–Acree (J‑A) model is a semi‑empirical correlation that describes the solubility of a solid solute in a binary solvent mixture as a function of composition and temperature. The original formulation expresses the logarithm of the mole‑fraction solubility x₃ in a mixture of solvents 1 and 2 as ln x₃ = w₁ ln x₃¹ + w₂ ln x₃² + w₁ w₂ Σi=0^n J_i (w₁ – w₂)^i, where w₁ and w₂ are the mass fractions of the pure solvents in the mixture, x₃¹ and x₃² are the solubilities in the pure solvents at the same temperature, and J_i are temperature‑independent interaction parameters fitted to experimental data.

To capture the temperature dependence, the J‑A model is extended by allowing each J_i to vary with temperature according to a van’t Hoff‑type expression: J_i(T) = A_i + B_i/T + C_i ln T. Alternatively, the pure‑solvent solubilities x₃¹(T) and x₃²(T) are correlated with the modified Apelblat equation ln x₃^k = A_k + B_k/T + C_k ln T (k = 1,2), and the J_i are kept constant. The combined model then reads ln x₃(T,w₁) = w₁ (A₁ + B₁/T + C₁ ln T) + w₂ (A₂ + B₂/T + C₂ ln T) + w₁ w₂ Σ J_i (w₁ – w₂)^i. This formulation has been successfully applied to the solubility of anthracene in ethanol–water, of benzoic acid in methanol–water, and of several pharmaceutical compounds (e.g., ibuprofen, carbamazepine) in propylene glycol–water mixtures over the range 278–323 K.

Parameter estimation proceeds in two stages: first, the pure‑solvent Apelblat parameters (A_k, B_k, C_k) are obtained from at least five temperature points in each pure solvent. Second, the mixture data at several compositions (typically w₁ = 0.1, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 0.9) and temperatures are regressed simultaneously for the J_i coefficients. The number of J_i terms (n) is chosen by the Akaike information criterion; for most drug‑like molecules n = 1 or 2 suffices. The model typically yields a root‑mean‑square deviation (RMSD) of 0.02–0.05 in ln x₃, corresponding to a 2–5 % error in mole‑fraction solubility.

Thermodynamic consistency can be checked by verifying that the derived enthalpy of solution ΔH_sol = –R (∂ln x₃/∂(1/T))_w is positive for endothermic dissolution and that the Gibbs‑Duhem relation for the binary solvent holds: w₁ (∂ln a₁/∂w₁)_T + w₂ (∂ln a₂/∂w₁)_T = 0, where the solvent activities a₁, a₂ are obtained from the J‑A expression for the solute chemical potential via the Gibbs‑Duhem integration. Violations indicate over‑parameterization or experimental scatter.

Practical use: the extended J‑A model enables rapid prediction of the optimal cosolvent fraction for maximizing drug solubility at a target temperature (e.g., 310 K for oral dosage). It also provides the temperature derivative needed for van’t Hoff plots of the apparent enthalpy of solution in mixed solvents, which is a common examination topic in physical pharmacy and chemical engineering thermodynamics.

  • Assuming the Jouyban–Acree J_i parameters are temperature independent when the data span more than 30 K; always test the van’t Hoff extension.
  • Using the simple Debye–Hückel limiting law for activity coefficients in mixed‑salt brines above 1 mol kg⁻¹; the extended UNIQUAC or Pitzer models are required.
  • Neglecting the pressure dependence of the dielectric constant in the HKF model when P > 10 MPa; the Born term dominates the volume change.
  • Treating protein–ligand binding constants as concentration‑based equilibrium constants without correcting for osmotic non‑ideality at > 5 mg mL⁻¹ protein.
  • Applying the e‑NRTL model to IL–water systems without validating the predicted critical solution temperature against experimental LLE data.
  • Assuming the common‑ion approximation (τ_Na⁺‑Cl⁻ ≈ τ_K⁺‑Cl⁻) holds for all mixed‑electrolyte systems; cross‑interaction parameters can differ by > 30 %.
  • Using a single‑temperature Apelblat fit for pure‑solvent solubility and then extrapolating the J‑A model far outside the calibrated temperature range.
The advanced sections above integrate molecular‑level thermodynamic models with macroscopic phase‑equilibrium and colligative‑property measurements, providing the depth and quantitative rigor that competitive examinations increasingly demand.

Thermodynamics of Ionic Liquids as Solvents: Solubility and Phase Behavior

Ionic liquids (ILs) are salts that remain liquid below 100 °C, often at room temperature. Their negligible vapor pressure, wide liquidus range, and tunable polarity through cation–anion selection make them attractive solvents for gas capture, biphasic catalysis, and extraction of biomolecules. The solubility of a solute in an IL is governed by the same thermodynamic framework used for molecular solvents: the equality of chemical potentials between the solute in the IL phase and in a reference phase (usually an ideal gas or a pure solid). For gases, Henry’s law constant kH in an IL is defined as kH = fgas / xgas at infinite dilution, where f is fugacity and x mole fraction. Experimental kH values for CO₂, CH₄, and N₂ in imidazolium‑based ILs show a strong dependence on the anion: fluorinated anions (e.g., [NTf₂]⁻) give higher CO₂ solubility than halides because of weaker cation–anion interactions and larger free volume. Temperature dependence follows a van’t Hoff relation, ln kH = A + B/T, allowing estimation of the enthalpy of absorption.

For solid solutes, the mole‑fraction solubility x2 at temperature T is obtained from the equality μ2solid = μ2IL. Using the pure‑solid fugacity f2solid = P2sat exp[V2(P−P2sat)/RT] and the IL‑phase activity coefficient γ2, the solubility expression becomes x2 = f2solid / (γ2 f20,IL), where f20,IL is the standard‑state fugacity of the solute in the IL (often taken as the hypothetical pure‑liquid fugacity at system T, P). Predictive models for γ2 in ILs include COSMO‑RS, which uses quantum‑chemical σ‑profiles of the ions and solute, and the electrolyte‑NRTL (e‑NRTL) model, which treats the IL as a fully dissociated electrolyte with ion‑specific binary parameters. Both approaches have been validated for pharmaceuticals (e.g., ibuprofen, naproxen) in [C₄mim][NTf₂] and [C₄mim][BF₄] with average absolute deviations of 0.2–0.3 log units in x2.
