Phase behavior of binary IL–solvent systems often exhibits upper critical solution temperatures (UCST) or lower critical solution temperatures (LCST) depending on the hydrogen‑bonding ability of the molecular co‑solvent. For example, the system [C₄mim][NTf₂] + water shows an LCST near 320 K, while [C₄mim][BF₄] + ethanol displays a UCST. The critical composition shifts with the alkyl chain length on the imidazolium cation, reflecting the balance between Coulombic and dispersive forces. Accurate correlation of these liquid–liquid equilibria (LLE) requires activity‑coefficient models that capture both electrostatic (Debye–Hückel or Pitzer terms) and short‑range (NRTL, UNIQUAC) contributions. The e‑NRTL model, with temperature‑dependent binary parameters τij = aij + bij/T + cij ln T, is widely used in process simulators for IL‑based extraction columns.

Key practical implications: (1) Gas solubility in ILs can be enhanced by selecting anions with high fluorination and cations with short alkyl chains, but viscosity increases may limit mass‑transfer rates. (2) Solid solubility predictions for drug‑IL combinations enable rational design of IL‑based drug delivery vehicles. (3) LLE data for IL–water–organic ternary systems are essential for designing biphasic reaction–separation processes. Examiners often test the ability to choose the appropriate thermodynamic model (COSMO‑RS vs e‑NRTL) for a given IL–solute pair and to interpret the sign of the enthalpy of absorption from temperature‑dependent Henry’s constants.

Phase Equilibria in Supercritical Fluid Extraction: Cosolvent Effects and Modeling

Supercritical fluid extraction (SFE) most commonly employs carbon dioxide (CO₂) because of its moderate critical temperature (304.1 K) and pressure (7.38 MPa), low toxicity, and easy removal. Pure supercritical CO₂ (scCO₂) is a non‑polar solvent with limited solubility for polar or high‑molecular‑weight solutes. Adding a cosolvent (modifier) such as ethanol, methanol, or water dramatically increases solute solubility by enhancing the fluid’s polarity and hydrogen‑bonding capacity. The phase equilibria of the ternary system solute–CO₂–cosolvent are described by an equation of state (EOS) that can represent both the supercritical region and the liquid‑like densities of the mixture. The Peng–Robinson (PR) EOS with the Wong–Sandler mixing rules, combined with an activity‑coefficient model (e.g., NRTL) for the excess Gibbs energy, is a standard industrial choice. The solubility of a solid solute i in the supercritical phase is calculated from the equality of fugacities: yi φi P = xi γi fi0, where yi is the mole fraction in the supercritical phase, φi its fugacity coefficient from the EOS, xi the mole fraction in the solid (unity for pure solid), γi the activity coefficient in the solid phase (often taken as 1), and fi0 the pure‑solid fugacity at system T, P (including the Poynting correction).

The cosolvent concentration in the supercritical phase is typically 1–10 mol % and is itself determined by the vapor–liquid equilibrium of the CO₂–cosolvent binary at the extraction conditions. The presence of the cosolvent shifts the critical point of the mixture to higher pressures and temperatures, a phenomenon known as critical‑point elevation. Accurate prediction of this shift requires an EOS with a reliable mixing rule; the PR‑WS‑NRTL combination reproduces the critical locus for CO₂–ethanol within 2 % in pressure. For solutes that are weak acids or bases (e.g., phenolic compounds), the cosolvent also influences the acid–base equilibrium in the supercritical phase, which can be modeled by coupling the EOS with a reaction equilibrium constant expressed in terms of fugacities.

Experimental solubility data are often correlated with the Chrastil equation, ln ρs = k ln ρ + a/T + b, where ρs is the solute density in the supercritical phase, ρ the CO₂ density, and k, a, b empirical parameters. When a cosolvent is present, the Chrastil equation is extended by adding a term proportional to the cosolvent mole fraction ymod: ln ρs = k ln ρ + a/T + b + c ymod. This semi‑empirical form is useful for quick screening but lacks the thermodynamic consistency of a full EOS approach. In competitive examinations, candidates are expected to (i) write the fugacity‑equality condition for solid–supercritical equilibrium, (ii) explain how the cosolvent modifies the solute’s fugacity coefficient and the mixture critical point, and (iii) identify the limitations of density‑based correlations versus EOS‑based models.

Worked Example: Solubility Enhancement by Ethanol in scCO₂
A solid pharmaceutical compound (molar mass 300 g mol⁻¹) has a pure‑solid vapor pressure of 1.2 × 10⁻⁶ Pa at 313 K. Its solubility in pure scCO₂ at 313 K and 15 MPa is 2.5 × 10⁻⁴ mol mol⁻¹. When 5 mol % ethanol is added to the CO₂, the measured solubility rises to 1.1 × 10⁻³ mol mol⁻¹. Assuming the solid‑phase fugacity is unchanged and the activity coefficient of the solute in the supercritical phase is unity, calculate the ratio of the solute’s fugacity coefficient in the CO₂–ethanol mixture to that in pure CO₂ at the same T, P.
From the fugacity‑equality condition for a pure solid: yi φi P = fisolid. Since fisolid and P are identical in both experiments, the product yi φi is constant. Let subscript 1 denote pure CO₂ and 2 denote CO₂ + 5 % ethanol. Then y1 φ1 = y2 φ2. Rearranging, φ2/φ1 = y1/y2 = (2.5 × 10⁻⁴) / (1.1 × 10⁻³) = 0.227. Thus the fugacity coefficient of the solute in the cosolvent‑containing mixture is only 22.7 % of its value in pure CO₂, reflecting the strong enhancement of solute–fluid interactions due to ethanol.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the effect of a cosolvent on supercritical solubility, first write the solid–fluid fugacity equality. Identify which terms change (fugacity coefficient, mixture density, critical point) and which remain constant (pure‑solid fugacity, system pressure). Use the ratio method shown above to avoid unnecessary EOS calculations. Remember that the Chrastil correlation is empirical; if the problem provides EOS parameters, prefer the thermodynamic route.

Thermodynamics of Protein Crystallization: Phase Diagrams, Solubility, and Nucleation

Protein crystallization is a classic example of solid–liquid equilibrium in a complex aqueous electrolyte solution. The solubility of a protein depends on temperature, pH, ionic strength, and the concentration of precipitants (e.g., ammonium sulfate, polyethylene glycol). A typical protein phase diagram plots precipitant concentration versus protein concentration, delineating three regions: undersaturated (clear solution), metastable (supersaturated but no spontaneous nucleation), and labile (spontaneous nucleation). The solubility curve (binodal) separates the undersaturated and metastable zones; the supersolubility curve separates metastable and labile zones. The width of the metastable zone is a kinetic parameter that narrows as the driving force for nucleation increases.

The thermodynamic solubility Csat at a given condition is defined by the equality of the protein chemical potential in the crystal and in solution: μprotcrystal = μprotsolution. In solution, μprot = μprot0 + RT ln (γprot Cprot), where γprot is the activity coefficient that captures protein–protein and protein–cosolute interactions. The second virial coefficient B22 (in units of mL mol g⁻²) is directly related to γprot at low concentrations: ln γprot ≈ 2 B22 Cprot. Negative B22 indicates net attractive interactions and promotes crystallization; positive B22 indicates repulsion and favors solubility. Experimental determination of B22 by static light scattering is a standard screening tool.

Classical nucleation theory (CNT) gives the steady‑state homogeneous nucleation rate J = J0 exp(−ΔG*/kT), where the critical free‑energy barrier ΔG* = 16π γ³ v² / (3 Δμ²). Here γ is the crystal–solution interfacial tension, v the molecular volume of the protein in the crystal, and Δμ = kT ln (C/Csat) the chemical‑potential difference (supersaturation). Because proteins have large v and relatively low γ (≈ 0.5–2 mJ m⁻²), the nucleation barrier is highly sensitive to supersaturation. Heterogeneous nucleation on dust particles or container walls reduces the effective barrier by a geometric factor f(θ) = (2 + cos θ)(1 − cos θ)²/4, where θ is the contact angle.

Practical crystallization screens vary precipitant type and concentration, pH, and additives (e.g., ligands, detergents) to locate conditions that yield a metastable supersaturation sufficient for controlled nucleation but not so high as to cause amorphous precipitation. The phase diagram is constructed experimentally by measuring the solubility curve (equilibrating crystal with solution) and the supersolubility curve (detecting the onset of spontaneous nucleation via turbidity). Examiners often ask to interpret a given phase diagram, to calculate the supersaturation ratio from concentration data, or to explain how a change in pH alters the protein’s net charge and thus B22 and the solubility curve.

Modeling Solid‑Liquid Equilibria in Multicomponent Systems: The UNIFAC‑Dortmund Approach

The UNIFAC (UNIQUAC Functional‑group Activity Coefficients) method predicts activity coefficients in liquid mixtures from group‑contribution parameters. The original UNIFAC (Lyngby) uses a single set of group‑interaction parameters fitted mainly to vapor–liquid equilibrium (VLE) data. The Dortmund modification (UNIFAC‑Dortmund) introduces temperature‑dependent interaction parameters amn(T) = Amn + Bmn <T> + Cmn <T>² and a revised combinatorial term that better represents size and shape asymmetry, especially for polymer–solvent and solid–liquid systems. For solid–liquid equilibrium (SLE) of a multicomponent mixture, the solubility of each solid component i is obtained from xi γi = fisolid / fi0,liq, where γi is the liquid‑phase activity coefficient predicted by UNIFAC‑Dortmund, fisolid the pure‑solid fugacity (including the Poynting correction), and fi0,liq the hypothetical pure‑liquid fugacity of the subcooled liquid at the system temperature.

Application to pharmaceutical polymorphs: Different polymorphs of the same compound have distinct crystal lattice energies, reflected in different pure‑solid fugacities. UNIFAC‑Dortmund can predict the relative solubilities of polymorphs in various solvent mixtures, guiding solvent selection for polymorph‑specific crystallization. For example, the solubility ratio of Form I to Form II of a drug in ethanol–water (30 % w/w water) at 298 K can be predicted within 10 % of experimental values if the group‑interaction parameters for the drug’s functional groups (e.g., aromatic CH, carbonyl, amine) are available in the Dortmund database. The model also handles mixed‑solvent systems (e.g., water–ethanol–propylene glycol) without additional binary parameters, because the group‑contribution framework inherently captures cross‑interactions.

Limitations: UNIFAC‑Dortmund does not explicitly account for strong specific interactions such as hydrogen‑bonding cooperativity or ionic association. For electrolyte‑containing systems, the model must be coupled with a Debye–Hückel or Pitzer term for the long‑range electrostatic contribution (the so‑called UNIFAC‑Dortmund‑Pitzer hybrid). Moreover, the accuracy for high‑melting‑point solids (ΔHfus > 50 kJ mol⁻¹) can be limited by the uncertainty in the subcooled‑liquid heat‑capacity extrapolation. In examinations, candidates should be able to (i) write the SLE condition with UNIFAC‑Dortmund activity coefficients, (ii) explain the role of temperature‑dependent group parameters, and (iii) identify when a hybrid model is necessary.

Thermodynamics of Gas Hydrate Inhibition: Kinetic and Thermodynamic Inhibitor Mechanisms

Gas hydrates are crystalline inclusion compounds where water molecules form hydrogen‑bonded cages that trap guest molecules (e.g., methane, CO₂, H₂S). In pipelines, hydrate formation can block flow. Inhibition strategies are classified as thermodynamic (THI) or kinetic (KHI). Thermodynamic inhibitors (methanol, ethylene glycol, triethylene glycol) shift the hydrate equilibrium curve to lower temperatures (or higher pressures) by reducing the water activity aw in the aqueous phase. The equilibrium condition from the van der Waals–Platteeuw model is Δμw0 = RT Σν νν ln(1 − Σi θiν), where Δμw0 is the chemical‑potential difference between empty hydrate lattice and bulk water, νν the number of cages of type ν per water molecule, and θiν the occupancy of guest i in cage ν. Adding a THI lowers aw, which effectively increases Δμw0 (since Δμw = Δμw0 + RT ln aw), requiring a larger driving force (lower T or higher P) for hydrate stability. The shift can be quantified by the Hammerschmidt equation: ΔT = K m / (1 − m), where m is the molality of inhibitor and K a constant (≈ 1.86 K kg mol⁻¹ for methanol).

Kinetic inhibitors (e.g., polyvinylcaprolactam, poly(N‑vinylpyrrolidone)) do not shift the equilibrium curve; instead they adsorb onto nascent hydrate nuclei, increasing the induction time and reducing the growth rate. Their performance is evaluated by the induction time tind at a fixed subcooling ΔTsub = Teq − Top. The adsorption is often described by a Langmuir‑type isotherm, and the inhibition efficiency correlates with the polymer’s molecular weight, amide functionality, and cloud point. Anti‑agglomerants (AAs) are a third class: they allow hydrate crystals to form but disperse them as a fine slurry in the oil phase, preventing blockage. AAs are typically surfactants with a hydrophobic tail and a polar head that adsorbs on the hydrate surface.

Modeling combined THI/KHI effects requires coupling the thermodynamic equilibrium shift (via water activity) with a kinetic model for nucleation and growth. A common approach uses the Kashchiev–Firoozabadi nucleation rate expression modified by an inhibitor‑dependent interfacial tension γ(CKHI) = γ0 (1 − α CKHI), where α is an empirical constant. The growth rate is similarly reduced by a factor exp(−β CKHI). In field applications, the total inhibitor dosage is optimized by balancing the CAPEX of THI regeneration against the OPEX of KHI injection. Examination questions may ask to (i) derive the temperature shift from the Hammerschmidt equation for a given methanol concentration, (ii) explain why KHIs do not alter the phase envelope, or (iii) discuss the limitations of the van der Waals–Platteeuw model when both THI and KHI are present.

  • Assuming that the UNIFAC‑Dortmund model can accurately predict SLE for ionic solids without adding an electrostatic term.
  • Confusing the metastable zone width with the thermodynamic solubility curve in protein phase diagrams.
  • Using the simple Chrastil correlation for cosolvent‑enhanced SFE when the problem provides EOS parameters and expects a fugacity‑based calculation.
  • Treating kinetic hydrate inhibitors as if they shift the equilibrium curve; they only affect induction time and growth rate.
  • Neglecting the Poynting correction when calculating the pure‑solid fugacity for high‑pressure SLE predictions.
  • Applying the Hammerschmidt equation for thermodynamic inhibitors at concentrations above 30 wt % where the linear approximation fails.
  • Forgetting that the second virial coefficient B₂₂ is concentration‑dependent and cannot be treated as constant across the entire metastable zone.

Mastery of these advanced topics requires consistent practice with real data sets and a clear distinction between thermodynamic equilibrium shifts and kinetic phenomena.

Thermodynamics of Ionic Liquid–Water Mixtures: Activity Coefficient Models and Phase Behavior

Ionic liquids (ILs) are salts that remain liquid below 100 °C and exhibit negligible vapor pressure. When mixed with water they form highly non‑ideal solutions because of strong Coulombic interactions, hydrogen‑bonding networks, and large size asymmetry between the bulky organic cation (e.g., 1‑ethyl‑3‑methylimidazolium, [EMIM]⁺) and the small water molecule. Accurate prediction of phase equilibria (vapor‑liquid, liquid‑liquid, and solid‑liquid) requires activity‑coefficient models that can capture both short‑range specific interactions and long‑range electrostatic contributions.

The most widely used local‑composition models for IL–water systems are the Non‑Random Two‑Liquid (NRTL) and the Universal Quasi‑Chemical (UNIQUAC) equations. In the NRTL formulation the excess Gibbs energy is expressed as

Gᴱ/RT = Σᵢ xᵢ Σⱼ τⱼᵢ Gⱼᵢ xⱼ / Σₖ Gₖᵢ xₖ

where τⱼᵢ = (gⱼᵢ – gᵢᵢ)/RT and Gⱼᵢ = exp(–αⱼᵢ τⱼᵢ). The binary interaction parameters τⱼᵢ and non‑randomness factors αⱼᵢ are regressed from experimental VLE or LLE data. For IL–water mixtures the parameters are strongly temperature dependent; a common practice is to correlate τⱼᵢ = Aⱼᵢ + Bⱼᵢ/T + Cⱼᵢ ln T.

Because the electrostatic contribution dominates at low IL concentrations, many practitioners augment NRTL with a Debye–Hückel or Pitzer term. The combined excess Gibbs energy becomes Gᴱ = Gᴱ_NRTL + Gᴱ_DH, where the Debye–Hückel part for a 1:1 electrolyte is

Gᴱ_DH/RT = –(A_φ I^(3/2)) / (1 + b I^(1/2))

with I the ionic strength, A_φ the Debye–Hückel constant (function of temperature and dielectric constant of the mixture), and b an adjustable parameter. The dielectric constant of the mixture is often estimated by the Onsager or Kirkwood–Fröhlich equation using pure‑component dielectric constants and the mixture composition.

Liquid‑liquid equilibrium (LLE) in IL–water systems frequently exhibits a miscibility gap with an upper critical solution temperature (UCST). The binodal curve is obtained by solving the equality of component chemical potentials in the two liquid phases: μᵢ^α = μᵢ^β. Using the activity‑coefficient model, this translates to xᵢ^α γᵢ^α = xᵢ^β γᵢ^β for each component i. The critical point satisfies the additional conditions (∂²ΔG_mix/∂x²)_T,P = 0 and (∂³ΔG_mix/∂x³)_T,P = 0, which can be evaluated analytically from the NRTL‑DH expression.

Experimental validation shows that the NRTL‑DH hybrid reproduces water activity data for [EMIM][BF₄]–water within ±0.01 over the whole composition range at 298 K. For systems with strongly coordinating anions (e.g., [EMIM][OAc]) the UNIQUAC model with a coordination‑number correction often yields a better fit because it explicitly accounts for the difference in molecular surface area and volume between the IL ions and water.

In examinations, candidates should be able to (i) write the full expression for the activity coefficient of water in an IL–water mixture using the NRTL‑DH model, (ii) explain how the dielectric constant of the mixture influences the Debye–Hückel term, and (iii) sketch the expected binodal curve for a UCST system and indicate the critical composition.

Solubility of Pharmaceuticals in Supercritical CO₂: Co‑solvent Effects and Modeling

Supercritical carbon dioxide (scCO₂) is a green solvent widely used for extraction and particle formation of active pharmaceutical ingredients (APIs). Pure scCO₂, however, has a low polarity (dielectric constant ≈ 1.3 at 313 K, 10 MPa) and cannot dissolve many polar or hydrogen‑bonding drugs. Adding a co‑solvent (entrainer) such as ethanol, methanol, or water dramatically increases solubility by enhancing the fluid’s polarity and providing specific solute–solvent interactions.

The solubility of a solid in a supercritical fluid is governed by the equality of fugacities: fᵢ^solid = fᵢ^fluid. For the solid phase the fugacity is expressed as

fᵢ^solid = Pᵢ^sat φᵢ^sat exp[ Vᵢ^solid (P – Pᵢ^sat) / (RT) ]

where Pᵢ^sat is the sublimation (or vapor) pressure of the pure solid, φᵢ^sat the fugacity coefficient at saturation (≈ 1 for low pressures), and Vᵢ^solid the molar volume of the solid (Poynting correction). In the fluid phase the fugacity is yᵢ φᵢ P, with yᵢ the mole fraction solubility and φᵢ the fugacity coefficient obtained from an equation of state (EOS).

The most common EOS for scCO₂ + co‑solvent + solute systems is the Peng–Robinson (PR) EOS with the Wong–Sandler mixing rules combined with an activity‑coefficient model (e.g., NRTL) for the excess Gibbs energy. The Wong–Sandler rule enforces the low‑pressure limit of the EOS to match the activity‑coefficient model, ensuring thermodynamic consistency. The binary interaction parameters kᵢⱼ for CO₂–co‑solvent and co‑solvent–solute pairs are regressed from binary VLE data; the CO₂–solute kᵢⱼ is often obtained by fitting the ternary solubility data.

Co‑solvent enhancement is quantified by the solubility enhancement factor E = yᵢ (with co‑solvent) / yᵢ (pure CO₂). For many APIs, a co‑solvent mole fraction of 0.05–0.10 in the supercritical phase yields E values of 10–1000. The effect is strongly dependent on the co‑solvent’s hydrogen‑bond donor/acceptor ability, which can be correlated with the Kamlet–Taft parameters α (acidity), β (basicity), and π* (polarity). A linear solvation energy relationship (LSER) of the form

ln yᵢ = c₀ + c₁ α + c₂ β + c₃ π* + c₄ δ

where δ is the Hildebrand solubility parameter of the co‑solvent, often provides a predictive tool for new co‑solvents.

Experimental techniques include the static‑analytic method (equilibrium cell with sampling) and the dynamic flow method (continuous extraction with gravimetric or chromatographic quantification). The static method yields equilibrium data at well‑defined T and P but requires long equilibration times for low‑solubility drugs; the dynamic method is faster but must correct for axial dispersion and incomplete extraction.

In examinations, students should be able to (i) derive the solubility expression starting from fugacity equality, (ii) explain the role of the Poynting correction and when it can be neglected, (iii) describe how the Wong–Sandler mixing rule couples an EOS with an activity‑coefficient model, and (iv) calculate the enhancement factor for a given co‑solvent composition using a provided LSER correlation.

Worked Example: Solubility Enhancement with Ethanol Co‑solvent
At 313 K and 15 MPa, the mole‑fraction solubility of ibuprofen in pure scCO₂ is 2.5 × 10⁻⁵. Adding ethanol to the supercritical phase at a mole fraction of 0.08 raises the solubility to 1.2 × 10⁻³. Calculate the enhancement factor E and, assuming a linear LSER ln y = c₀ + c₁ α with c₁ = 4.2, estimate the change in the Kamlet–Taft acidity parameter α of the fluid caused by the ethanol addition.
Enhancement factor E = y_with / y_without = (1.2 × 10⁻³) / (2.5 × 10⁻⁵) = 48. The LSER gives ln y_with = c₀ + c₁ α_with and ln y_without = c₀ + c₁ α_without. Subtracting, ln(y_with / y_without) = c₁ (α_with – α_without). Thus α_with – α_without = ln E / c₁ = ln 48 / 4.2 ≈ 3.871 / 4.2 ≈ 0.92. The ethanol co‑solvent increases the effective acidity parameter of the supercritical fluid by approximately 0.92 units.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the solubility of a solid in scCO₂ with a co‑solvent, first write the fugacity‑equality expression and identify which terms are supplied (Pᵢ^sat, Vᵢ^solid, φᵢ from EOS). If the Poynting correction is not given, state the condition (P – Pᵢ^sat) Vᵢ^solid ≪ RT under which it can be omitted. For co‑solvent effects, remember that the enhancement factor is a ratio of mole fractions at identical T and P; you do not need the absolute solubility if only E is requested. Always check whether the problem provides binary interaction parameters for the EOS or expects you to use a given LSER correlation.

Thermodynamic Modeling of Electrolyte Solutions at High Temperatures and Pressures: The Helgeson–Kirkham–Flowers (HKF) Model

The Helgeson–Kirkham–Flowers (HKF) model is the standard framework for calculating standard‑state thermodynamic properties of aqueous ions and neutral species up to 1000 °C and 5 kbar. It extends the revised Helgeson–Kirkham (HK) equations of state by incorporating the Born solvation term and a non‑electrostatic “intrinsic” contribution that captures short‑range ion–water interactions. The model is essential for geochemical modeling (e.g., hydrothermal ore formation, nuclear waste repository performance) and for high‑temperature industrial processes such as supercritical water oxidation.

The standard‑state Gibbs energy of an aqueous species i at temperature T and pressure P is written as

Gᵢ°(T,P) = Gᵢ°(T_r,P_r) + ∫T_r^T Cₚᵢ° dT – T ∫T_r^T (Cₚᵢ°/T) dT + ∫P_r^P Vᵢ° dP

where the reference state (T_r = 298.15 K, P_r = 1 bar) properties are known from calorimetry. The heat‑capacity function Cₚᵢ°(T,P) and volume function Vᵢ°(T,P) are expressed as sums of a non‑solvation (intrinsic) part and a Born solvation part:

Cₚᵢ° = Cₚᵢ°_int + Cₚᵢ°_Born, Vᵢ° = Vᵢ°_int + Vᵢ°_Born

The Born functions depend on the dielectric constant of water ε(T,P) and the effective Born radius rᵢ of the ion. The dielectric constant is calculated from the Franck–Upton–Kell equation or the more recent Sverjensky et al. formulation, which remains accurate to 1000 °C and 5 kbar. The intrinsic heat capacity is parameterized as

Cₚᵢ°_int = a₁ + a₂/(T – θ)² + a₃/(T – θ) + a₄ T + a₅ T²

with θ = 228 K (the “collapse” temperature of water’s hydrogen‑bond network). The intrinsic volume uses a similar polynomial in pressure. The Born coefficients (ωᵢ, the Born coefficient) are fitted to experimental standard‑state data (ΔG_f°, ΔH_f°, S°, Cₚ°, V°) for each ion.

For neutral species the Born term is zero, and the HKF model reduces to the revised HK equations with the same intrinsic parameterization. The model also provides the standard‑state equilibrium constant K° for reactions via ΔG°_rxn = –RT ln K°, enabling calculation of speciation in high‑temperature brines.

Implementation in software such as SUPCRT92, GEMS, or PHREEQC uses a database of HKF parameters (a₁–a₅, c₁–c₄, ω, rᵢ) for hundreds of aqueous species. The user supplies T, P, and bulk composition; the code solves the mass‑action equations together with the activity‑coefficient model (often the extended Debye–Hückel or Pitzer model) to obtain individual ion activities.

Examination questions typically test the ability to (i) write the full expression for the standard‑state Gibbs energy of an ion including both intrinsic and Born contributions, (ii) explain the physical meaning of the Born coefficient ω and its relation to the effective ionic radius, (iii) describe how the dielectric constant of water influences the temperature and pressure dependence of the Born functions, and (iv) outline the steps required to compute the equilibrium constant for a mineral dissolution reaction at 400 °C and 1 kbar using the HKF database.

Phase Behavior of Mixed‑Solvent Electrolyte Systems: The Mixed‑Solvent Electrolyte (MSE) Model

Many industrial processes (e.g., CO₂ capture with aqueous amine blends, lithium‑ion battery electrolytes, nuclear fuel reprocessing) involve electrolytes dissolved in mixed organic–water solvents. The Mixed‑Solvent Electrolyte (MSE) model, developed by Wang and coworkers, provides a unified thermodynamic framework that simultaneously treats solvent–solvent, solvent–ion, and ion–ion interactions across the full composition range from pure water to pure organic solvent.

The MSE model expresses the excess Gibbs energy as a sum of three contributions:

Gᴱ = Gᴱ_solvent + Gᴱ_ion‑solvent + Gᴱ_ion‑ion

The solvent term Gᴱ_solvent is described by a local‑composition model (NRTL or UNIQUAC) using binary parameters for each solvent pair (e.g., water–methanol, water–ethylene glycol). The ion‑solvent term uses a Born‑type solvation model where the solvation Gibbs energy of ion i in the mixed solvent is

ΔG_solv,i = – (zᵢ² e² N_A) / (8π ε₀ rᵢ) (1 – 1/ε_mix)

with ε_mix the composition‑dependent dielectric constant of the solvent mixture, calculated from the pure‑component dielectric constants and the solvent‑solvent NRTL parameters via the Onsager–Kirkwood equation. The ion‑ion term adopts the Pitzer‑type virial expansion for the excess Gibbs energy of the electrolyte subsystem, but the Pitzer parameters (β⁽⁰⁾, β⁽¹⁾, C_φ) are made functions of the solvent composition to capture the changing ionic strength and dielectric environment.

A key feature of the MSE model is the “solvent‑composition‑dependent” Pitzer parameters, which are correlated as

β⁽⁰⁾(x_s) = β⁽⁰⁾_water + (β⁽⁰⁾_organic – β⁽⁰⁾_water) x_s + Δβ⁽⁰⁾ x_s (1 – x_s)

where x_s is the mole fraction of the organic solvent. This allows the model to reproduce the dramatic decrease in activity coefficients observed when water is replaced by a low‑dielectric solvent.

The MSE model has been validated for systems such as H₂O–MEA–CO₂, H₂O–LiCl–ethylene carbonate, and H₂O–NaNO₃–methanol up to 200 °C and 100 bar. It predicts vapor‑liquid equilibria, solid‑liquid equilibria (salt precipitation), and speciation (e.g., carbamate formation in amine‑CO₂ systems) with average absolute deviations in water activity below 0.02.

In an exam setting, students should be able to (i) write the total excess Gibbs energy expression for a ternary mixed‑solvent electrolyte system, (ii) explain how the dielectric constant of the mixed solvent enters the Born solvation term and why it is composition dependent, (iii) describe the procedure for regressing solvent‑dependent Pitzer parameters from binary and ternary data, and (iv) discuss the limitations of the MSE model when the organic solvent participates in chemical reaction (e.g., esterification) that changes the solvent composition during equilibrium calculations.

Thermodynamics of Gas Solubility in Deep Eutectic Solvents: COSMO‑RS Predictions

Deep eutectic solvents (DESs) are formed by mixing a hydrogen‑bond acceptor (typically a quaternary ammonium salt such as choline chloride) with a hydrogen‑bond donor (e.g., urea, glycerol, ethylene glycol) at a molar ratio that yields a large freezing‑point depression. Their negligible volatility, tunable polarity, and low cost make them attractive for gas capture (CO₂, H₂S, CH₄). Predicting gas solubility in DESs requires a model that can handle strong, specific hydrogen‑bonding interactions without relying on extensive experimental binary data.

The COSMO‑RS (Conductor‑like Screening Model for Real Solvents) method uses quantum‑chemical σ‑profiles of the individual DES components (salt ions and HBD molecules) to compute the chemical potential of a gas solute in the liquid mixture. The central equation for the residual chemical potential of solute i in a solvent mixture is

μᵢ^res = RT ln γᵢ^res = RT ∫ p_i(σ) [ μ_s(σ) – μ_s^ref(σ) ] dσ

where p_i(σ) is the σ‑profile of the solute (obtained from a COSMO calculation on the isolated gas molecule), μ_s(σ) is the σ‑potential of the solvent mixture, and μ_s^ref(σ) is the σ‑potential of a reference solvent (usually the pure solute liquid at the same T). The σ‑potential of the mixture is constructed from the σ‑profiles of the DES constituents weighted by their mole fractions, assuming ideal mixing of the σ‑profiles (the “ideal‑mixing” approximation). For DESs, the σ‑profiles of the cation, anion, and HBD are computed separately; the mixture σ‑profile is then

p_mix(σ) = Σ_k x_k p_k(σ)

where x_k are the mole fractions of the individual species in the DES (e.g., 0.33 choline⁺, 0.33 Cl⁻, 0.34 urea for a 1:2 choline chloride:urea DES). The COSMO‑RS calculation yields the activity coefficient γᵢ at infinite dilution; the Henry’s constant follows from Hᵢ = γᵢ^∞ Pᵢ^sat φᵢ^sat (with Pᵢ^sat the hypothetical vapor pressure of the gas at the system temperature).

Validation studies show that COSMO‑RS predicts CO₂ solubility in choline‑chloride‑based DESs within a factor of 2–3 without any fitted parameters. Accuracy improves when the “hydrogen‑bonding correction” (the HB‑term in the COSMO‑RS energy expression) is calibrated against a single experimental data point. The method also captures the effect of water contamination: adding water introduces a new σ‑profile that strongly competes for hydrogen‑bonding sites, typically reducing gas solubility.

Examination questions may ask candidates to (i) outline the steps required to generate σ‑profiles for the DES components using a quantum‑chemical package (geometry optimization, COSMO single‑point calculation), (ii) write the expression for the mixture σ‑profile and explain the ideal‑mixing assumption, (iii) derive the Henry’s constant from the COSMO‑RS activity coefficient at infinite dilution, and (iv) discuss why COSMO‑RS can predict the trend in CO₂ solubility across a series of DESs (e.g., choline chloride + urea vs. choline chloride + glycerol) without experimental binary interaction parameters.

  • Assuming the NRTL‑DH model can be applied to IL–water systems without regressing temperature‑dependent binary parameters.
  • Neglecting the Poynting correction when calculating solid fugacity for high‑pressure scCO₂ solubility, leading to systematic under‑prediction.
  • Using the standard HKF database for species that lack fitted Born coefficients (ω) and treating ω as zero.
  • Treating the mixed‑solvent dielectric constant as a simple linear mole‑fraction average instead of the Onsager–Kirkwood expression required by the MSE model.
  • Applying COSMO‑RS with the ideal‑mixing σ‑profile assumption to DESs that exhibit strong non‑ideal hydrogen‑bond network formation (e.g., eutectic points far from ideal mixing).
  • Confusing the Henry’s constant derived from COSMO‑RS (infinite‑dilution basis) with the finite‑concentration solubility obtained from an EOS‑based approach.
  • Forgetting that the Born solvation term in the HKF model requires the dielectric constant of water at the exact T and P of interest, not the 298 K value.

Mastery of these advanced thermodynamic models enables reliable prediction of phase equilibria in complex, industrially relevant fluid systems.

Thermodynamics of Ionic Liquid Mixtures: The e‑NRTL Model for Multi‑Component Systems

The electrolyte‑NRTL (e‑NRTL) framework extends the classical NRTL activity‑coefficient model to handle long‑range electrostatic forces in ionic liquids (ILs) and their mixtures with molecular solvents. The total excess Gibbs energy is written as a sum of a short‑range NRTL term and a long‑range Debye‑Hückel term: Gex = Gex,NRTL + Gex,DH. The NRTL part uses binary interaction parameters τij and non‑randomness factors αij for every pair of species (cation–anion, cation–solvent, anion–solvent, solvent–solvent). The Debye‑Hückel contribution employs the Pitzer‑Debye‑Hückel expression with an effective ionic strength calculated from the mole fractions of the ionic species. Temperature dependence of τij is usually correlated as τij = aij + bij/T + cij ln T, allowing VLE and LLE predictions over wide temperature ranges. A key practical point is that the cation and anion are treated as separate components; the overall IL mole fraction is split according to the stoichiometric ratio (e.g., 0.5 for a 1:1 salt). When fitting binary data, the anion–cation τ parameters are often fixed from pure‑IL density or conductivity data, while the ion–solvent parameters are regressed against VLE or solubility measurements. The model has been successfully applied to imidazolium‑based ILs mixed with water, alcohols, and CO₂, reproducing both phase‑split envelopes and gas‑loading isotherms without invoking association schemes. Limitations arise when the IL exhibits strong hydrogen‑bond networking that cannot be captured by pairwise τ parameters; in such cases a third‑order interaction term or a COSMO‑RS σ‑profile correction is added.

Thermodynamics of Microemulsion Phase Behavior: The HLD‑NAC Model

The Hydrophilic‑Lipophilic Difference (HLD) – Net Average Curvature (NAC) model provides a unified thermodynamic description of microemulsion phase behavior for surfactant‑oil‑water systems. The HLD equation for a non‑ionic surfactant is HLD = ln(S) – k·EACN + σ – a·(T – Tref), where S is the salinity (wt % NaCl), EACN is the equivalent alkane carbon number of the oil, σ is the surfactant characteristic curvature, a is the temperature coefficient, and Tref is a reference temperature (usually 25 °C). For ionic surfactants the salinity term is replaced by ln(Cs) where Cs is the surfactant concentration. The NAC is related to HLD by NAC = HLD / (2·ξ), where ξ is the surfactant packing parameter. When HLD = 0 the system forms a bicontinuous middle phase (Type III); HLD < 0 yields oil‑in‑water (Type I) and HLD > 0 yields water‑in‑oil (Type II) microemulsions. The model predicts the solubilization ratios of oil and water in the middle phase through the empirical relations Voil = koil·|NAC|−1 and Vwater = kwater·|NAC|−1. Temperature, salinity, oil chain length, and surfactant structure all shift HLD linearly, enabling rapid formulation screening. The HLD‑NAC framework has been extended to mixed surfactants by using a mole‑fraction weighted average of σ and a, and to polymer‑surfactant systems by adding a polymer‑induced curvature term. Its main limitation is the assumption of a single, composition‑independent packing parameter; near the phase‑inversion temperature the curvature can become composition dependent, requiring a full free‑energy minimization with a bending‑elasticity term.

Worked Example: Calculating HLD for a Formulation
A formulation contains 2 wt % NaCl, an oil with EACN = 10, a non‑ionic surfactant with σ = 2.5 and a = 0.05 °C⁻¹, at T = 40 °C (Tref = 25 °C). Compute the HLD and identify the expected microemulsion type.

Step 1: Convert salinity to weight fraction: S = 2 wt % = 0.02. ln(S) = ln(0.02) = –3.912.

Step 2: Evaluate the temperature term: a·(T – Tref) = 0.05·(40 – 25) = 0.75.

Step 3: Assemble HLD: HLD = ln(S) – k·EACN + σ – a·(T – Tref). For a typical non‑ionic surfactant k ≈ 0.17. Thus –k·EACN = –0.17·10 = –1.7.

HLD = –3.912 – 1.7 + 2.5 – 0.75 = –3.862.

Since HLD < 0, the system is predicted to form a Type I (oil‑in‑water) microemulsion at 40 °C.

Exam‑Day Strategy
Memorize the HLD equation and the physical meaning of each term (salinity, oil EACN, surfactant curvature, temperature). Practice converting between HLD and NAC, and be ready to state the phase type for a given sign of HLD. A quick mental check: increasing salinity or decreasing temperature drives HLD negative (Type I), while increasing oil chain length or temperature drives HLD positive (Type II).

Thermodynamics of Protein Crystallization: Phase Diagrams, Nucleation, and Growth

Protein crystallization is governed by the same thermodynamic principles that describe solid–liquid equilibria, but the large molecular size and anisotropic interactions introduce distinctive features. The solubility curve (concentration vs. temperature) defines the boundary between the undersaturated (clear solution) and supersaturated (metastable) regions. In a typical phase diagram, the metastable zone lies between the solubility curve and the nucleation (or supersolubility) curve; spontaneous nucleation occurs only beyond the latter. The driving force for nucleation is the chemical potential difference Δμ = RT ln(S), where S = C/Csat is the supersaturation ratio. Classical nucleation theory gives the critical nucleus size r* = 2γVm/Δμ and the nucleation rate J = J₀ exp(–ΔG*/kT) with ΔG* = 16πγ³Vm²/(3Δμ²). Protein–protein interactions are often described by a second virial coefficient B₂; a negative B₂ indicates net attraction and correlates with a narrow metastable zone. pH, ionic strength, and precipitant type (e.g., PEG, ammonium sulfate) shift the solubility curve by altering the protein’s net charge and the solvent dielectric constant. The Hofmeister series ranks ions by their ability to salt‑out or salt‑in proteins, which can be rationalized through preferential interaction parameters Γ23 (protein–ion) and Γ33 (ion–ion). Once nuclei surpass r*, growth proceeds by diffusion‑limited or surface‑integration mechanisms; the growth rate G is often expressed as G = kg (S – 1)g. Impurities and additives can adsorb on specific crystal faces, modifying the habit and the effective interfacial tension γ. Modern high‑throughput screening maps the phase diagram by varying precipitant concentration and pH, and the resulting “crystallization phase diagram” is used to design seeding strategies that bypass the stochastic nucleation step.

Thermodynamics of Gas Solubility in Polymer Membranes: Dual‑Mode Sorption Model

The dual‑mode sorption model reconciles the observed non‑linear gas sorption isotherms in glassy polymers by postulating two distinct sorption populations: Henry’s law dissolution in the dense polymer matrix and Langmuir‑type hole‑filling in excess free‑volume microvoids. The total concentration C (cm³(STP)/cm³ polymer) at pressure p is C = kD·p + (CH·b·p)/(1 + b·p), where kD is the Henry’s law constant (cm³(STP)/cm³·atm), CH is the hole saturation constant (cm³(STP)/cm³), and b is the hole affinity constant (atm⁻¹). At low pressures the Langmuir term is linear (C ≈ (kD + CHb)p), giving an apparent solubility higher than the equilibrium Henry’s law value. As pressure increases, the holes saturate and the isotherm approaches the Henry’s law line. The model parameters are obtained by fitting experimental sorption data; kD correlates with the polymer’s fractional free volume, while CH and b reflect the size and energy distribution of microvoids. For gas mixtures, competitive Langmuir sorption is used: each gas i occupies a fraction of the holes, leading to coupled equations Ci = kD,ipi + (CH·bipi)/(1 + Σ bjpj). This coupling explains plasticization (e.g., CO₂‑induced swelling) where the penetrant increases the hole affinity for itself and other gases. The dual‑mode framework is the basis for the solution‑diffusion model of membrane permeability: Pi = Di·Si, where the solubility coefficient Si = Ci/pi is pressure dependent. Accurate prediction of mixed‑gas separation performance therefore requires simultaneous fitting of pure‑gas isotherms and mixed‑gas permeation data.

Thermodynamics of Reactive Distillation: Coupled Reaction‑VLE Equilibria

Reactive distillation combines chemical reaction and vapor‑liquid equilibrium in a single stage, exploiting the removal of products to shift equilibrium‑limited reactions toward completion. The thermodynamic description requires simultaneous satisfaction of the reaction equilibrium constant Kr = Π aiνi and the VLE relations yiP = xiγiPisat for each component i. For a generic esterification A + B ⇌ C + D (e.g., acetic acid + ethanol ⇌ ethyl acetate + water), the liquid‑phase activities ai = xiγi are computed with an activity‑coefficient model (NRTL, UNIQUAC, or e‑NRTL for aqueous systems). The reaction equilibrium constant is temperature dependent via the van’t Hoff equation d ln Kr/dT = ΔHr°/RT². In a reactive stage, the material balances include the reaction extent ξ: ni = ni,0 + νiξ. The phase‑equilibrium condition couples ξ to the vapor composition because the vapor phase removes products (often the ester and water) at different rates. A reactive azeotrope occurs when the reaction equilibrium composition coincides with a VLE azeotrope, fixing the liquid composition regardless of stage holdup. Design calculations use the concept of “reactive residue curve maps” where the vector field is defined by the combined reaction and distillation driving forces. The feasible product compositions are bounded by the reactive distillation lines that connect reactive azeotropes and pure‑component vertices. Water removal (e.g., by a side‑stream decanter) effectively reduces the water activity, increasing Kr·awater and pushing conversion higher. The thermodynamic consistency of the model is verified by the Gibbs‑Duhem equation applied to the reactive system: Σ xi d ln γi = 0 at constant T, P, and ξ.

  • Confusing the e‑NRTL binary parameters for ion–solvent pairs with those for solvent–solvent pairs when fitting multicomponent IL mixtures.
  • Assuming the HLD equation is valid for all surfactant classes without adjusting the salinity term for ionic surfactants.
  • Neglecting the temperature dependence of the protein second virial coefficient B₂ when estimating the metastable zone width.
  • Treating the dual‑mode Langmuir capacity CH as pressure independent in mixed‑gas sorption calculations.
  • Overlooking the shift in reactive azeotrope composition caused by non‑ideal activity coefficients in reactive distillation design.
  • Using a single set of NRTL parameters for both reaction and separation steps without verifying Gibbs‑Duhem consistency for the reactive mixture.
  • Review these traps before the exam to avoid common misapplications of advanced solution thermodynamics.

Thermodynamics of Solid–Liquid Equilibria in Multicomponent Systems: The Schröder–van Laar Equation and Its Extensions

The Schröder–van Laar equation provides the fundamental relationship between the solubility of a pure solid component i in a liquid mixture and the thermodynamic properties of the solid and liquid phases. For a binary system the equation reads

ln xi = −ΔHfus,i/R · (1/T − 1/Tfus,i) + ln γi

where xi is the mole fraction of component i in the liquid at equilibrium, ΔHfus,i the molar enthalpy of fusion, Tfus,i the melting temperature of the pure solid, γi the activity coefficient of i in the liquid, and R the gas constant. The derivation assumes that the solid phase is pure i (no solid solution), that the heat capacity difference between solid and liquid is negligible over the temperature range of interest, and that the pressure effect on the solid fugacity is small (Poynting correction omitted).

In multicomponent mixtures the equation is applied to each solid‑forming component simultaneously. The set of equations

ln xi = −ΔHfus,i/R · (1/T − 1/Tfus,i) + ln γi (i = 1…k)

must be solved together with the constraint Σ xi = 1 for the liquid phase. The activity coefficients γi are obtained from a suitable excess‑Gibbs‑energy model (NRTL, UNIQUAC, Wilson, or e‑NRTL for electrolyte‑containing systems). This framework predicts eutectic compositions and temperatures, solid‑solution boundaries, and the effect of a third component (e.g., an antisolvent) on the solubility of a target solute.

Extensions of the basic equation address its limiting assumptions. The heat‑capacity correction adds a term ΔCp,i/R · [ln(T/Tfus,i) + (Tfus,i/T − 1)] to the right‑hand side, where ΔCp,i = Cp,liq − Cp,sol. The Poynting correction accounts for pressure dependence of the solid fugacity: exp[Vs,i(P − Psat)/RT], with Vs,i the molar volume of the solid. When solid solutions form, the solid‑phase activity ais replaces the pure‑solid reference, leading to ln (xiγi) = ln ais − ΔGfus,i/RT. The resulting equations are the basis for modern process simulators that compute solid–liquid equilibrium (SLE) in pharmaceutical crystallization, hydrate formation, and mineral processing.

Practical use requires reliable pure‑component fusion data (ΔHfus, Tfus, ΔCp) and a validated liquid‑phase activity‑coefficient model. Parameter estimation for the liquid model is often performed by regressing binary VLE or LLE data; the same parameters are then used in SLE predictions. Consistency checks such as the Gibbs‑Duhem integration for the liquid phase and the comparison of predicted versus experimental eutectic points are essential before the model is trusted for design.

Thermodynamics of Protein Crystallization: Phase Diagrams, Nucleation Kinetics, and the Role of Precipitants

Protein crystallization is governed by the same thermodynamic principles that describe small‑molecule solubility, but the large molecular size, conformational flexibility, and strong specific interactions make the phase behavior exceptionally rich. The solubility curve of a protein in a given precipitant (e.g., ammonium sulfate, polyethylene glycol, or salt) is typically represented in a temperature–concentration (T–C) or precipitant‑concentration–protein‑concentration (Cppt–Cprot) diagram. The curve separates three regions: undersaturated (clear solution), metastable (supersaturated but nucleation‑free), and labile (spontaneous nucleation). The width of the metastable zone is a key practical parameter; it determines the operating window for controlled crystal growth.

The equilibrium solubility Csat can be described by a modified van’t Hoff relation that incorporates the preferential interaction coefficient Γ23 (protein–precipitant) and the protein–protein second virial coefficient B22. A widely used semi‑empirical expression is

ln Csat = ln C0 − K · Cppt + α · Cppt²

where C0 is the solubility in the absence of precipitant, K reflects the salting‑out strength (Hofmeister series), and α accounts for curvature at high precipitant concentrations. The second virial coefficient B22 is directly related to the slope of the osmotic pressure versus concentration curve and serves as a predictor of crystallization propensity: moderately negative B22 (≈ −1 × 10⁻⁴ mol mL g⁻²) correlates with successful crystallization, while strongly negative values lead to amorphous precipitation.

Nucleation kinetics are described by classical nucleation theory (CNT) adapted for proteins. The steady‑state nucleation rate J is

J = A exp(−ΔG*/kBT)

with the critical free‑energy barrier ΔG* = 16πγ³v²/3(Δμ)², where γ is the crystal–solution interfacial tension, v the molecular volume in the crystal, and Δμ = kBT ln S the chemical‑potential difference (S = C/Csat the supersaturation ratio). Protein‑specific factors — anisotropic patchy interactions, conformational entropy loss upon ordering, and the presence of heterogeneous nucleation sites — cause the effective γ to be much larger than for small molecules, making nucleation extremely sensitive to supersaturation.

Precipitants act by reducing the protein’s chemical potential in solution. Salts follow the Hofmeister series (e.g., (NH₄)₂SO₄ > NaCl > NaSCN for salting‑out), which can be rationalized by the preferential exclusion or binding of ions to the protein surface. Polymers such as PEG exert depletion attraction: the excluded volume of polymer coils creates an effective attractive potential between protein molecules. The phase diagram therefore shifts with precipitant type, pH, ionic strength, and temperature. Mapping the diagram experimentally (e.g., by the hanging‑drop vapor‑diffusion method) and fitting the solubility curve with the above expressions enables rational design of crystallization screens and optimization of crystal size and quality for structural biology.

Constructing a Protein Solubility Phase Diagram
A protein exhibits a solubility of 12 mg mL⁻¹ in 1.0 M (NH₄)₂SO₄ at 20 °C and 4 mg mL⁻¹ in 2.0 M (NH₄)₂SO₄ at the same temperature. Assuming the semi‑empirical relation ln Csat = ln C0 − K · Cppt + α · Cppt² holds, determine C0, K, and α. Then predict the solubility at 1.5 M (NH₄)₂SO₄.
Convert concentrations to natural logarithms: ln 12 = 2.4849, ln 4 = 1.3863. Set up two equations: 2.4849 = ln C0 − K·1.0 + α·1.0² 1.3863 = ln C0 − K·2.0 + α·4.0 Subtract the first from the second: −1.0986 = −K·1.0 + α·3.0 → K = 1.0986 + 3α. Insert K into the first equation: 2.4849 = ln C0 − (1.0986 + 3α) + α → ln C0 = 2.4849 + 1.0986 + 2α = 3.5835 + 2α. A third datum is needed to fix α; assume literature value α ≈ 0.05 M⁻² for (NH₄)₂SO₄. Then K = 1.0986 + 0.15 = 1.2486 M⁻¹, ln C0 = 3.5835 + 0.10 = 3.6835 → C0 ≈ 39.7 mg mL⁻¹. Predict at 1.5 M: ln Csat = 3.6835 − 1.2486·1.5 + 0.05·2.25 = 3.6835 − 1.8729 + 0.1125 = 1.9231 → Csat ≈ 6.84 mg mL⁻¹.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a protein crystallization question supplies two solubility points, treat the semi‑empirical equation as a system of two equations in three unknowns. The exam will either give α explicitly or ask you to assume a typical value (0.04–0.06 M⁻² for common salts). Always state the assumption before solving. For nucleation‑rate comparisons, remember that J scales exponentially with (ln S)⁻²; a modest increase in supersaturation can raise J by orders of magnitude.

Thermodynamics of Membrane Separation: Solution‑Diffusion Model, Facilitated Transport, and Mixed‑Gas Plasticization

The solution‑diffusion model remains the cornerstone for describing gas permeation through dense polymer membranes. The permeability Pi of component i is the product of its diffusion coefficient Di and its solubility coefficient Si: Pi = Di Si. The solubility coefficient is defined as the equilibrium concentration Ci in the polymer divided by the upstream partial pressure pi: Si = Ci/pi. For gases that obey Henry’s law in the polymer, Si is constant; for condensable gases (CO₂, H₂S, hydrocarbons) the dual‑mode sorption model (Henry + Langmuir) captures the pressure‑dependent solubility.

Facilitated transport membranes incorporate a mobile or fixed carrier that reversibly reacts with a target gas (e.g., CO₂ + carrier ⇌ CO₂‑carrier complex). The overall flux Ji becomes the sum of the solution‑diffusion flux and the carrier‑mediated flux. For a mobile carrier with concentration Cc and reaction equilibrium constant Kr, the facilitated flux is

Jfac = Dc Cc Kr pi / (1 + Kr pi) · Δpi/l

where Dc is the carrier diffusion coefficient and l the membrane thickness. The carrier saturation at high pi limits the maximum facilitated flux, leading to a permeability that decreases with increasing feed pressure — a hallmark of facilitated transport.

Mixed‑gas plasticization is a critical phenomenon for CO₂‑rich feeds. Sorption of CO₂ swells the polymer matrix, increasing the fractional free volume and thereby enhancing the diffusion coefficients of all gases. In the dual‑mode framework this appears as an increase in the Langmuir capacity CH and affinity b for CO₂, and a concomitant rise in the Henry’s law constant kD for the other components. The competitive Langmuir expression for a binary mixture is

Ci = kD,i pi + (CH bi pi) / (1 + b1p1 + b2p2)

Plasticization is modeled by letting CH and bi become functions of the total penetrant concentration (or the CO₂ concentration). The resulting coupled sorption‑diffusion equations must be solved numerically to predict mixed‑gas selectivity α12 = (P1/P2)mix. Accurate prediction requires simultaneous fitting of pure‑gas isotherms, pure‑gas permeation, and mixed‑gas permeation data, because the plasticization parameters cannot be extracted from single‑gas measurements alone.

Design of industrial membrane units (e.g., natural‑gas sweetening, hydrogen recovery) uses the solution‑diffusion model embedded in a cross‑flow or counter‑current module model. The stage cut θ, pressure ratio r = pperm/pfeed, and membrane area A are optimized to meet product purity and recovery specifications while minimizing compression energy. The thermodynamic consistency of the sorption model (Gibbs‑Duhem integration for the polymer‑penetrant mixture) is a prerequisite for reliable process simulation.

Thermodynamics of Liquid–Liquid Extraction: Distribution Coefficients, Selectivity, and Phase‑Forming Component Effects

Liquid–liquid extraction (LLE) separates components based on their differential partitioning between two immiscible or partially miscible liquid phases. The distribution coefficient (partition ratio) of solute i is defined as Di = xiI/xiII (mole‑fraction basis) or Ki = ciI/ciII (concentration basis). The selectivity (separation factor) for a pair of solutes i and j is βij = Di/Dj. High βij and a favorable Di (typically 0.1 < Di < 10) are required for an economical extraction.

The equilibrium tie‑line data for a ternary system (solute + carrier + solvent) are correlated with activity‑coefficient models such as NRTL or UNIQUAC. The equilibrium condition for each component k is

xkI γkI = xkII γkII

where the activity coefficients γk depend on the local composition of each phase. The binodal curve (phase envelope) is obtained by solving the equality of component chemical potentials together with the material‑balance constraints. The plait point (critical point of the miscibility gap) marks the composition where the two phases become identical; near the plait point the distribution coefficients approach unity and selectivity collapses.

Phase‑forming components (e.g., salts in aqueous two‑phase systems, or polymers in polymer‑salt systems) dramatically shift the binodal curve. In aqueous two‑phase extraction (ATPE) the phase diagram is often expressed in terms of polymer concentration versus salt concentration. The tie‑line length (TLL) correlates with the driving force for partitioning; a longer TLL generally yields higher D for hydrophobic proteins. The partition coefficient of a protein can be modeled by the Albertsson equation

ln Kp = a + b · TLL + c · (pH − pI) + d · I

where a, b, c, d are empirical constants, pI the isoelectric point, and I the ionic strength. This semi‑empirical form captures the combined effects of excluded volume, electrostatic interactions, and hydrophobic surface patches.

Process design uses the extraction factor E = D · (S/F) (solvent‑to‑feed flow ratio) and the number of theoretical stages N from the Kremser equation for counter‑current cascades. For a target recovery R, the required stages are

N = ln[(R · (E − 1) + 1)/E] / ln E

When the equilibrium is non‑linear (D varies with composition), stage‑wise calculations with the actual tie‑line data or a thermodynamic model are necessary. The choice of solvent is guided by the Hildebrand/Hansen solubility parameters: a good solvent for the solute should have a δ value close to that of the solute, while being far from the carrier’s δ to ensure phase split. Environmental and safety constraints (volatility, toxicity, biodegradability) further narrow the solvent list.

Thermodynamics of Electrolyte Solutions at High Ionic Strength: The Davies Equation, Extended Pitzer Models, and Activity‑Coefficient Consistency

At ionic strengths I > 0.1 mol kg⁻¹ the Debye–Hückel limiting law fails, and more sophisticated expressions are required to compute mean ionic activity coefficients γ±. The Davies equation extends the Debye–Hückel form by adding an empirical linear term in I:

log₁₀ γ± = −A z₊ z₋ [√I/(1 + √I) − 0.3 I]

where A ≈ 0.509 kg¹ᐟ² mol⁻¹ᐟ² at 25 °C, z₊ and z₋ are the cation and anion charges. The Davies equation is simple, requires only the ionic strength, and works reasonably up to I ≈ 0.5 mol kg⁻¹ for 1:1 electrolytes, but it cannot capture specific ion‑pair interactions or the asymmetry between cations and anions.

The Pitzer model provides a rigorous virial‑expansion framework for the excess Gibbs energy of electrolyte solutions. The molal activity coefficient of an electrolyte MX is

ln γMX = |z₊ z₋| fγ + m (2ν₊ ν₋/ν) BMXγ + m² (2ν₊ ν₋/ν)² CMXγ + …

where fγ is the Debye–Hückel term, m the molality, ν₊, ν₋ the stoichiometric numbers, ν = ν₊ + ν₋, and BMXγ, CMXγ are temperature‑dependent binary and ternary interaction parameters. For mixed‑electrolyte systems the model includes cross‑terms θij (cation‑cation or anion‑anion) and ψijk (triplet interactions). The extended Pitzer model (e.g., Harvie–Møller–Weare) adds parameters for ion‑pair formation and neutral‑species interactions, enabling accurate prediction of solubilities in brines up to saturation (I > 10 mol kg⁻¹).

Thermodynamic consistency demands that the activity‑coefficient model satisfy the Gibbs‑Duhem equation at constant T and P: Σ mi d ln γi = 0. For the Pitzer formalism this condition is built into the parameterization procedure; however, when parameters are regressed from separate data sets (e.g., osmotic coefficients from isopiestic measurements and enthalpies from calorimetry), inconsistencies can arise. The Herington and Van Ness tests, originally developed for VLE models, can be adapted to electrolyte data by checking the integral consistency of osmotic coefficients φ versus composition.

Specific ion interaction theory (SIT) offers a simpler alternative for moderate ionic strengths (I ≤ 3 mol kg⁻¹). It expresses the activity coefficient as

log₁₀ γi = −zi² D + Σ ε(i,j) mj

where D is the Debye–Hückel term and ε(i,j) are binary interaction coefficients. SIT parameters are tabulated for many ion pairs and are widely used in geochemical modeling (e.g., PHREEQC). The choice among Davies, SIT, and Pitzer depends on the required accuracy, the ionic strength range, and the availability of reliable interaction parameters for the specific electrolyte mixture.

  • Assuming the Davies equation remains accurate above I = 0.5 mol kg⁻¹ for multivalent electrolytes.
  • Neglecting the ternary Pitzer parameter CMX when modeling concentrated brines, leading to systematic under‑prediction of activity coefficients.
  • Using a single set of binary Pitzer parameters for both cation‑anion and cation‑cation interactions without verifying the Gibbs‑Duhem consistency of the combined dataset.
  • Treating the protein second virial coefficient B₂₂ as constant across the entire precipitant concentration range in crystallization screening.
  • Ignoring the pressure‑dependent plasticization of the Langmuir capacity CH in mixed‑gas membrane permeation calculations.
  • Assuming the distribution coefficient D is independent of solute loading in liquid‑liquid extraction design, which fails near the plait point.
  • Applying the Schröder–van Laar equation without the heat‑capacity correction for solids with large ΔCp (e.g., pharmaceuticals with polymorphic transitions).

Thermodynamic Modeling of Mixed‑Solvent Electrolyte Solutions with the e‑NRTL Framework

The electrolyte‑NRTL (e‑NRTL) model extends the classical NRTL equation by adding a long‑range Debye–Hückel term and a Born solvation contribution, allowing simultaneous description of short‑range molecular interactions and long‑range electrostatic forces in mixed‑solvent electrolyte systems. The excess Gibbs energy is written as a sum of three parts: Gex = Gex,SR + Gex,LR + Gex,Born. The short‑range term uses the standard NRTL binary interaction parameters τij and non‑randomness factors αij for every pair of species (solvent‑solvent, solvent‑ion, ion‑ion). The long‑range term adopts the Pitzer‑Debye–Hückel expression with a concentration‑dependent dielectric constant εmix that is calculated from the solvent composition using a mixing rule such as εmix = Σ xk εk. The Born term accounts for the free‑energy change of transferring an ion from a reference dielectric (usually water) to the mixed solvent and is proportional to (1/εmix – 1/εref) zi².

Parameter estimation proceeds in a hierarchical fashion. First, pure‑solvent NRTL parameters are regressed from binary vapor‑liquid equilibrium (VLE) data. Next, solvent‑ion parameters are fitted to mean ionic activity coefficients measured in single‑solvent electrolyte solutions (e.g., NaCl in water, NaCl in methanol). Finally, ion‑ion parameters are adjusted using mixed‑solvent electrolyte data such as osmotic coefficients or solubility products. Because the model contains many adjustable parameters, regularization (e.g., Bayesian priors) is often employed to avoid over‑fitting. The e‑NRTL framework has been successfully applied to systems such as H₂O‑CH₃OH‑NaCl, H₂O‑CH₃CN‑LiBr, and CO₂‑H₂O‑NaCl at elevated pressures, reproducing experimental activity coefficients within ±3 % up to ionic strengths of 6 mol kg⁻¹.

A practical advantage of e‑NRTL is its compatibility with commercial process simulators (Aspen Plus, gPROMS) where the model is implemented as a built‑in property method. Users must supply the binary τ and α parameters for all pairs, the Debye–Hückel Aφ parameter (function of temperature and dielectric constant), and the Born radii for each ion. The model predicts not only activity coefficients but also enthalpies of mixing, heat capacities, and vapor‑phase fugacities, enabling rigorous design of extraction, crystallization, and electrodialysis units operating with mixed solvents.

Limitations arise when specific chemical association (hydrogen‑bond complexes, ion‑pair formation) dominates. In such cases the e‑NRTL short‑range term may be insufficient, and a chemical‑equilibrium sub‑model (e.g., association constants for ion pairs) must be coupled. Moreover, the dielectric‑constant mixing rule assumes ideal mixing of polarizabilities; for highly non‑ideal solvent mixtures (e.g., water‑ionic liquid) a more sophisticated εmix correlation (e.g., Onsager‑Kirkwood) improves accuracy. Despite these caveats, e‑NRTL remains the most versatile thermodynamic engine for mixed‑solvent electrolyte processes in both academic research and industrial flowsheeting.

Polymorphic Transitions and Their Influence on Solid Solubility Predictions

Polymorphism — the existence of multiple crystal structures for the same chemical entity — introduces a thermodynamic hierarchy of solid phases, each with its own lattice energy, heat capacity, and molar volume. The stable polymorph at a given temperature and pressure is the one with the lowest Gibbs free energy G = H – TS. Because solubility is governed by the equality of chemical potentials between solid and dissolved species, the polymorph that is thermodynamically stable under the solution conditions dictates the equilibrium solubility. Metastable polymorphs can persist kinetically, exhibiting higher apparent solubilities (the “spring‑and‑parachute” phenomenon) until they convert to the stable form.

The temperature dependence of polymorph stability is captured by the Clapeyron equation applied to the solid‑solid transition: dP/dT = ΔStr/ΔVtr. For most organic pharmaceuticals the transition is enantiotropic (reversible with temperature) or monotropic (one form always more stable). Enantiotropic systems possess a transition temperature Ttr where the two polymorphs have equal G. Below Ttr the low‑temperature form is stable; above it the high‑temperature form prevails. The solubility curves of the two polymorphs intersect at Ttr, producing a characteristic “crossover” in the van’t Hoff plot (ln x vs 1/T). Ignoring this crossover leads to systematic errors in extrapolated solubilities, especially when data are collected only on one side of Ttr.

Quantitative prediction requires the molar enthalpy of fusion ΔHfus and heat‑capacity difference ΔCp for each polymorph. The Schröder–van Laar equation extended with a heat‑capacity correction reads: ln xi = –ΔHfus,i/R (1/T – 1/Tm,i) + ΔCp,i/R [ln(T/Tm,i) + Tm,i/T – 1]. For enantiotropic pairs, the transition enthalpy ΔHtr and ΔCp,tr must be included to enforce continuity of G at Ttr. Experimental determination of these quantities relies on differential scanning calorimetry (DSC) for ΔHfus and ΔHtr, and temperature‑modulated DSC or heat‑capacity spectroscopy for ΔCp.

In practice, polymorph screening (slurry conversion, solvent‑mediated transformation) identifies the stable form at the process temperature. Process engineers then use the stable‑form solubility for design of crystallization, filtration, and drying steps. If a metastable form is deliberately produced (e.g., for bioavailability enhancement), its higher solubility must be modeled with the appropriate ΔHfus and a kinetic conversion model (often first‑order with Arrhenius temperature dependence) to predict the “parachute” descent. Failure to account for polymorphic transitions is a leading cause of batch‑to‑batch variability in pharmaceutical manufacturing and of erroneous solubility databases.

Worked Example: Polymorph Crossover Calculation
A drug exists as two enantiotropic polymorphs, α (stable below 320 K) and β (stable above 320 K). At 310 K the measured mole‑fraction solubility of α is 2.5 × 10⁻⁴. The melting points are Tm,α = 450 K, Tm,β = 460 K. ΔHfus,α = 30 kJ mol⁻¹, ΔHfus,β = 28 kJ mol⁻¹. Assume ΔCp ≈ 0 for both forms. Estimate the solubility of β at 330 K.
First compute the solubility of α at the transition temperature 320 K using the van’t Hoff relation: ln xα(320) = ln xα(310) – ΔHfus,α/R (1/320 – 1/310). With R = 8.314 J mol⁻¹ K⁻¹, ΔHfus,α/R = 30 000/8.314 ≈ 3608. ln xα(310) = ln(2.5 × 10⁻⁴) ≈ –8.294. 1/320 – 1/310 = –0.000101. Product = –3608 × (–0.000101) ≈ +0.364. Thus ln xα(320) ≈ –8.294 + 0.364 = –7.930 → xα(320) ≈ 3.6 × 10⁻⁴. At the transition, xα = xβ. Now extrapolate β to 330 K: ln xβ(330) = ln xβ(320) – ΔHfus,β/R (1/330 – 1/320). ΔHfus,β/R = 28 000/8.314 ≈ 3368. 1/330 – 1/320 = –0.0000947. Product = –3368 × (–0.0000947) ≈ +0.319. ln xβ(330) = –7.930 + 0.319 = –7.611 → xβ(330) ≈ 4.9 × 10⁻⁴. The predicted solubility of the high‑temperature polymorph at 330 K is 4.9 × 10⁻⁴ mole fraction.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a problem gives solubility data for one polymorph and asks for the other at a different temperature, always locate the transition temperature first. Use the van’t Hoff equation (or Schröder–van Laar if ΔCp is supplied) to bring the known solubility to the transition point, then extrapolate with the second polymorph’s ΔHfus. Sketching a quick ln x vs 1/T diagram helps avoid sign errors in the temperature differences.

Thermodynamics of Nanoparticle Solubility: Ostwald Ripening and the Gibbs‑Thomson Effect

Nanoparticles exhibit a size‑dependent solubility because the curvature of the solid–liquid interface raises the chemical potential of the solid phase. The Gibbs‑Thomson (Kelvin) equation for a spherical particle of radius r reads: ln(xr/x∞) = 2γVm/(rRT), where xr is the mole‑fraction solubility of the particle, x∞ the bulk solubility, γ the solid–liquid interfacial tension, Vm the molar volume of the solid, R the gas constant, and T the absolute temperature. For typical organic crystals (γ ≈ 0.05 J m⁻², Vm ≈ 1 × 10⁻⁴ m³ mol⁻¹) a 10 nm particle at 298 K shows a solubility enhancement of roughly 1.5‑fold; at 2 nm the enhancement exceeds an order of magnitude.

Ostwald ripening is the kinetic manifestation of this thermodynamic driving force: larger particles grow at the expense of smaller ones because the solution concentration equilibrates to the solubility of the larger particles, leaving the smaller particles undersaturated and causing them to dissolve. The Lifshitz‑Slyozov‑Wagner (LSW) theory predicts a cubic growth law for the mean particle radius: r³ – r₀³ = K t, where the rate constant K = 8γVm² D c∞/(9RT). Here D is the solute diffusion coefficient and c∞ the bulk molar concentration. The theory assumes diffusion‑controlled growth, a dilute particle volume fraction, and a capillary‑length scale much smaller than the mean inter‑particle distance.

In real formulations (e.g., nanosuspensions for poorly soluble drugs), several factors modify the ideal LSW picture. Polydispersity broadens the size distribution and accelerates ripening because the solubility gradient scales with 1/r. Adsorbed polymers or surfactants lower the effective interfacial tension γ, reducing both the equilibrium solubility enhancement and the ripening rate. However, steric stabilization can also create a kinetic barrier that decouples dissolution from diffusion, leading to “apparent” ripening rates slower than LSW predicts. Electrostatic stabilization (charged particles) introduces a double‑layer contribution to the chemical potential, which can be incorporated by replacing γ with an effective interfacial free energy that includes the electrostatic term.

Experimental quantification of nanoparticle solubility often uses the “solubility‑versus‑size” method: a series of narrowly sized fractions (obtained by centrifugation or field‑flow fractionation) are equilibrated with solvent, and the dissolved concentration is measured by HPLC or UV spectroscopy. Plotting ln c versus 1/r yields a straight line whose slope gives 2γVm/(RT), providing a direct experimental estimate of γ. This approach has validated the Gibbs‑Thomson equation for systems such as griseofulvin, fenofibrate, and inorganic salts (e.g., BaSO₄) down to radii of 5 nm.

From a process perspective, controlling Ostwald ripening is critical for shelf‑life of nanosuspensions and for the design of antisolvent precipitation where a narrow particle size distribution is desired. Strategies include: (i) adding a ripening inhibitor that selectively adsorbs on high‑curvature surfaces, (ii) formulating at temperatures where the solubility enhancement is minimal, (iii) using a co‑solvent that reduces γ, and (iv) engineering a rapid quench to “freeze” the size distribution before ripening proceeds. Thermodynamic modeling that couples the Gibbs‑Thomson equation with population balance equations (PBEs) enables predictive simulation of particle‑size evolution during storage and processing.

Solubility of Ionic Liquids in Supercritical CO₂: Density‑Based Correlations and the Chrastil Equation

Ionic liquids (ILs) are increasingly used as green solvents, but their removal or recovery often employs supercritical CO₂ (scCO₂) extraction. The solubility of an IL in scCO₂ is typically low (10⁻⁶–10⁻³ mole fraction) and strongly dependent on fluid density ρ. The Chrastil equation, originally developed for solid solutes, correlates solubility y (mass fraction) with density and temperature: ln y = k ln ρ + a/T + b, where k, a, b are empirical constants. For ILs, the association number k often reflects the number of CO₂ molecules clustering around the ion pair; values of 2–6 are common. The parameter a captures the enthalpic contribution (≈ –ΔHsol/R), while b accounts for entropic effects and the reference state.

Because ILs consist of discrete cations and anions, the dissolution mechanism in scCO₂ can involve ion‑pair extraction, ion‑dipole complexation with CO₂ quadrupoles, or formation of reverse micelles at higher IL concentrations. The simple Chrastil form does not distinguish these mechanisms, but its density‑based nature makes it robust for interpolation within the experimental envelope (typically 80–300 bar, 313–353 K). For extrapolation, a thermodynamic model such as the Peng–Robinson equation of state with the Wong–Sandler mixing rule and the NRTL activity‑coefficient model for the liquid phase provides a more physically grounded description. The binary interaction parameters are regressed from the same solubility data used for Chrastil fitting.

Experimental determination of IL solubility in scCO₂ uses a static‑equilibrium cell with online sampling (e.g., supercritical fluid extraction coupled to gravimetric analysis or to a mass‑spectrometer). Care must be taken to avoid co‑extraction of water, which dramatically increases IL solubility because water acts as a co‑solvent and reduces the ion‑pair lattice energy. Reported data for imidazolium‑based ILs (e.g., [C₄mim][BF₄], [C₆mim][PF₆]) show that fluorinated anions enhance CO₂‑philicity, giving higher k values (≈5) compared with non‑fluorinated anions (≈2). Cation alkyl‑chain length also matters: longer chains increase van‑der‑Waals interactions with CO₂, raising solubility by up to an order of magnitude.

Process design for IL recovery from scCO₂ typically operates in a counter‑current extractor where the IL‑rich liquid phase is contacted with scCO₂ at a pressure where IL solubility is maximal, then the pressure is reduced to precipitate the IL. The Chrastil correlation enables rapid estimation of the required pressure swing: for a target recovery of 99 %, the pressure drop must reduce ρ such that y falls below the IL concentration in the raffinate. Because the Chrastil parameters are temperature‑dependent, an isothermal pressure swing is preferred to avoid additional heating/cooling steps. Recent work combines the Chrastil equation with a mass‑transfer model (film theory) to size the extractor height, showing that the overall mass‑transfer coefficient scales with ρ⁰·⁵, consistent with the density dependence of CO₂ diffusivity.

Limitations of the Chrastil approach include its inability to predict solubility in the presence of co‑solvents (e.g., ethanol) and its empirical nature, which restricts transferability to new IL families. For rigorous design, a hybrid strategy is recommended: use Chrastil for quick screening and initial parameter estimation, then refine with an equation‑of‑state model validated against a minimal set of high‑pressure solubility measurements.

Common Traps

  • Assuming the e‑NRTL model requires only binary solvent‑solvent parameters; ion‑specific τ and α values are mandatory for quantitative predictions.
  • Neglecting the polymorphic transition temperature when extrapolating solubility data, which leads to crossover errors in van’t Hoff plots.
  • Using the bulk solubility value for nanoparticles without applying the Gibbs‑Thomson correction, underestimating dissolution rates by orders of magnitude.
  • Treating the Chrastil association number k as a universal constant for all ionic liquids; it varies with anion fluorination and cation alkyl length.
  • Ignoring the effect of trace water on IL solubility in scCO₂, which can increase apparent solubility by a factor of 10–100.
  • Applying the LSW ripening law to highly concentrated nanosuspensions where particle‑volume fraction violates the dilute‑limit assumption.
  • Fitting e‑NRTL parameters to a single data set (e.g., only osmotic coefficients) without checking Gibbs‑Duhem consistency across activity, enthalpy, and VLE data.

Thermodynamic Modeling of Liquid‑Liquid Equilibrium with the Wilson Equation

The Wilson equation, introduced in 1964, provides a local‑composition model for the excess Gibbs energy of liquid mixtures. Its central assumption is that the local mole fraction of component j around a central molecule of component i differs from the bulk composition according to a Boltzmann factor involving the interaction energy λij − λii. The resulting expression for the molar excess Gibbs energy is GE/RT = −∑i xi ln(∑j xj Λij), where Λij = (Vj/Vi) exp[−(λij − λii)/RT]. The model contains two adjustable binary parameters per pair (Λ12 and Λ21) and requires pure‑component molar volumes Vi as input. Because the Wilson equation cannot predict liquid‑liquid phase splitting (it lacks a miscibility gap), it is restricted to completely miscible or partially miscible systems where the binodal curve does not appear. For liquid‑liquid equilibrium (LLE) calculations, the Wilson parameters are usually regressed from tie‑line data; the resulting activity coefficients γi = exp[−ln(∑j xjΛij) + 1 − ∑k (xkΛki/∑j xjΛkj)] are then used in the equality of component fugacities across the two liquid phases. Compared with the NRTL and UNIQUAC models, Wilson offers a simpler algebraic form and often fits vapor‑liquid equilibrium (VLE) data for polar‑nonpolar systems with comparable accuracy, but it fails for systems exhibiting strong asymmetry or association. In ternary LLE, Wilson parameters from the three constituent binaries can be combined without additional ternary parameters, allowing prediction of the ternary binodal surface and tie‑triangles. A practical limitation is the temperature dependence of Λij; the standard approach assumes an Arrhenius form Λij = Aij exp(−Bij/T), where Aij and Bij are fitted to VLE or LLE data at several temperatures. When applying Wilson to electrolyte‑containing mixtures, the model must be coupled with a separate electrolyte term (e.g., Pitzer or e‑NRTL) because the local‑composition concept does not account for long‑range Coulombic forces. In summary, the Wilson equation remains a valuable tool for correlating VLE and LLE of non‑associated, moderately non‑ideal binary and ternary systems, provided its inability to describe phase splitting is respected.

Gas Solubility in Subcritical Water: Density‑Based Correlations and the Chrastil‑Type Approach

Subcritical water (typically 373–647 K, 10–220 bar) exhibits a dramatic decrease in dielectric constant and a corresponding increase in density‑dependent solvating power for non‑polar gases. Unlike supercritical fluids, subcritical water remains a liquid, but its density ρ can be varied by 30–40 % over the operating window, making density‑based correlations attractive.
