The Chrastil equation, originally developed for solid solutes in supercritical CO₂, has been adapted to gas‑in‑water systems by expressing the mole‑fraction solubility y as ln y = k ln ρ + a/T + b, where k reflects the number of water molecules clustering around a gas molecule, a ≈ −ΔHsol/R captures the enthalpy of solution, and b accounts for entropy and reference‑state effects. For CO₂ in subcritical water, experimental data (e.g., 423 K, 100 bar) give k ≈ 2.1, a ≈ −1800 K, b ≈ 5.3, reproducing solubilities within ±5 % over 373–573 K and 50–200 bar. The density ρ is obtained from an accurate equation of state such as IAPWS‑95. A key advantage of the Chrastil form is its ability to interpolate smoothly between sparse high‑pressure measurements, which are experimentally demanding. However, the model does not explicitly incorporate the effect of water’s hydrogen‑bond network restructuring, which becomes significant near the critical point; consequently, extrapolation beyond the fitted temperature range can produce systematic deviations. An alternative is the Krichevsky‑Kasarnovsky equation, which relates the Henry constant H to the partial molar volume of the gas at infinite dilution, but it requires accurate V̄2∞ data that are scarce for subcritical water. In practice, a hybrid strategy is recommended: use the Chrastil correlation for rapid screening and process design (e.g., sizing a supercritical water oxidation reactor), and validate critical points with a thermodynamic model such as the Peng‑Robinson EOS coupled with the Wong‑Sandler mixing rule and an NRTL activity‑coefficient term for the aqueous phase. This combination captures both the density‑driven solubility enhancement and the non‑ideality arising from water’s polarity change.

Examiner's trap
A typical question asks: “Estimate the mole‑fraction solubility of CO₂ in subcritical water at 473 K and 150 bar using the Chrastil correlation with parameters k = 2.1, a = –1800 K, b = 5.3. The IAPWS‑95 density at these conditions is 0.78 g cm⁻³.”
First convert density to kg m⁻³: 0.78 g cm⁻³ = 780 kg m⁻³. Compute ln ρ = ln(780) ≈ 6.66. Then ln y = 2.1 × 6.66 + (–1800)/473 + 5.3 = 13.99 – 3.81 + 5.3 = 15.48. Hence y = exp(15.48) ≈ 5.3 × 10⁶. This value is clearly unphysical because the Chrastil equation in its original form uses density in g cm⁻³, not kg m⁻³. Re‑evaluate with ρ = 0.78 g cm⁻³: ln ρ = ln(0.78) ≈ –0.248. Then ln y = 2.1 × (–0.248) – 3.81 + 5.3 = –0.52 – 3.81 + 5.3 = 0.97. Thus y = exp(0.97) ≈ 2.64. Since mole fraction cannot exceed 1, the result indicates that the chosen parameters are not valid at this state point; the correct approach is to use parameters regressed for the specific temperature‑pressure range. The trap is blindly applying a correlation outside its calibrated domain and ignoring unit consistency.
Exam‑day strategy
When a density‑based correlation appears, always verify the units of ρ used in the original publication. Write the unit conversion explicitly in your working; a single unit mismatch can change the answer by orders of magnitude. Also, check whether the correlation parameters were fitted for the same temperature‑pressure region as the question; if not, state the limitation rather than forcing a calculation.

Solid‑Liquid Equilibrium of Pharmaceutical Polymorphs: The van’t Hoff‑Apelblat Hybrid Model

Polymorphism in active pharmaceutical ingredients (APIs) leads to distinct solid‑liquid equilibria because each polymorph possesses a unique crystal lattice energy and, consequently, a different intrinsic solubility. The classical van’t Hoff equation, ln x = −ΔHfus/R (1/T − 1/Tm) + ΔCp/R [ln(T/Tm) + Tm/T − 1], relates the mole‑fraction solubility x to the fusion enthalpy ΔHfus, melting temperature Tm, and heat‑capacity difference ΔCp. For many APIs, ΔCp is unknown, and the van’t Hoff plot (ln x vs 1/T) shows curvature due to temperature‑dependent ΔHfus and solid‑phase transitions. The Apelblat equation, ln x = A + B/T + C ln T, provides an empirical three‑parameter fit that captures curvature without requiring thermodynamic properties. The hybrid model combines both: the van’t Hoff term supplies a physically grounded baseline, while the Apelblat correction accounts for residual non‑ideality and heat‑capacity effects. For carbamazepine Form III, experimental solubilities in ethanol from 293–333 K are fitted with the hybrid form ln x = −ΔHfus/R (1/T − 1/Tm) + A + B/T + C ln T, yielding ΔHfus = 28.5 kJ mol⁻¹, Tm = 453 K, and Apelblat parameters A = 12.3, B = −1500 K, C = −1.8. The hybrid model predicts solubilities within ±3 % across the range, whereas the pure van’t Hoff equation deviates by up to 15 % at the extremes. Parameter estimation proceeds by non‑linear regression; the physically meaningful ΔHfus and Tm can be constrained by DSC measurements, reducing correlation among parameters. A critical limitation is that the hybrid model does not explicitly treat solid‑solid transitions (e.g., enantiotropic polymorph conversion) that may occur within the temperature window; if a transition temperature Ttr lies inside the range, the solubility curve exhibits a kink that must be modeled by separate van’t Hoff branches for each polymorph. In such cases, the hybrid approach is applied piecewise, with continuity of chemical potential enforced at Ttr. The method is widely used in pharmaceutical process development for designing crystallization cooling profiles and for predicting the maximum achievable yield of a desired polymorph.

Electrolyte Solutions at High Temperature and Pressure: The Helgeson‑Kirkham‑Flowers (HKF) Model

The HKF model, developed in the 1980s for geochemical applications, provides a comprehensive equation‑of‑state for aqueous electrolytes up to 1000 K and 5 kbar. It expresses the standard‑state Gibbs free energy of formation ΔGf° of an aqueous ion as a function of temperature T and pressure P through a series of terms derived from the Born solvation model and a non‑solvation (intrinsic) contribution. The key equations are: ΔGf°(T,P) = ΔGf°(Tr,Pr) + ∫TrT ΔS° dT − ∫PrP ΔV° dP, where the standard‑state entropy ΔS° and volume ΔV° are given by analytic functions of T and P. The non‑solvation heat capacity Cp°n is modeled as Cp°n = c1 + c2/(T − θ)², with θ ≈ 228 K representing the temperature of maximum density of water. The Born term accounts for electrostatic solvation: ΔGBorn = −(NAz²e²/8πε0re)(1 − 1/ε), where ε is the dielectric constant of water (from the IAPWS formulation), re is the effective Born radius, and z is the ionic charge. The model requires a set of species‑specific parameters: ΔGf°(Tr,Pr), ΔS°(Tr,Pr), c1, c2, and re. These are regressed from experimental data including standard‑state enthalpies, heat capacities, volumes, and equilibrium constants at elevated T and P. For NaCl(aq), the HKF parameters reproduce the equilibrium constant of the dissociation reaction NaCl ⇌ Na⁺ + Cl⁻ within ±0.02 log K units from 273–873 K and 1–5 kbar. The model has been extended to neutral species (e.g., CO₂(aq), CH₄(aq)) by setting z = 0 and fitting the non‑solvation parameters to solubility data. A major advantage of HKF over Pitzer or e‑NRTL is its ability to predict thermodynamic properties far beyond the experimental range, which is essential for modeling hydrothermal ore formation, carbon sequestration in deep saline aquifers, and nuclear waste repository performance. Limitations include the reliance on the Born approximation, which becomes less accurate for highly charged or large ions where specific ion‑water interactions dominate, and the need for a consistent dielectric‑constant model at extreme conditions. Modern implementations couple HKF with the Deep Earth Water (DEW) model to improve the description of water’s dielectric behavior above 800 K.

Thermodynamics of Supercritical Fluid Extraction with Co‑solvents: The Modified Chrastil Equation

Supercritical fluid extraction (SFE) of natural products often employs a polar co‑solvent (e.g., ethanol, methanol) at 1–10 mol % to enhance the solubility of polar analytes in supercritical CO₂. The original Chrastil equation, ln y = k ln ρ + a/T + b, correlates the solute mass‑fraction solubility y with CO₂ density ρ and temperature T but does not account for co‑solvent concentration. A widely used modification adds a linear term in the co‑solvent mole fraction xcs: ln y = k ln ρ + a/T + b + c xcs. The parameter c quantifies the co‑solvent’s effectiveness; for ethanol in CO₂ extracting caffeine, c ≈ 12.5, indicating that a 5 mol % ethanol addition increases ln y by ~0.6 (≈80 % increase in solubility). The density ρ is calculated from a high‑accuracy EOS (e.g., Span‑Wagner) at the system T and P. Parameter estimation requires solubility measurements at several (T, P, xcs) points; typically 12–15 data points suffice to regress k, a, b, c with R² > 0.98. The modified Chrastil model is empirical but offers rapid interpolation for process optimization, such as determining the minimum pressure needed to achieve a target yield at a fixed co‑solvent loading. Its limitations become apparent when the co‑solvent induces a phase‑behavior change (e.g., formation of a second liquid phase) or when the co‑solvent concentration exceeds ~15 mol %, where the linear assumption fails and a quadratic term d xcs² is required. In such regimes, a thermodynamic model like the Peng‑Robinson EOS with the Wong‑Sandler mixing rule and an NRTL activity‑coefficient model for the liquid phase provides a more rigorous description, albeit at higher computational cost. A practical workflow for SFE design: (1) use the modified Chrastil correlation to screen pressure‑temperature‑co‑solvent space; (2) identify a promising operating window; (3) validate with a full EOS‑based model using binary interaction parameters fitted to the same data; (4) perform a sensitivity analysis on c to assess robustness against co‑solvent feed fluctuations. This hybrid approach balances speed and accuracy, which is critical for techno‑economic evaluation of SFE processes in the pharmaceutical and nutraceutical industries.

  • Assuming the Wilson equation can predict liquid‑liquid phase splitting; it cannot, and using it for binodal calculations yields erroneous tie‑lines.
  • Applying the Chrastil correlation with density in kg m⁻³ instead of the original g cm⁻³, leading to solubility predictions that are orders of magnitude off.
  • Fitting the van’t Hoff‑Apelblat hybrid model across a polymorphic transition temperature without splitting the regression into separate branches, which masks the kink in the solubility curve.
  • Using HKF parameters for an ion outside the temperature‑pressure range of the original regression (e.g., extrapolating Na⁺ parameters to 1200 K) without verifying the dielectric‑constant model validity.
  • Neglecting the quadratic co‑solvent term in the modified Chrastil equation when the co‑solvent mole fraction exceeds 0.1, causing systematic under‑prediction of solubility enhancement.
  • Treating the Wilson binary parameters as temperature‑independent when the system exhibits strong enthalpy‑entropy compensation, resulting in poor VLE predictions at temperatures far from the regression set.
  • Ignoring the requirement that the Born radius in the HKF model be consistent with the chosen standard state (1 mol kg⁻¹ vs 1 mol L⁻¹), which introduces a systematic offset in calculated equilibrium constants.

These advanced models extend the core solution thermodynamics toolkit and are frequently tested in competitive examinations through conceptual twists and numerical traps.
